8:                                                           ANITA PASOTTI AND MARCO ANTONIO PELLEGRINI
9: 
10: 
11:                                                   Abstract. Mariusz Meszka has conjectured that given a prime p = 2n + 1
12:                                                   and a list L containing n positive integers not exceeding n there exists a
13:                                                   near 1-factor in Kp whose list of edge-lengths is L. In this paper we propose
14:                                                   a generalization of this problem to the case in which p is an odd integer not
15:                                                   necessarily prime. In particular, we give a necessary condition for the existence
16:                                                   of such a near 1-factor for any odd integer p. We show that this condition is
17:                                                   also sufficient for any list L whose underlying set S has size 1, 2, or n. Then
18:                                                   we prove that the conjecture is true if S = {1, 2, t} for any positive integer
19:                                                   t not coprime with the order p of the complete graph. Also, we give partial
20:                                                   results when t and p are coprime. Finally, we present a complete solution for
21:                                                   t ≤ 11.
22: 
23: 
24:

27:                                              Throughout this paper Kv will denote the complete graph on {0, 1, . . . , v − 1}
28:                                           for any positive integer v. For the basic terminology on graphs we refer to [14].
29:                                           Following [9], we define the length ℓ(x, y) of an edge [x, y] of Kv as
36:                                                                                                2 it results δ(Γ) = ℓ(Γ). For
37:                                           our convenience, if a list L consists of a1 1′ s, a2 2′ s, . . . , at t′ s, we will write
38:                                           L = {1a1 , 2a2 , . . . , tat }.
39:                                              The following conjecture [4, 13] is due to Marco Buratti (2007, communication
40:                                           to Alex Rosa).
41:                                           Conjecture (Buratti). For any prime p = 2n + 1 and any list L of 2n positive
42:                                           integers not exceeding n, there exists a Hamiltonian path H of Kp with ℓ(H) = L.
43:                                              In [9] Peter Horak and Alex Rosa generalized Buratti’s conjecture. Such a gen-
44:                                           eralization has been restated in a easier form in [10] as follows.
45:                                           Conjecture (Horak and Rosa). Let L be a list of v − 1 positive integers not ex-
46:                                           ceeding ⌊ v2 ⌋. Then there exists a Hamiltonian path H of Kv such that ℓ(H) = L if,
47:                                           and only if, the following condition holds:

58:    Following [10], by BHR(L) we will denote the above conjecture for a given list
59: L. Some partial results have been obtained about this problem, see [5, 7, 9, 10, 11],
60: but the conjecture is still wide open.
61: Recently, Mariusz Meszka formulated a very similar conjecture.
62: Conjecture (Meszka). For any prime p = 2n + 1 and any list L of n positive
63: integers not exceeding n, there exists a near 1-factor F of Kp with ℓ(F ) = L.
64:    We recall that a near 1-factor of K2n+1 is a subgraph of K2n+1 consisting in n
65: disjoint edges and one isolated vertex, while a 1-factor of K2n is a subgraph of K2n
66: consisting in n disjoint edges.
67:    To best of our knowledge, all the results on the problem proposed by Meszka
68: are contained in [12], where Rosa proved that the conjecture is true when the
69: elements of the list are {1, 2, 3} or {1, 2, 3, 4} or {1, 2, 3, 4, 5}. Also he proved that
70: the conjecture is true when the list L contains a sufficient number of 1’s, in detail,
71:                                               2
72: if L = {1a1 , 2a2 , . . . , nan } with a1 ≥ n2 . Moreover, with the aid of a computer, we
73: have verified the validity of Meszka’s conjecture for all primes p ≤ 23.
74:    Working on this conjecture it is easy to see that the hypothesis p prime is not
75: necessary, as it happens for Buratti’s one. Indeed we find another condition which
76: is necessary and which led us to propose our conjecture.
77: Conjecture. Let v = 2n + 1 be an odd integer and L be a list of n positive integers
78: not exceeding n. Then there exists a near 1-factor F of Kv such that ℓ(F ) = L if,
79: and only if, the following condition holds:
80:                  for any divisor d of v, the number of multiples of d
81: (2)
82:                         appearing in L does not exceed v−d
83:                                                          2 .
84: 
85:    By MPP(L) we will denote our conjecture for a given list L. Clearly, if v is a
86: prime then our conjecture reduces to Meszka’s one. With the aid of a computer,
87: we have verified its validity for all odd integers v ≤ 23. We point out that in
88: the statement, the actual conjecture is the sufficiency. In fact we can prove that
89: condition (2) is necessary.
90: Proposition 1. The list L of edge-lengths of any near 1-factor of Kv satisfies
91: condition (2).
92: Proof. Let F be a near 1-factor of Kv with ℓ(F ) = L and let d be a divisor of v.
93: Denote by D the sublist of L containing all the multiples of d appearing in L, hence
94: we have to prove that |D| ≤ v−d2 . Note that if the length of an edge is a multiple
95: of d, the vertices of such an edge have to be in the same residue class modulo d
96: and that F has exactly dv vertices in each residue class modulo d. Also, vd is an odd
97: number and this implies that with the elements of each residue class modulo d we

115:    For reader convenience we recall the definition of a Cayley multigraph. A list
116: Ω of elements of an additive group G is said to be symmetric if 0 ∈      / Ω and the
117: multiplicities of g and −g in Ω coincide for any g ∈ G. If Ω does not have repeated
118: elements then one can consider the Cayley graph on G with connection set Ω, de-
119: noted Cay[G : Ω], whose vertex-set is G and in which [x, y] is an edge if and only
120: if x − y ∈ Ω. Cayley graphs have a great importance in combinatorics and they
121: are precisely the graphs admitting an automorphism group acting sharply transi-
122: tively on the vertex-set (see, e.g., [8]). If, more generally, the symmetric list Ω has
123: repeated elements one can consider the Cayley multigraph on G with connection
124: multiset Ω, also denoted Cay[G : Ω] and with vertex-set G, where the multiplicity

631: gcd(t, v) = 1. Let v = qt + r, with 0 < r < t. We construct an incomplete matrix

11:                                                  Abstract. Mariusz Meszka has conjectured that given a prime p = 2n + 1
12:                                                  and a list L containing n positive integers not exceeding n there exists a
13:                                                  near 1-factor in Kp whose list of edge-lengths is L. In this paper we propose
14:                                                  a generalization of this problem to the case in which p is an odd integer not
15:                                                  necessarily prime. In particular, we give a necessary condition for the existence
16:                                                  of such a near 1-factor for any odd integer p. We show that this condition is
17:                                                  also sufficient for any list L whose underlying set S has size 1, 2, or n. Then
18:                                                  we prove that the conjecture is true if S = {1, 2, t} for any positive integer
19:                                                  t not coprime with the order p of the complete graph. Also, we give partial
20:                                                  results when t and p are coprime. Finally, we present a complete solution for
21:                                                  t ≤ 11.

60: Theorem 1.1. For every integer n ≥ 9,
61: 
62:                                    dlog3 ne + 2 ≤ aw([n], 3) ≤ dlog2 ne + 1.
63: 
64: Moreover, aw([n], 3) = dlog3 ne + 2 for n ∈ {3, 4, 5, 6, 7} and aw([8], 3) = 5.
65:    Theorem 1.1 is Proven by Lemmas 2.3 and 2.6 (for n ≥ 9), and Remark 2.1 gives exact values of aw([n], 3)
66: that justify the second statement. We conjecture that the lower bound is, essentially, correct:
67: 
68: Conjecture 1.2. There exists a constant C such that aw([n], 3) ≤ dlog3 ne + C for all n ≥ 3.
69:    The behavior of aw([n], k) is, however, different for k ≥ 4. Instead of logarithmic, it is almost linear:
70: Theorem 1.3. For k ≥ 4,
71:                                       √
72:                                ne−O( log n) < aw([n], k) ≤ ne− log log log n−ω(1) .
73: 
74:    Theorem 1.3 is established by Lemma 2.8 and Corollary 2.14.
75:    Finally, we consider arithmetic progressions in the cyclic group Zn .
76: 
77: Remark 1.4. For positive integers n and k, aw(Zn , k) ≤ aw([n], k), because every AP in [n] corresponds to
78: an AP in Zn .
79:     However, because progressions in Zn may “wrap around,” there are additional APs in Zn , some of which
80: may be rainbow. Thus it is possible that a rainbow k-AP is required with a coloring of Zn with aw([n], k) − 1
81: colors, so strict inequality is possible. As we see in Theorem 1.5, there is an infinite sequence of values of n
82: for which aw(Zn , 3) = 3.
83: Theorem 1.5.
84:   1. For all positive integers m, aw(Z2m , 3) = 3.
85:   2. For an integer n ≥ 2 having every prime factor less than 100,
86: 
87:                                            aw(Zn , 3) = 2 + f2 + f3 + 2f4 .                                    (1)
88: 
89:       Here f4 denotes the number of odd prime factors of n in the set Q4 := {17, 31, 41, 43, 73, 89, 97}. The
90:       quantity f3 is the number of odd prime factors of n in Q3 , where Q3 is the set of all odd primes less
91:       than 100 and not in Q4 . Both f3 and f4 are counted according to multiplicity. Finally, f2 = 0 if n odd
92:       and f2 = 1 if n is even.
93:    Theorem 1.5 is established by Lemma 3.2 and Corollary 3.15.
94:    For k ≥ 4, the bounds we obtain for aw(Zn , k) are the same as those for aw([n], k):
95: Theorem 1.6. For k ≥ 4,
96:                                       √
97:                                ne−O( log n) < aw(Zn , k) ≤ ne− log log log n−ω(1) .
98: 
99:     Theorem 1.6 is established by Theorem 1.1, Remark 1.4, and Lemma 3.20.
100:     The structure of the paper is as follows: Section 2 presents results pertaining to aw([n], k), with Theorem
101: 1.1 proved in Section 2.1 and Theorem 1.3 proved in Section 2.2. Results pertaining to aw(Zn , k) appear
102: in Section 3, with Theorem 1.5 proved in Section 3.1 and Theorem 1.6 proved in Section 3.2. Section 4
103: describes the methods and algorithms used to compute values of aw([n], k) and aw(Zn , k), while Section 5
104: contains conjectures and open questions for future research.
105:     In the remainder of this section we establish a basic but necessary observation that aw(S, ·) is monotone
106: in k.
107: 
108: Observation 1.7. Let G be an additive (abelian) group such as the integers or the integers modulo n, let S
109: be a finite nonempty subset of G, and let k ≥ 3 be an integer. Then aw(S, k) ≤ aw(S, k + 1).
110: 
111: 
112:                                                         2
113: 
114:     Observation 1.7 follows immediately from Proposition 1.8 below and was noted noted by Uherka in [13]
115: for the function aw([n], ·).
145: k ≥ 4 confirm that the growth rates are indeed radically different.
146: 
147: 2.1      Main results for aw([n], 3)
148: Before we address Theorem 1.1, we show a summary of the computed data in this case in Remark 2.1 below.
149: Remark 2.1. The exact values of aw([n], 3) are known from computer computations (described in Section 4)
150: for n ≤ 58, and are recorded here.
151: 
152:     1. aw([n], 3) = 2 for n ∈ {1}.
153:     2. aw([n], 3) = 3 for n ∈ {2, 3}.
154:     3. aw([n], 3) = 4 for n ∈ {4, . . . , 7} ∪ {9}.
155: 
156:     4. aw([n], 3) = 5 for n ∈ {8} ∪ {10, . . . , 21} ∪ {27}.

195: 2.1.1   Theorem 1.1: Proof of lower bound
196: Proposition 2.2. Let n be a positive integer and let s ∈ {−2, −1, 0, 1, 2}. Then aw([3n−s], 3) ≥ aw([n], 3)+1
197: provided n ≥ s.
198: Proof. Let r = aw([n], 3) and s ∈ {0, 1, 2}. We construct an exact r-coloring of [3n − s] that does not contain
199: a rainbow 3-AP. By definition there exists an exact (r − 1)-coloring, denoted c, of [n] such that there is no
200: rainbow 3-AP in [n]. Color [3n − s] in the following manner: If i + s is divisible by 3, then ĉ(i) = c((i + s)/3),
201: otherwise ĉ(i) = r. Consider a 3-AP, K, in [3n − s]. Then either the three terms in K + s are all divisible by
202: 3 or at least two of the terms in K + s are not divisible by 3. If all terms in K + s are divisible by 3, then K
203: is not rainbow under ĉ, since there is no rainbow 3-AP under c. If two terms of K + s are not divisible by 3
204: then those two terms are both colored r and K is not rainbow. Hence aw([3n − s], 3) ≥ r + 1 for s ∈ {0, 1, 2}.
205:     For s ∈ {−2, −1}, use the same coloring as for s = 0.
206: 
207:    Using Proposition 2.2, we establish the lower bound in Theorem 1.1.
208: Lemma 2.3. Let n be a positive integer. Then aw([n], 3) ≥ dlog3 ne + 2.
209: 
210: Proof. The proof is by induction. The cases n = 1, 2, 3 are true by inspection. Suppose n > 3 and that
211: aw([m], 3) ≥ dlog3 me + 2 for all m satisfying 1 ≤ m < n. We show that aw([n], 3) ≥ dlog3 ne + 2. First, we
212: write n = 3m − s, where s ∈ {0, 1, 2} and 2 ≤ m < n. Then by Proposition 2.2,
213: 
214:      aw([n], 3) = aw([3m − s], 3) ≥ aw([m], 3) + 1 ≥ dlog3 me + 2 + 1 = dlog3 (3m)e + 2 ≥ dlog3 ne + 2.
215:

226: 2.1.2    Theorem 1.1: Proof of upper bound
227: Proposition 2.5. For n ≥ 2, there exists m ≤ b n2 c such that aw([n], 3) ≤ aw ([m], 3) + 1.
228: Proof. We may assume that n ≥ 3, since the case n = 2 follows by inspection. Let r = aw([n], 3). Then
229: there exists an (r − 1)-coloring, namely c, of [n] that has no rainbow 3-AP. Let t be the length of a shortest
230: consecutive integer sequence in [n] that contains all r − 1 colors, say the interval is {s + 1, s + 2, . . . , s + t} for
231: some s. Define ĉ to be an (r − 1)-coloring of [t] = {1, 2, . . . , t} so that ĉ(j) := c(s + j) for 1 ≤ j ≤ t. Notice
232: that ĉ(1) and ĉ(t) cannot be the same color and each must be the only element of its color class, or else we
233: could find a smaller t. Let ĉ(1) = a and define bi to be the smallest element of [t] such that [bi ] has i+1 colors
234: for 1 ≤ i ≤ r − 2. Note that if bi is odd, i.e., bi = 2x + 1, then {1, x + 1, 2x + 1} is a rainbow 3-AP. So the
235: set of even numbers of [t] are colored with exactly r − 2 colors with no rainbow 3-AP. Define m = b 2t c ≤ b n2 c
236: and consider the coloring c̃ of [m] induced by the coloring ĉ of the even integers in [t]. The coloring c̃ uses
237: at least r − 2 colors and has with no rainbow 3-AP, so aw([n], 3) − 1 = (r − 2) + 1 ≤ aw ([m], 3).
238: 
239:    Using Proposition 2.5, we establish the upper bound in Theorem 1.1.
240: Lemma 2.6. For n ≥ 9, aw([n], 3) ≤ dlog2 ne + 1
241: Proof. The proof is by strong induction on n. We consider 9 ≤ n ≤ 17 as the base case, and the result is
242: established for these values by computation (see Remark 2.1).
243:     As the induction hypothesis, assume aw([m], 3) ≤ dlog2 me + 1 for 9 ≤ m ≤ n, and suppose that n ≥ 17.
244: Then 9 ≤ b n+1                                                      n+1
245:              2 c ≤ n, and by Proposition 2.5, there exists an m ≤ b 2 c such that
246: 
247:                                           aw([n + 1], 3) ≤ aw ([m] , 3) + 1.
248: So, by the induction hypothesis,

264: 2.2     Main results for aw([n], k), k ≥ 4
265: In this section we specialize to the case k ≥ 4, focusing on lower and upper bounds that give aw([n], k) =
266: n1−o(1) . Lemma 2.8 gives the lower bound and Corollary 2.14 gives the upper bound.
267:     Let sz(n, k) denote the largest size of a set S ⊆ [n] such that S contains no k-AP (similar notation was
268: introduced in [5] in honor of Szemerédi [12]). Determining bounds on sz(n, k) is a fundamental problem in
269: the study of arithmetic progressions. Behrend [3], Gowers [6], and others [9, 10] have established various
270: bounds on sz(n, k). Proposition 2.7 provides a strong link between sz(n, k) and our anti-van der Waerden
271: numbers, allowing us to use known results on sz(n, k) to bound aw(n, k).
272: Proposition 2.7. For all n > k ≥ 3,
273:                                     sz(n, bk/2c) + 1 ≤ aw([n], k) − 1 ≤ sz(n, k).

287: Proposition 2.7 is trivial but is, in fact useful in the case of k ≥ 6. We provide a similar lower bound for
288: k ∈ {4, 5} in Lemma 2.8 by carefully studying Behrend’s original construction [3] of a relatively large set
289: S ⊂ [n] that contains no 3-AP, thus giving a lower bound on sz(n, 3).
290:    Let {a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 } be a 4-AP. A set A ⊂ {a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 } of size |A| = 3 is called a punctured 4-AP. If
291: such a punctured 4-AP A is not a 3-AP, then it is of the form A = {a1 , a2 , a4 } or A = {a1 , a3 , a4 }. We
292: prove that Behrend’s construction in fact contains no punctured 4-AP (Proposition 2.9 below). This leads
293: to Lemma 2.8 below.

420: 2.3     Additional results for aw([n], k) valid for all k
421: In this section we present some additional elementary results for aw([n], k). The next proposition describes
422: a relationship between aw([n], k) and aw([n − 1], k).
423: Proposition 2.15. Let n and k be positive integers. Then aw([n], k) ≤ aw([n − 1], k) + 1.
424: Proof. Let r = aw([n], k). Note that if n < k our result follows from the definition. Suppose n ≥ k. Then
425: there is some exact (r − 1)-coloring of [n] that has no rainbow k-AP, and without loss of generality n is
426: colored r − 1. Consider this coloring restricted to [n − 1]. Then we have two cases:
427:    1. This is an exact (r − 1)-coloring of [n − 1].
428:    2. The only integer in [n] with the color r − 1 is n, so this is an exact (r − 2)-coloring of [n − 1].
429:    Note that since [n] had no rainbow k-AP in both of our cases we still do not have a rainbow k-AP. So

550: 3.1     Main results for aw(Zn , 3)
551: When we turn to the special case k = 3, many values of aw(Zn , 3) can be computed, and new phenomena
552: appear. Our main results in this case are described by Theorem 1.5, which we establish here.
553:    Currently available computational data is given in Table 2; the row label displays the range of n for
554: which the values of aw(Zn , 3) are reported in that row, and the column heading is the ones digit within this
555: range. This data led to the discovery of several results.
556: Definition 3.1. When dealing with a coloring c of Zst , the ith residue class modulo s is Ri := {j ∈ Zst : j ≡ i
557: (mod s)} and the ith residue palette modulo s is Pi := {c(`) : ` ∈ Ri }. For a positive integer t, we call the
558: elements of the two residue classes, R0 and R1 , modulo 2 in Z2t the even numbers and the odd numbers,
559: respectively.
560: 
561: 3.1.1   Proof of Theorem 1.5, aw(Z2m , 3)
562: As we see from the computed values in Table 2, aw(Zn , 3) does not have an increasing lower bound, and
563: in fact aw(Zn , 3) = 3 whenever n is a power of 2, as proved in Lemma 3.2 below, which is the first part of
564: Theorem 1.5.
565: Lemma 3.2. For all positive integers m, aw(Z2m , 3) = 3.
566: Proof. We prove this by induction on m ≥ 1 and observe that aw(Z2 , 3) = 3 by definition and aw(Z2m , 3) ≥ 3
567: trivially. Let m > 1 and let c be a coloring of Z2m with no rainbow 3-AP. We will show that the number of
568: colors in c is at most 2.
569:     Let A and B denote the residue palettes of the even and odd numbers. Since the even numbers and the
570: odd numbers each form a copy of Z2m−1 within Z2m , the fact that aw(Z2m−1 , 3) = 3 implies that |A| ≤ 2
571: and |B| ≤ 2. If A ⊆ B or B ⊆ A, then at most two colors are used by c and the result follows.
572:     So suppose a ∈ A \ B and b ∈ B \ A. There exist elements `a , `b ∈ Z2m such that c(`a ) = a and c(`b ) = b.
573: Observe that `a is even and `b is odd, so for d := `b − `a , d is an odd number. Since gcd(d, 2m ) = 1, d is a
574: generator of Z2m as an additive group. Thus, every element in Z2m is describable as an element `a + gd for
575: some g ∈ {0, . . . , 2m − 1}.

595: However, there are several examples of odd primes p for which aw(Zp , 3) = 4. We show in Example 3.3

936: 3.3    Additional results for aw(Zn , k)
937: In this section, we present computed data for aw(Zn , k), k ≥ 4, establish the value of aw(Zn , k) for k = n,
938: n − 1, and n − 2, and present some examples that show some additional results fail to extend from [n] to
939: Zn . Table 4 below lists the computed values of aw(Zn , k) for k = 4, . . . , n in the row labeled n.
940:     Next we examine aw(Zn , k) for k close to n.
941: Proposition 3.22. For positive n and k we have aw(Zn , k) = n if and only if k = n.
942: Proof. If k = n the result is obvious. Now suppose that k < n and consider an exact (n − 1)-coloring of Zn .
943: Then there are two numbers with the same color and all other numbers are colored distinctly. Suppose x
944: and y are the the two numbers with the same color. Then {x + 1, ..., x + k} is a k-AP that does not contain
945: x, and so is rainbow. Therefore aw(Zn , k) ≤ n − 1.
993: 
994:    Proposition 3.22 shows that the “if” direction of Theorem 2.16 (k ≥ d n2 e + 1 implies aw([n], k) = n) does
995: not extend to Zn . Example 3.25 below shows that the extension of Proposition 2.15 to Zn , which would
996: assert that aw(Zn , k) ≤ aw(Zn−1 , k) + 1, is not true in general. There are counterexamples in both the cases
997: k = 3 and k ≥ 4.
998: Example 3.25. According to our computed data (see Table 2 in Section 3.1), aw(Z30 , 3) = 5 and aw(Z29 , 3) =
999: 3. Furthermore, aw(Z8 , 4) = 6 and aw(Z7 , 4) = 4 (see Table 4 in Section 3.2).
1000:     Example 3.26 below shows that Theorem 2.17, which bounds the anti-van der Waerden number of a sum
1001: in terms of the anti-van der Waerden numbers of the summands, does not extend to Zn .

1015: 4      Computation
1016: Many of the results we have proved in this paper were first conjectured from examination of data. In this
1017: section, we briefly discuss an efficient algorithm to find an exact r-coloring of [n] or Zn that avoids a rainbow
1018: k-AP, if such a coloring exists. For the sake of brevity, we will focus on the case of coloring [n] since this
1019: case has a few extra properties that the Zn case does not. Specifically, we have [m] ⊆ [n] for all m ≤ n while
1020: Zn contains a copy of Zm if and only if m divides n.
1021:     Fix k, n, and r and assume that all values of aw([m], k) have been computed for k ≤ m < n. Let
1022: c : [n] → [r] ∪ {∗} be a function called a partial r-coloring, where every position i has color c(i) ∈ [r] or
1023: c(i) = ∗ and i is uncolored. By starting with all positions uncolored, we recursively attempt to extend a
1024: partial r-coloring c where the positions in [i] are colored to an exact r-coloring c0 that avoids rainbow k-APs.
1025: We branch at each recursive call for all possible choices of color for c(i + 1) such that no k-AP within [i + 1]
1026: is colored with k distinct colors. To guarantee that no chosen color creates a rainbow k-AP, we maintain a
1027: list of sets D(j) ⊆ [r] that contain all of the possible colors for the position j. Specifically, assigning c(j) to
1042: values of aw([n], k) and aw(Zn , k), extremal colorings, and reports of computation time.

1045: 5      Conjectures and open questions
1046: We conclude by summarizing some open questions and conjectures.
1047:    Uherka [13] observed that aw([n], 3) is not a monotone function in n, as there are values of n where
1048: aw([n], 3) = aw([n − 1], 3) − 1. Does this happen infinitely often? Are larger drops possible?
1049: 
1050: Conjecture 5.1. For positive integers n and k, aw([n], k) ≥ aw([n − 1], k) − 1.
1051:    Conjecture 1.2 states that the lower bound aw([n], 3) ≥ dlog3 ne + 2 is correct to within an additive
1052: constant. We further conjecture that the lower bound in Lemma 2.3 is in fact the exact value when n is a
1053: power of three. It is true for the computed data available (see Remark 2.1).
1054: 
1055: Conjecture 5.2. Let m be a nonnegative integer. Then aw([3m ], 3) = m + 2.
1056: Question 5.3. Is it true that aw([3n], 3) = aw([n], 3) + 1 for all positive integers n?

1110: uses exactly aw(S, k) − 1 colors. Singleton extremal colorings exist for all of our computed examples where
1111: S = Zn and k = 3, as we have seen by the computer computations described in Section 4 for every value of
1112: n ≤ 58. Is this always the case?
1113: Conjecture 5.4. For k = 3, there exists a singleton extremal coloring of [n] and of Zn .
1114:     Proposition 3.14 gives an effective lower bound on aw([n], 3) for n composite with certain odd factors.
1115: If Conjecture 5.4 holds for Zn , then the condition for Proposition 3.14 applies to all factors, implying the
1116: following conjecture.
1117: Conjecture 5.5. For p an odd prime and t ≥ 3, aw(Zpt , 3) ≥ aw(Zt , 3) + aw(Zp , 3) − 2.
1118:     If this conjecture holds, then computing aw(Zn , 3) depends only on the values of aw(Zp , 3) for the prime
1119: factors p of n. Since 3 ≤ aw(Zp , 3) ≤ 4 for all primes p < 100, we consider which primes allow aw(Zp , 3) = 3.
1120: 
1121: Question 5.6. Are there infinitely many primes p such that aw(Zp , 3) = 3?
1122: Question 5.7. Is aw(Zp , 3) > 3 for all p prime, p ≡ 1 (mod 8)?
1123: Question 5.8. Does there exist a prime p such that aw(Zp , 3) ≥ 5?
1124: 
1125:    One suggested approach to finding primes p for which aw(Zp , 3) = 3 is to search for primes p such that
1126: the multiplicative group Z×
1127:                           p is generated by 2. In that case, a singleton extremal coloring of Zp would have
1128: only two colors. However, the existence of an infinite family of such primes is still open.
1129: Conjecture 5.9 (Artin’s Conjecture). [11, p. 217] There are infinitely many primes p such that 2 is a
1130: generator of the multiplicative group Z×
1131:                                        p.
1132: 
1133:    If Artin’s Conjecture holds, we suspect it would give us an infinite family of Zp such that aw(Zp , 3) = 3.

69: with AW (k, n) used to denote our aw([n], k). Other results on balanced colorings of the integers with no

71:     First, we consider the set S = [n]. The value of aw([n], 3) is logarithmic in n:
72: Theorem 1.1. For every integer n ≥ 9,
73: 
74:                                    dlog3 ne + 2 ≤ aw([n], 3) ≤ dlog2 ne + 1.
75: 
76: Moreover, aw([n], 3) = dlog3 ne + 2 for n ∈ {3, 4, 5, 6, 7} and aw([8], 3) = 5.
77:    Theorem 1.1 is proven by Lemmas 2.3 and 2.6 (for n ≥ 9), and Remark 2.1 gives exact values of aw([n], 3)
78: that justify the second statement. We conjecture that the lower bound is, essentially, correct:
79: Conjecture 1.2. There exists a constant C such that aw([n], 3) ≤ dlog3 ne + C for all n ≥ 3.
80: 
81:    The behavior of aw([n], k) is, however, different for k ≥ 4. Instead of logarithmic, it is almost linear:
82: Theorem 1.3. For k ≥ 4,
83:                                       √
84:                                ne−O( log n) < aw([n], k) ≤ ne− log log log n−ω(1) .
85: 
86:    Theorem 1.3 is established by Lemma 2.8 and Corollary 2.14.
87:    Finally, we consider arithmetic progressions in the cyclic group Zn .
88: Remark 1.4. For positive integers n and k, aw(Zn , k) ≤ aw([n], k), because every AP in [n] corresponds to
89: an AP in Zn .
90: 
91:    However, because progressions in Zn may “wrap around,” there are additional APs in Zn , some of which
92: may be rainbow. Thus it is possible that every coloring of Zn with aw([n], k) − 1 colors guarantees a rainbow
93: k-AP, so strict inequality is possible. As was shown in [8, Theorem 3.5] (and follows from Theorem 1.6
94: below), there are infinitely many values of n for which aw(Zn , 3) = 3, for example, when n is a power of two.
95: Definition 1.5. Let n ≥ 3 be an integer. Define f2 (n) to be 0 if n is odd and 1 if n is even. Define f3 (n)
96: to be the number of odd prime factors p of n that have aw(Zp , 3) = 3 and f4 (n) to be the number of odd
97: prime factors p of n that have aw(Zp , 3) = 4, both counted according to multiplicity.
98: Theorem 1.6. For every prime number p, 3 ≤ aw(Zp , 3) ≤ 4. For an integer n ≥ 2, the value of aw(Zn , 3)
99: is determined by the values of aw(Zp , 3) for the prime factors p:
100: 
101:                                    aw(Zn , 3) = 2 + f2 (n) + f3 (n) + 2f4 (n).
102: 
103: For an integer n ≥ 2 having every prime factor less than 100, f4 (n) is the number of odd prime factors of
104: n in the set Q4 := {17, 31, 41, 43, 73, 89, 97} and f3 (n) is the number of odd prime factors of n in Q3 , where
105: Q3 is the set of all odd primes less than 100 and not in Q4 .
106:    Theorem 1.6 is established by Proposition 3.5, Corollary 3.15 and Proposition 3.17.
107:    For k ≥ 4, the bounds we obtain for aw(Zn , k) are the same as those for aw([n], k):

118:     The structure of the paper is as follows: Section 2 presents results pertaining to aw([n], k), with Theorem
119: 1.1 proved in Section 2.1 and Theorem 1.3 proved in Section 2.2. Results pertaining to aw(Zn , k) appear

123:     In the remainder of this section we establish a basic but necessary observation that aw(S, ·) is monotone
124: in k.
125: Observation 1.8. Let G be an additive (abelian) group such as the integers or the integers modulo n, let S
126: be a finite nonempty subset of G, and let k ≥ 3 be an integer. Then aw(S, k) ≤ aw(S, k + 1).
127:     Observation 1.8 follows immediately from Proposition 1.9 below and was noted noted by Uherka in [13]
128: for the function aw([n], ·).

132: no rainbow k-AP, then aw(S, k) ≥ r + 1.

206:    Now we turn to the proof of Theorem 1.1, beginning with the lower bound.
207: Proposition 2.2. Let n be a positive integer and let s ∈ {−2, −1, 0, 1, 2}. Then aw([3n−s], 3) ≥ aw([n], 3)+1
208: provided n ≥ s.
209: Proof. Let r = aw([n], 3) and s ∈ {0, 1, 2}. We construct an exact r-coloring of [3n − s] that does not contain
210: a rainbow 3-AP. By definition there exists an exact (r − 1)-coloring, denoted c, of [n] such that there is no
211: rainbow 3-AP in [n]. Color [3n − s] in the following manner: If i + s is divisible by 3, then ĉ(i) = c((i + s)/3),
212: otherwise ĉ(i) = r. Consider a 3-AP, K, in [3n − s]. Then either the three terms in K + s are all divisible by
213: 3 or at least two of the terms in K + s are not divisible by 3. If all terms in K + s are divisible by 3, then K
214: is not rainbow under ĉ, since there is no rainbow 3-AP under c. If two terms of K + s are not divisible by 3
215: then those two terms are both colored r and K is not rainbow. Hence aw([3n − s], 3) ≥ r + 1 for s ∈ {0, 1, 2}.
216:     For s ∈ {−2, −1}, use the same coloring as for s = 0.
217: 
218:    Using Proposition 2.2, we establish the lower bound in Theorem 1.1.
219: Lemma 2.3. Let n be a positive integer. Then aw([n], 3) ≥ dlog3 ne + 2.
220: Proof. The proof is by induction. The cases n = 1, 2, 3 are true by inspection. Suppose n > 3 and that
221: aw([m], 3) ≥ dlog3 me + 2 for all m satisfying 1 ≤ m < n. We show that aw([n], 3) ≥ dlog3 ne + 2. First, we
222: write n = 3m − s, where s ∈ {0, 1, 2} and 2 ≤ m < n. Then by Proposition 2.2,
223: 
224:      aw([n], 3) = aw([3m − s], 3) ≥ aw([m], 3) + 1 ≥ dlog3 me + 2 + 1 = dlog3 (3m)e + 2 ≥ dlog3 ne + 2.
225: 
226: 
227: Example 2.4. Induction and the proof of Proposition 2.2 produce the following exact (m + 1)-coloring of
228: [3m ] that does not have a rainbow 3-AP: For x ∈ [3m ] with the prime factorization x = 2e2 3e3 5e5 · · · pep ,
229: c(x) = m + 1 − e3 . This attains the value in Lemma 2.3.

237: bi ∈ [n] to be the least x such that the induced coloring on [x] has exactly i colors. Then for all i ∈ [r − 1],
238: bi+1 ≥ 2bi . Furthermore, for any 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ r, we have bj ≥ 2j−i bi .
239: Proof. Observe that b1 = 1. Suppose that bi+1 < 2bi for some i ∈ [r − 1]. Then {2bi − bi+1 , bi , bi+1 } is a
240: rainbow 3-AP. The last statement follows by induction, since bi ≤ 2−1 bi+1 ≤ 2−2 bi+2 ≤ · · · ≤ 2−(j−i) bj .

253: 2.2     Main results for aw([n], k), k ≥ 4
254: In this section we specialize to the case k ≥ 4, focusing on lower and upper bounds that give aw([n], k) =
255: n1−o(1) . Lemma 2.8 gives the lower bound and Corollary 2.14 gives the upper bound.
256:     Let sz(n, k) denote the largest size of a set S ⊆ [n] such that S contains no k-AP (similar notation was
257: introduced in [5] in honor of Szemerédi [12]). Determining bounds on sz(n, k) is a fundamental problem in
258: the study of arithmetic progressions. Behrend [3], Gowers [6], and others [9, 10] have established various
259: bounds on sz(n, k). Proposition 2.7 provides a strong link between sz(n, k) and our anti-van der Waerden
260: numbers, allowing us to use known results on sz(n, k) to bound aw(n, k).

273: Lemma 2.6 and Behrend’s results (stated in Theorem 2.10 and Proposition 2.11 below) show that the upper
274: bound in Proposition 2.7 is not useful for k = 3. Observe that when k ∈ {4, 5}, the lower bound in

276: k ∈ {4, 5} in Lemma 2.8 by carefully studying Behrend’s original construction [3] of a relatively large set
277: S ⊂ [n] that contains no 3-AP, thus giving a lower bound on sz(n, 3).
278:    Let {a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 } be a 4-AP with a1 ≤ a2 ≤ a3 ≤ a4 . A set A ⊂ {a1 , a2 , a3 , a4 } of size |A| = 3 is called
279: a punctured 4-AP. If such a punctured 4-AP A is not a 3-AP, then it is of the form A = {a1 , a2 , a4 } or
280: A = {a1 , a3 , a4 }. We prove that Behrend’s construction in fact contains no punctured 4-AP (Proposition 2.9
281: below). This leads to Lemma 2.8 below.

995: 4    Computation
996: Many of the results we have proved in this paper were first conjectured from examination of data. In this
997: section, we briefly discuss an efficient algorithm to find an exact r-coloring of [n] or Zn that avoids a rainbow
998: k-AP, if such a coloring exists. For the sake of brevity, we will focus on the case of coloring [n] since this
999: case has a few extra properties that the Zn case does not. Specifically, we have [m] ⊆ [n] for all m ≤ n while
1000: Zn contains a copy of Zm if and only if m divides n.
1001:     Fix k, n, and r and assume that all values of aw([m], k) have been computed for k ≤ m < n. Let
1002: c : [n] → [r] ∪ {∗} be a function called a partial r-coloring, where every position i has color c(i) ∈ [r] or
1003: c(i) = ∗ and i is uncolored. By starting with all positions uncolored, we recursively attempt to extend a
1004: partial r-coloring c where the positions in [i] are colored to an exact r-coloring c0 that avoids rainbow k-APs.
1005: We branch at each recursive call for all possible choices of color for c(i + 1) such that no k-AP within [i + 1]
1006: is colored with k distinct colors. To guarantee that no chosen color creates a rainbow k-AP, we maintain a
1007: list of sets D(j) ⊆ [r] that contain all of the possible colors for the position j. Specifically, assigning c(j) to
1071: 
1072:                                                         18
1073: 
1074: 5     Conjectures and open questions
1075: We conclude by summarizing some open questions and conjectures, beginning with those related to [n].
1076:    Uherka [13] observed that aw([n], 3) is not a monotone function in n, as there are values of n where
1077: aw([n], 3) = aw([n − 1], 3) − 1. Does this happen infinitely often? Are larger drops possible?
1078: Conjecture 5.1. For positive integers n and k, aw([n], k) ≥ aw([n − 1], k) − 1.
1079:    Conjecture 1.2 states that the lower bound aw([n], 3) ≥ dlog3 ne + 2 is correct to within an additive
1080: constant. We further conjecture that the lower bound in Lemma 2.3 is in fact the exact value when n is a
1081: power of three. It is true for the computed data available (see Remark 2.1).
1082: Conjecture 5.2. Let m be a nonnegative integer. Then aw([3m ], 3) = m + 2.
1083: Question 5.3. Is it true that aw([3n], 3) = aw([n], 3) + 1 for all positive integers n?
1084:     We now turn our attention to Zn .
1085: 
1086: Question 5.4. Are there infinitely many primes p such that aw(Zp , 3) = 3?
1087:     Based on [8, Theorem 3.5] (see also Theorem 3.3), one approach to finding primes p for which aw(Zp , 3) =
1088: 3 is to search for primes p such that the multiplicative group Z×
1089:                                                                 p is generated by 2. However, the existence
1090: of an infinite family of such primes is still open.
1091: 
1092: Conjecture 5.5 (Artin’s Conjecture). [11, p. 217] There are infinitely many primes p such that 2 is a
1093: generator of the multiplicative group Z×
1094:                                        p.
1095: 
1096:     If Artin’s Conjecture holds, it would give us an infinite family of Zp such that aw(Zp , 3) = 3. Jungić et
1097: al. also established another family of primes p with aw(Zp , 3) = 3 (see Theorem 3.3), namely those primes
1098: p such that p−1                                 ×   p−1
1099:                2 is odd and the order of 2 in Zp is 2 .

138: 1.2. Relation to the literature. There is a plethora of combinatorial puzzles in
139: recreational mathematics that are ‘naturally’ associated with the generation of a
140: particular finite group, e.g. the already-mentioned 15-puzzle, the Rubik’s cube, and
141: many others (see [22] for an excellent treatment of this subject).
142:    In recent years various authors have attempted to study group generation sys-
143: tematically by referring to variants of such recreational puzzles. In 1974, Wilson
144: generalized the 15-puzzle to a puzzle played on an arbitrary simple graph with n
145: vertices, and he studied the groups generated by the associated permutations of
146: vertices [29]; in general the groups obtained contain An , however in one case, when
147: n = 7, the corresponding group is isomorphic to S5 . A variation of Wilson’s game

1150:             n < f (λ).

1162:             More ambitiously, we can ask:
1163:             Question 8.3. For what values of n and λ can one classify the groups π∞ (D) (up
1164:             to isomorphism) of all simple pliable 2-(n, 4, λ) designs D?
1165:               Theorem C constitutes an answer to this question for all values of n and λ ⩽ 2.
1166:             8.2. Codes and Designs. Recall that Conway et al. use the projective plane P3
1167:             to construct the perfect ternary Golay code. They do this by taking a subcode of
1168:             the F3 -rowspace of the incidence matrix of P3 . Using the designs (other than P3 )
1169:             described in this paper for which π∞ (D) is acting primitively on Ω\{∞} but does
1170:             not contain An−1 , we constructed the following three codes in GAP:
1171:                   i) C, the F2 -rowspace of the incidence matrix of D;
1255:                                 0jkl
1256:                                   ..      ..      ..
1257:                                    .       .       .
1258: Thus the first column consists of lines containing pairs of the form {0, x} and second
1259: of those containing {1, x}, and so on. Also, an element added to a line B is chosen
1260: minimally subject to the restriction that it has not already appeared in another
1261: line with elements already in B. Thus two blocks intersect in at most one point
1262: and D is a pliable 2-(∞, 4, 1). Furthermore, this design has a non trivial puzzle
1263: group since, for example, [0, 1, 4, 0] = (1, 4)(2, 3)(7, a)(5, 6).

1265: Question 8.7. Can one describe the puzzle groups of other infinite quadruple sys-
1266: tems?

135:    In §8 we present a conjecture relating to this observed behaviour of π∞ (D), along
136: with a number of open questions, and avenues for future work.
137: 1.2. Relation to the literature. There is a plethora of combinatorial puzzles in
138: recreational mathematics that are ‘naturally’ associated with the generation of a
139: particular finite group, e.g. the already-mentioned 15-puzzle, the Rubik’s cube, and
140: many others (see [22] for an excellent treatment of this subject).
141:    In recent years various authors have attempted to study group generation sys-
142: tematically by referring to variants of such recreational puzzles. In 1974, Wilson
143: generalized the 15-puzzle to a puzzle played on an arbitrary simple graph with n
144: vertices, and he studied the groups generated by the associated permutations of
145: vertices [28]; in general the groups obtained contain An , however in one case, when
146: n = 7, the corresponding group is isomorphic to S5 . A variation of Wilson’s game
1139:                                 8. Open questions
1140:   To conclude the paper we outline several avenues for further work.
1141: 8.1. Possibilities for π∞ (D). We see no reason why Corollary 6.7 should not hold
1142: more generally. More precisely, we make the following conjecture:
1143: Conjecture 8.1. Let D = (Ω, B) be a simple pliable 2-(n, 4, λ) design. For all
1144: λ > 0 there exists f (λ) such that either π∞ (D) acts primitively on Ω\{∞} or
1145: n < f (λ).
1146:    Observe that π∞ (D) is generated by even permutations if λ is odd and by odd
1147: permutations if λ is even. By [19, Corollary 3], Conjecture 8.1 would imply the
1148: following:
1149: Conjecture 8.2. Let D = (Ω, B) be a simple pliable 2-(n, 4, λ) design. For all
1150: λ > 0 there is some g(λ) such that for all n > g(λ),
1151:                               
1152:                             ∼    Sn−1 if λ ≡ 0 (mod 2);
1153:                     π∞ (D) =
1154:                                  An−1 if λ ≡ 1 (mod 2).
1155:   Put another way, Conjecture 8.2 would imply that for each λ > 0, there exist
1156: only finitely many puzzle groups which are not alternating or symmetric groups.
1157: More ambitiously, we can ask:
1158: Question 8.3. For what values of n and λ can one classify the groups π∞ (D) (up
1159: to isomorphism) of all simple pliable 2-(n, 4, λ) designs D?
1160:   Theorem C constitutes an answer to this question for all values of n and λ ⩽ 2.
1161: 8.2. Codes and Designs. Recall that Conway et al. use the projective plane P3
1162: to construct the perfect ternary Golay code. They do this by taking a subcode of
1163: the F3 -rowspace of the incidence matrix of P3 . Using the designs (other than P3 )
1164: described in this paper for which π∞ (D) is acting primitively on Ω\{∞} but does
1165: not contain An−1 , we constructed the following three codes in GAP:
1166:       i) C, the F2 -rowspace of the incidence matrix of D;
1250:                                 0jkl
1251:                                   ..      ..      ..
1252:                                    .       .       .
1253: Thus the first column consists of lines containing pairs of the form {0, x} and second
1254: of those containing {1, x}, and so on. Also, an element added to a line B is chosen
1255: minimally subject to the restriction that it has not already appeared in another
1256: line with elements already in B. Thus two blocks intersect in at most one point
1257: and D is a pliable 2-(∞, 4, 1). Furthermore, this design has a non trivial puzzle
1258: group since, for example, [0, 1, 4, 0] = (1, 4)(2, 3)(7, a)(5, 6).
1259: Question 8.6. What is the puzzle group of the infinite Boolean quadruple system?
1260: Question 8.7. Can one describe the puzzle groups of other infinite quadruple sys-
1261: tems?

1264: this problem is by imposing an ordering on elements of each line. This can be

117:    Secondly, an infinite family of designs has been constructed for which the associated hole stabilizers are primitive
118: but neither symmetric nor alternating. Specifically it is proved that for all m ⩾ 2 the groups O+                 −

122: n = 36) that are listed in Table 1 of §4. (Note that PSp4 (3) : 2 ∼

73: Regularity Lemma, including a proof of the well-known Erdős-Stone theorem.
74:     In Chapter 6, we investigate the robust outexpansion property for digraphs.
75: By showing that a sufficiently large digraph satisfying certain degree conditions is
76: a robust outexpander, we are able to prove an approximate version of a conjecture
77: of Nash-Williams.
78:     We conclude the project by considering what conditions are sufficient to
79: ensure any orientation of a Hamilton cycle in a digraph. That is, we no longer
80: require that the edges are oriented consistently as a directed cycle, the edges
81: may change direction along the cycle.
82:     It should be noted that throughout this project we have omitted floors and
83: ceilings where this does not affect the argument given.

1712: 5.2     Hamilton Cycles in Oriented Graphs
1713: We define an oriented graph to be a digraph which can be obtained by orienting
1714: an undirected simple graph. So an oriented graph does not contain any cycles
1715: of length two. In [11], Keevash, Kühn and Osthus give a bound on the mini-
1716: mum semidegree which ensures a Hamilton cycle of standard orientation in any
1717: sufficiently large oriented graph.
1718:

3875:     In Chapter 6 we defined a robust outexpander, originally introduced by Kühn,
3882: in such graphs because they are fairly common, for example, any sufficiently
3883: large oriented graph with minimum semidegree at least (3/8 + α)n is a robust
3884: outexpander. We also showed that satisfying degree sequence conditions which
3885: are slightly stronger than those in Conjecture 30 implies robust outexpansion in
3886: Lemma 34. In Theorem 42 we showed that we can find a Hamilton cycle in a
3887: robust outexpander of linear minimum degree and hence we were able to prove
3888: an approximate version of Nash-Williams’ conjecture.
3889:     In Theorem 31, Keevash, Kühn and Osthus prove that a minimum semidegree
3890: of (3n − 4)/8 guarantees a Hamilton cycle in a sufficiently large oriented graph.
3891: Interestingly, we saw that this bound no longer suffices when we instead ask for
3892: Hamilton cycles of all possible orientations. In Theorem 44, Kelly showed that
3893: a minimum semidegree of (3/8 + α)n guarantees any orientation of a Hamilton
3894: cycle in any sufficiently large oriented graph.

9:                                                   Abstract. We compute the h1 -localized cohomology of the motivic Steenrod
10:                                                   algebra over C. This serves as the input to an Adams spectral sequence that
11:                                                   computes the motivic stable homotopy groups of the η-local motivic sphere.
12:                                                   We compute some of the Adams differentials, and we state a conjecture about
13:                                                   the remaining differentials.

59: Theorem 1.1. The h1 -localized algebra ExtA [h1−1 ] is a polynomial algebra over
60: F2 [h±1                    4
61:       1 ] on generators v1 and vn for n ≥ 2, where:
62:      (1) v14 is in the 8-stem and has Adams filtration 4.
63:      (2) vn is in the (2n+1 − 2)-stem and has Adams filtration 1.
64:     Although it is simple to state, this is actually a surprising answer. The elements
65: d0 , e0 , and e0 g are indecomposable elements of ExtA that are all h1 -local. From
66: consideration of the May spectral sequence, or from the cohomology of A(2), one
67: might expect to have the relation e30 + d0 · e0 g = 0.
68:     In the terms of Theorem 1.1, e30 + d0 ·e0 g corresponds to h91 v33 + h91 v22 v4 . Theorem
69: 1.1 says that this expression is non-zero after h1 -localization, so it is non-zero before
70: localization as well. The only possibility is that e30 + d0 · e0 g equals h51 B1 .
71:     The relation e30 + d0 · e0 g = h51 B1 in ExtA is hidden in the motivic May spec-
72: tral sequence. As described in [I2], it is tightly connected to the classical Adams
73: differential d3 (h1 h5 e0 ) = h21 B1 [BJM, Corollary 3.6].
74:     Our h1 -local calculation surely implies other similarly exotic relations in higher
75: stems. We do not yet possess a sufficiently detailed understanding of ExtA in that
76: range, so we cannot identify any explicit examples with certainty. However, we
77: expect to see a hidden relation e0 (e0 g)2 + d0 · e0 g 3 = h51 · g 2 B1 in ExtA in the
78: 91-stem. Here, we anticipate that e0 g 3 and g 2 B1 are indecomposable elements.
79:     So far, we have only discussed the entirely algebraic question of computing
91: know that d2 (e0 ) = h21 d0 and d2 (e0 g) = h21 e20 [I2]. This implies the analogous
92: h1 -local differentials d2 (v3 ) = h1 v22 and d2 (v4 ) = h1 v32 .
93:     Unfortunately, we have not been able to identify all of the Adams differentials.
94: We expect the answer to turn out as stated in Conjecture 1.2.

825: 6.1. Motivic modular forms and Adams differentials. In the classical case,
826: the topological modular forms spectrum tmf is a spectrum whose F2 -cohomology is
827: equal to the quotient Acl //A(2)cl . This implies that ExtA(2)cl is the E2 -page of the
828: Adams spectral sequence converging to the 2-complete homotopy groups of tmf .
829:    One might speculate that there is a motivic spectrum mmf (called “motivic
830: modular forms”) whose motivic F2 -cohomology is isomorphic to A//A(2). Then
831: ExtA(2) would be the E2 -page of the motivic Adams spectral sequence converging
832: to the 2-complete motivic homotopy groups of mmf . However, no such motivic
833: spectrum is known to exist. See [NSO] for one piece of the program for constructing
834: mmf .

43:                                                                                          1. Introduction
44:                                              Reduction trees are a way of encoding a substitution procedure dictated by the relations of
45:                                           an algebra. Reduced forms can be read off from reduction trees. We establish a framework for
46:                                           studying reduced forms in several related algebras. We accomplish this by introducing a notion
60: 
61: roots of our framework by constructing shellings of canonical triangulations of flow polytopes and
62: showing how these shellings can be seen on the corresponding reduction trees in the subdivision
63: algebra. As a corollary to our results we settle a conjecture of A.N. Kirillov.
64:    We now highlight our main results. We state them somewhat informally, in order to avoid
65: introducing a lot of notation. They are explained in detail in later sections, and we point back to
66: the corresponding statement here. For the background and definitions see Section 2.
67: Theorem 1. With the reduction order O the simplices corresponding to the leaves of RG        O induce
68:                                                   O
69: a shellable triangulation of FGe . The leaves of RG can be described explicitly in terms of the afore-
70: mentioned shelling.
71: Theorem 2. Specialized shifted multiparameter reduced forms of partial reduction trees with the
72: strong embeddable property equal the h-polynomials of reduction trees. In particular, they have
73: nonnegative coefficients.
74:     We use a generalization of Theorem 2 to settle Kirillov’s [3, Conjecture 7].
75:    The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we define flow polytopes. Next we explain how
76: to subdivide flow polytopes and how we can encode the subdivisions with a reduction tree. Then
77: we define the (multiparameter) subdivision algebra as well as the (multiparameter) associative
78: quasi-classical Yang-Baxter algebra of A.N. Kirillov.
79:    In Section 3 we construct canonical triangulations for flow polytopes and introduce the notion
80: of weak embeddability of reduction trees in order to construct a particular shelling order for the
81: canonical triangulations. In Section 4 we introduce the notion of strong embeddability of reduction
82: trees and indicate how to use it to give a description of the full set of leaves of the reduction tree in
83: the special reduction order O. In Section 5 we study a refinement of the h-vector for our canonical
84: triangulations.
85:    Section 6 parts from geometry and focuses on the structure of reduction trees which became
87: reduction trees, key notions that are seen to unify our proofs. We also define the h-polynomial
88: of a reduction tree and show that it equals the specialized (shifted) reduced form. We generalize
89: our results from reduction trees to partial reduction trees. As a corollary we prove special cases of
90: Conjecture 7 of A.N. Kirillov [3] in Section 7 and demonstrate via counterexamples that Conjecture
91: 7 [3] cannot hold in its full generality.
92:    In Section 8 we prove that our canonical triangulation is indeed a triangulation. We postpone
93: this proof to the end as it is technical, and its ideas are not used elsewhere in the paper.
94: 
95:                                  2. Definitions and Prelimiaries
96:    For completeness, in this section we include several key definitions used throughout the paper,
97: following [8]. For further details see [8].

1223:   The purpose of this section is to provide a proof of Theorem 4 as well as several auxiliary results
1224: used in the text. While we found it easier to define flow polytopes FGe in Definition 6 as a convex
1225: hull of vertices, in this section we will prefer their definition as intersection of halfspaces. As such,
1226: we now proceed to introduce some new notation and then prove Theorem 4.
1227: Definition 27. Fix a graph G on the vertex set [n + 1]. Fix an order on the edges of the complete
1228: graph Kn+1 : e1 , . . . , e(n+1) . Let x1 , . . . , xk be the set of base variables, by which we mean a set
1229:                                2

1231: finitely many nonzero entries whose coordinates ci , i ∈ [m], satisfy the following two properties:
1256:         ijkX is in boldface, it indicates that the corresponding graph is alternating. For

1269: corresponding variable in c. Namely, if (i, j) = eb in the ordering of the edges of Kn+1 and e is the

46: 
47:                                                                                          1. Introduction
48:                                              Nonnegativity properties abound in mathematics, and whenever one arises, the most satisfying
49:                                           explanation of integer nonnegativity is to demonstrate what a certain nonnegative quantity counts.
50:                                           The present paper is written in this spirit and explains nonnegativity properties of polynomials
51:                                           using combinatorial abstractions of geometric ideas. This is a follow up on the paper [10] where
98: for proving nonnegativity results for reduced forms. This technique is related, though different
99: from the one established for proving nonnegativity of shifted reduced forms in subdivision algebras
100: in [10], since our method relies on the study of h-polynomials of reduction trees as opposed to h-
101: polynomials of triangulations. As a corollary to our results we settle a conjecture of A.N. Kirillov,
102: see Theorem 16.
103:    The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we define flow polytopes. Next we explain how
104: to subdivide flow polytopes and how we can encode the subdivisions with a reduction tree. Then
105: we define the (multiparameter) subdivision algebra as well as the (multiparameter) associative
106: quasi-classical Yang-Baxter algebra of A.N. Kirillov.
107:    In Section 3 we construct canonical triangulations for flow polytopes and introduce the notion
108: of weak embeddability of reduction trees in order to construct a particular shelling order for the
109: canonical triangulations. In Section 4 we introduce the notion of strong embeddability of reduction
110: trees and indicate how to use it to give a description of the full set of leaves of the reduction tree in
111: the special reduction order O. In Section 5 we study a refinement of the h-vector for our canonical
112: triangulations.
120: the previous ones. We introduce weak and strong embeddable properties of partial reduction trees,
121: key notions that are seen to unify our proofs. We also define the h-polynomial of a reduction tree
122: and show that it equals the specialized (shifted) reduced form. We generalize our results from
123: reduction trees to partial reduction trees. As a corollary we prove special cases of Conjecture 7 of
124: A.N. Kirillov [5] in Section 7 and demonstrate via counterexamples that Conjecture 7 [5] cannot
125: hold in its full generality.
126:    In Section 8 we prove that our canonical triangulation is indeed a triangulation. We postpone
127: this proof to the end as it is technical, and its ideas are not used elsewhere in the paper.
128:

23:                                           We are interested in the largest family of subsets of [n] := {1, 2, . . . , n} avoiding certain subposets.

31:                                               To establish this theorem, we use a method that is a certain generalization of the so-called circle
32:                                           method of Katona [14]. That is, we double count pairs (Q, F ) that satisfy a certain property where
33:                                           Q is a family of subsets built from a partition of permutation of [n]. What is novel in our paper
34:                                           is that we associate a d-dimensional 0 − 1 hypermatrix to Q and we establish a connection to the
35:                                           theory of forbidden submatrices with the following lemma.

57:    The organization of this paper is the following. In the rest of this section we survey results
58: about forbidden weak and induced posets and forbidden submatrices. Then in Section 2 we prove
59: Lemma 2 and in Section 3 we prove Theorem 3. Finally, we make some further observations and
60: conjectures in Section 4.

62: 1.1   Forbidden weak subposets
63: Definition 1.4. Let P be a finite poset, and F be a family of subsets of [n]. We say that P
64: is contained in F as a weak subposet if and only if there is an injection α : P → F satisfying
65: x1 <p x2 ⇒ α(x1 ) ⊂ α(x2 ) for all x1 , x2 ∈ P . F is called P -free if P is not contained in F as a
66: weak subposet. We define the corresponding extremal function as
67: 
68:                                 La(n, P ) := max{|F| | F is P -free}.
69: 
70:    We denote the number of elements of a poset P by |P |. The linearly ordered poset on k elements,
71: a1 < a2 < . . . < ak , is called a chain of length k, and is denoted by Pk . Using our notation Sperner’s
72: theorem can be stated as follows.

81: of order n. This implies                                     

115: 1.2   Forbidden induced subposets
116: Definition 1.10. We say that P is contained in F as an induced subposet if and only if there is
117: an injection α : P → F satisfying x1 <p x2 ⇔ α(x1 ) ⊂ α(x2 ) for all x1 , x2 ∈ P . F is called induced
118: P -free if P is not contained in F as an induced subposet. We define the corresponding extremal
119: function as
120:                           La# (n, P ) := max{|F| | F is induced P -free}.
121:     Despite considerable progress made on forbidden weak subposets, very little is known about for-
122: bidden induced subposets. The obvious exception is Pk , as here the weak and induced containment
123: are equivalent, thus Theorem 1.6 implies La(n, Pk ) = La# (n, Pk ).

164:    It is interesting to note that the constant C in our upper bound on La# (n, P ) depends on the
165: dimension of P , as opposed to h(P ) in the upper bound on La(n, P ) in Theorem 1.8 and 1.9. This
166: notion is deﬁned as follows.
167: Definition 1.13. The dimension of a poset P is the least integer t for which there exists t linear
168: orderings, <1 , <2 . . . <t , of the elements of P such that for every x and y in P , x <P y if and only
169: if x <i y for all 1 ≤ i ≤ t.

171: 1.3    Forbidden submatrices
172: A d-dimensional hypermatrix is an n1×..×nd size ordered array. For short, we refer to such a
173: hypermatrix as a d-matrix of size nd if n1 = . . . = nd = n. So a vector is a 1-matrix and a matrix is
174: a 2-matrix. We refer to the entries of a d-matrix M as M (i) where i = (i1 , . . . id ) and 1 ≤ ij ≤ nj
175: for every 1 ≤ j ≤ d. A j-column is a set of entries {M (i) | 1 ≤ ij ≤ nj }. In this paper we only deal
176: with d-matrices whose entries are all 0 and 1. We denote the number of 1’s in a d-matrix M by

14:                                                       We prove that for every poset P , there is a constant C such that the size ofany family
15:                                                   of subsets of [n] that does not contain P as an induced subposet is at most C ⌊ nn ⌋ , settling a
16:                                                                                                                                    2
17:                                                   conjecture of Katona, and Lu and Milans [24]. We obtain this bound by establishing a connection
18:                                                   to the theory of forbidden submatrices and then applying a higher dimensional variant of the
19:                                                   Marcus-Tardos theorem, proved by Klazar and Marcus. We also give a new proof of their result.
20: 
21: 
22:                                           1       Introduction
23:                                           We are interested in the largest family of subsets of [n] := {1, 2, . . . , n} avoiding certain subposets.
24:                                              Our main result is the following.
25: 
26:                                           Theorem 1. For every poset P , there is a constant C such that the     size of any family of subsets
27:                                                                                                            n 
28:                                           of [n] that does not contain an induced copy of P is at most C ⌊ n ⌋ .
29:                                                                                                                     2
30: 
31:                                               To establish this theorem, we use a method that is a certain generalization of the so-called circle
32:                                           method of Katona [15]. That is, we double count pairs (Q, F ) that satisfy a certain property where
33:                                           Q is a family of subsets built from a partition of permutation of [n]. What is novel in our paper
34:                                           is that we associate a d-dimensional 0 − 1 hypermatrix to Q and we establish a connection to the
35:                                           theory of forbidden submatrices with the following lemma.
36: 
37:                                           Lemma 2. For every d-dimensional poset P on k elements, there is a d-dimensional k×..×k
61: main result, Lemma 2 that establishes the connection between the two theories. In Section 3 we
62: prove Theorem 3 to make our paper self-contained, and also because our proof substantially diﬀers
63: from the proof in [20] (in fact we have learned about their result only after independently proving
64: Theorem 3). Finally, we make some further observations and conjectures in Section 4.
65: 
66: 1.1   Forbidden weak subposets
67: Definition. Let P be a finite poset, and F be a family of subsets of [n]. We say that P is contained
68: in F as a weak subposet if and only if there is an injection α : P → F satisfying x1 <p x2 ⇒ α(x1 ) ⊂
69: α(x2 ) for all x1 , x2 ∈ P . F is called P -free if P is not contained in F as a weak subposet. We
70: define the corresponding extremal function as
71: 
72:                                 La(n, P ) := max{|F| | F is P -free}.
73: 
74:    We denote the number of elements of a poset P by |P |. The linearly ordered poset on k elements,
75: a1 < a2 < . . . < ak , is called a chain of length k, and is denoted by Pk . Using our notation Sperner’s
76: theorem can be stated as follows.
77: 
78: Theorem 1.4 (Sperner [32]).                                     
79:                                                              n
80:                                         La(n, P2 ) =               .
81:                                                            ⌊n/2⌋
82:    Erdős extended Sperner’s theorem to Pk -free families for all k ≥ 2.
83: 
84: Theorem 1.5 (Erdős [9]). La(n, Pk ) is equal to the sum of the k − 1 largest binomial coefficients
85: of order n. This implies                                     
86:                                                           n
87:                                 La(n, Pk ) ≤ (k − 1)            .
88:                                                         ⌊n/2⌋
89:    Notice that, since any poset P is a weak subposet of a chain of length |P |, Theorem 1.5 implies
90: 
91: Corollary 1.6.                                                   
92:                                                    n             n
93:                            La(n, P ) ≤ (|P |−1)          =O             .
94:                                                  ⌊n/2⌋         ⌊n/2⌋
95:     Later Katona, Tarján, De Bonis, Swanepoel, Griggs, Lu, Li, Thanh, Methuku and Tompkins
96: [7, 8, 11, 13, 17, 26, 36] studied various other posets including brushes, two-end-forks, N , diamond,
97: butterﬂy, skew butterﬂy, cycles C4k on two levels. One of the ﬁrst general results is due to Bukh
98: who obtained bounds on La(n, P ) for all posets whose Hasse diagram is a tree. Let h(P ) denote
99: the height (maximum length of a chain) of P .
100: 
101: Theorem 1.7 (Bukh [3]). If T is a finite poset whose Hasse diagram is a tree of height h(T ) ≥ 2,
102: then                                                  
103:                                                    n
104:                        La(n, T ) = (h(T ) − 1)           (1 + O(1/n)) .
105:                                                  ⌊n/2⌋

109:                                                    2
110: 
111:    Using general structures instead of chains for double counting, Burcsi and Nagy obtained a
112: similar but weaker version of this theorem for general posets. Later this was generalized by Chen
113: and Li for m > 1.
114: Theorem 1.8 (Burcsi-Nagy, Chen-Li [4, 6]). For any poset P , when n is sufficiently large, the
115: inequality                                                               
116:                          1          1 2                                 n
117:            La(n, P ) ≤        |P | + (m + 3m − 2)(h(P ) − 1) − 1
118:                        m+1          2                                 ⌊n/2⌋
119: holds for any fixed m ≥ 1.
120:    Very recently, this general bound was improved by Grósz, Tompkins and the ﬁrst author.
121: Theorem 1.9 (Grósz-Methuku-Tompkins [14]). For any poset P , when n is sufficiently large, the
122: inequality
123:                             1                                    n 

125:                           2                                        ⌊n/2⌋
126: holds for any fixed k ≥ 2.

128: 1.2   Forbidden induced subposets
129: Definition. We say that P is contained in F as an induced subposet if and only if there is an
130: injection α : P → F satisfying x1 <p x2 ⇔ α(x1 ) ⊂ α(x2 ) for all x1 , x2 ∈ P . F is called induced
131: P -free if P is not contained in F as an induced subposet. We define the corresponding extremal
132: function as
133:                           La# (n, P ) := max{|F| | F is induced P -free}.
134:     Despite considerable progress made on forbidden weak subposets, very little is known about for-
135: bidden induced subposets. The obvious exception is Pk , as here the weak and induced containment
136: are equivalent, thus Theorem 1.5 implies La(n, Pk ) = La# (n, Pk ).
137:                                                                                          n 
138:     The ﬁrst result of this type is due to Carroll and Katona [5] who showed that ⌊n/2⌋     (1 + n1 +
139:                              n
140: Ω( n12 )) ≤ La# (n, V2 ) ≤ ⌊n/2⌋ (1 + n2 + O( n12 )). Recently Boehnlein and Jiang extended Theorem
141:                                 

145: h(T ) ≥ 2, then                                          

155: condition.
156:     Note that while La(n, P ) ≤ La# (n, P ), no nontrivial upper bound for general P , was known

158: independently, by Lu and Milans that the analogue of Corollary 1.6 holds for induced posets as
159: well.

177:    It is interesting to note that the constant C in our upper bound on La# (n, P ) depends on the
178: dimension of P , as opposed to h(P ) in the upper bound on La(n, P ) in Theorem 1.7 and 1.8. This
179: notion is deﬁned as follows.
180: 
181: Definition. The dimension of a poset P is the least integer t for which there exists t linear order-
182: ings, <1 , <2 . . . <t , of the elements of P such that for every x and y in P , x <P y if and only if
183: x <i y for all 1 ≤ i ≤ t.

185: 1.3    Forbidden submatrices
186: A d-dimensional hypermatrix is an n1×..×nd size ordered array. For short, we refer to such a
187: hypermatrix as a d-matrix of size nd if n1 = . . . = nd = n. So a vector is a 1-matrix and a matrix is
188: a 2-matrix. We refer to the entries of a d-matrix M as M (i) where i = (i1 , . . . id ) and 1 ≤ ij ≤ nj
189: for every 1 ≤ j ≤ d. A j-column is a set of entries {M (i) | 1 ≤ ij ≤ nj }. In this paper we only deal
237: such that M (i) = 1.
238: 
239:    The most important result about excluded permutation matrices is the theorem of Marcus-
240: Tardos, which was conjectured by Füredi and Hajnal [10] and shown by Klazar [19] to also imply
241: the Stanley-Wilf conjecture.
242: 
243: Theorem 1.14 (Marcus-Tardos [25]). If A is a permutation matrix of size k2 , then ex2 (n, A) ≤
244:       2
245: 2k4 kk n = O(n).
246: 
247:     We need the following straightforward generalization of this result.
248: Theorem 3 (Klazar-Marcus [20]). If A is a permutation d-matrix of size kd , then exd (n, A) =
249: O(nd−1 ).
250:     An equivalent reformulation of Deﬁnition 1.2 gives the following connection between permuta-
251: tion d-matrices and posets of dimension d.
252:

254: d-matrix MP of size kd whose 1-entries are in bijection with the elements of the poset such that if
436: which gives La# (n, D2 ) ≤ (2.583 + o(1)) ⌊ nn ⌋ . For the non-induced case Kramer, Martin and

12:                                                  Abstract.S We conjecture that every infinite group G can be partitioned into countably many
13:                                                  cells G =   An such that cov(An A−1
14:                                                                                    n ) = |G| for each n ∈ ω. Here cov(A) = min{|X| : X ⊆
15:                                                           n∈ω
16:                                                  G, G = XA}. We confirm this conjecture for each group of regular cardinality and for some
17:                                                  groups (in particular, Abelian) of an arbitrary cardinality.
18: 
19: 
20:                                                                                      Introduction
21:                                              For any finite partition of an infinite group, at last one cell of the partition has a rich
22:                                           combinatorial structure. The Ramsey Theory of groups gives a plenty of concrete examples
23:                                           (see [4], [7])
24:                                              On the other hand, the subsets of a group could be classified by their size. For corresponding
25:                                           partition problems see the survey [8].
26:                                              Given a group G and a subset A of G, we denote
27:                                                                         cov(A) = min{|X| : X ⊆ G, G = XA}.
39:                                           history and results behind this problem see the survey [1].
40:                                              In [2, Question F], J. Erde asked whether, given a partition P of an infinite group G such
41:                                           that |P| < |G|, there is A ∈ P such that cov(AA−1 ) is finite. After some simple examples
42:                                           answering this question extremely negatively, we run into the following conjecture.
43:                                                                                                                              S
44:                                           Conjecture. Every infinite group G of cardinality κ can be partitioned G =            An so that
45:                                                                                                                                    n<ω
46:                                           cov(An A−1
47:                                                   n ) = κ for each n ∈ ω.
48: 
49:                                              In this note we confirm Conjecture for every group of regular cardinality and for some groups
50:                                           (in particular, Abelian) of an arbitrary cardinality.
51: 
52:                                             2010 Mathematics Subject Classification. 03E05, 20B07, 20F69.
53:                                             Key words and phrases. partition of groups, covering number.
54:                                                                                              1
55: 
56: 2                               IGOR PROTASOV, SERGII SLOBODIANIUK

59:     For a cardinal κ, we denote by cf (κ) the cofinality of κ.
60: Theorem
127:           n 6= ∅.                                                                
128: Remark 1.
129:    It is well-known [3, Theorems 23.1 and 24.1] that each Abelian group can be embedded
130: into the direct product of countable groups. Applying Theorem 2, we confirm conjecture for
131: Abelian groups. Moreover, if a group G of cardinality κ has an Abelian homomorphic image
132: of cardinality κ (in particular, if G is a free group of rank κ) then Conjecture is valid for G.
133: Remark 2.
134:                                                                  S
135:   Every infinite group G can be written as a union G =                  Hα of subsets of cardinality < κ
136:                                                                α<cf κ
137: (and so cov(Hα Hα−1 ) = κ). Hence, Conjecture holds also if cf κ = ℵ0 .

15:                                                T. Košir and P. Oblak have shown that Q has parts that differ pairwise by at least two
16:                                                [KO]. Such partitions, which are also known as “super distinct” or “Rogers-Ramanujan”,
17:                                                are exactly those that are stable or “self-large” in the sense that Q(Q) = Q.
18:                                                    In 2012 P. Oblak formulated a conjecture concerning the cardinality of Q−1 (Q) when
19:                                                Q is stable with two parts, and proved some special cases. R. Zhao refined this to posit
20:                                                that the partitions in Q−1 (Q) for Q = (u, u − r) with r ≥ 2 could be arranged in an
21:                                                (r − 1) × (u − r) table T (Q) where the entry in the k-th row and `-th column has k + `
22:                                                parts. We prove this Table Theorem, and then generalize the statement to propose a
23:                                                Box Conjecture for the set of partitions Q−1 (Q) for an arbitrary stable partition Q.
