60: 1    Introduction.
61: Fix an infinite field k, denote by Mn (k) the ring of n × n matrices with entries in k acting
62: on the vector space V = kn . Let P be a partition of n and denote by B = JP the nilpotent
63: Jordan block matrix of partition P . Let CB = {A ∈ Mn (k) | AB = BA} be the centralizer of
64: B in Mn (k), and let NB be the subvariety of nilpotent elements in CB .
65:     There has been substantial work in the last ten years studying the map Q that takes P to
66: the Jordan type Q(P ) of a generic element of NB . P. Oblak conjectured a beautiful recursive
67: description of Q(P ). This conjecture appears close to resolved (see Section 5.1, Conjecture
68: 5.2, Remark 5.6, and [BKO, Obl1, Kh1, Kh2, IKh, Bas2]).
69:     An almost rectangular partition is one whose largest part is at most one larger than its
70: smallest part. R. Basili introduced the invariant rP , the smallest number of almost rectangular
71: partitions whose union is P , and showed that Q(P ) has rP parts (Theorem 2.5). T. Košir
72: and P. Oblak showed that Q(P ) is stable: it has parts differing pairwise by at least two
73: (Theorem 2.7). However, even in cases where the Oblak recursive conjecture had been shown
74: some time ago, (as rP = 2 [KO], or rP = 3 [Kh2]) the set Q−1 (Q) remained mysterious. In
75: 2012 P. Oblak made a second conjecture: when Q is a stable partition with two parts, that is
76: Q = (u, u−r) with u > r ≥ 2, then the cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = (r −1)(u−r). In 2013, R. Zhao
77: noticed an even stronger pattern in Q−1 (Q) for such Q. She conjectured that there is a table
78: T (Q) of partitions Pk,` where the number of parts in Pk,` is k +`: see Theorem 1.1 immediately
79: below. We here prove a precise version, the Table Theorem (Theorems 3.14, 3.25). We then
80: propose a Box Conjecture 5.9 describing Q−1 (Q) for arbitrary stable Q (Section 5.2) and we
81: show some special cases where Q has three parts (Section 5.4).
82:     The question, which pairs of conjugacy classes can occur for pairs of commuting matrices
83: reduces to the case where both matrices are nilpotent. There is an extensive literature on
84: commuting pairs of nilpotent matrices, including [GurSe, Obl1, Obl2, KO, IKh, Kh1, Kh2,
85: Bas2] and others, some of whose results we specifically cite. Connections to the Hilbert scheme
86: are made in [Bar, Bas1, BI, Prem, BuEv], and commuting nilpotent orbits occur in the study
87: of Artinian algebras [HW, BI]. However, the study of the map P → Q(P ) seems to be,
88: surprisingly, very recent, beginning with [Bar, Bas1, Pan1, Prem]: apparently, early workers
89: in the area were more drawn to determining commuting vector spaces of matrices of maximum
90: dimension (see [Ma, J, SuTy] and references in the latter). There is further recent work on
91: commuting r-tuples of nilpotent matrices, as [GurSe, GurNg, Ši, NgSi] and these also appear
96: to be connected to the study of group schemes [FrPS, Ng]. There is much study of nilpotent
97: orbits for Lie algebras, as [Gi, BroBru, CoMc, Pan2]; for generalizations of problems considered
98: here to other Lie algebras than sln , see [Pan1]
99:    P. Oblak conjectured the formula |Q−1 (u, u − r)| = (r − 1)(u − r) for the cardinality of
100: the in principle known set of partitions P for which Q(P ) = (u, u − r), where u, r ∈ N with
101: u > r ≥ 2.1 Our main result is
102: Theorem 1.1. Let Q = (u, u − r) where u > r ≥ 2.
103:   i. The cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r) (P. Oblak conjectured this in [Obl2, p. 609, and
104:      Remark 2]).
105: 
106:  ii. The set Q−1 (Q) may be arranged as an (r − 1) × (u − r) array T (Q) of partitions
107: 
108:                                Pk,` = Pk,` (Q), 1 ≤ k ≤ r − 1, 1 ≤ ` ≤ u − r                          (1.1)
109: 
110:        in a natural way, such that the number of parts of Pk,` is k + `.
111: Remark 1.2. We call this the Table Theorem. Below Theorem 3.14 specifies each Pk,` in
112: T (Q), and shows that Q(Pk,` ) = Q; Theorem 3.25 says that T (Q) is all of Q−1 (Q). Some
113: special cases had been shown prior to our work here: P. Oblak had shown it for 2 ≤ r ≤ 4
114: in [Obl2]. R. Zhao in [Z] had shown thes case (u − r) = 1, 2, 3 and also the case u 
131: weighting on UB determined by the sl2 -triple associated to B, where B = JP for an arbitrary
132: partition P . In Section 4.2, where B is again Ju,u−r and so UB = NB , we first show that
133: these equations of loci are sl2 -homogeneous (Lemma 4.2) and then give some examples. In
134: Section 4.3, joint with M. Boij, we conjecture that the closure Z(Pk,` ) is a complete intersection
135: of codimension k + ` − 2 in NB . We also propose equations for Z(Pk,` ).
136:     After reviewing P. Oblak’s recursive conjecture in Section 5.1, we propose in Section 5.2
137: a Box Conjecture for Q−1 (Q), where Q is an arbitrary stable partition (see Conjecture 5.9).
138:    1
139:     The formula has a remarkable symmetry: the proposed value for |Q−1 ((u, u − r))| is the same as that for
140:   −1
141: |Q ((u, r − 1))|. Understanding this symmetry was a goal of R. Zhao in her study of the two sets: it remains
142: obscure to us.
143: 
144: 
145:                                                      3
146: 
147: The stable partitions are those whose parts differ pairwise by at least two, and are called
148: “Rogers-Ramanujan” or “super-distinct” in the partitions literature. The Box Conjecture in
149: short states that if Q is a stable partition with k parts then its key S(Q) gives the lengths
150: of the sides of a k-dimensional box B(Q) containing the elements of Q−1 (Q), and that B(Q)
151: has further regularities. In Section 5.3 we note that the number p(a, Q) of partitions having a
152: given stable diagonal hook partition Q, and a given number a of parts is exactly the analogous
153: number for Q−1 (Q), under the Box Conjecture. This shows that the Conjecture is consistent
154: with known formulas for the number of partitions of n with a parts. In Section 5.4 we show
155: some special cases of the Box Conjecture for Q having three parts.
156:     We believe that this article introduces a new approach to viewing the map Q : P → Q(P ).
157: While our methods are elementary, our results suggest interesting algebraic and geometric
158: explanations and consequences.

160: work on the P → Q(P ) problem, and to Polona Oblak for a second fascinating conjecture
161: that was the start of our work.

164: KTH Stockholm during his visit in May 2014. Discussions during this visit and subsequent
165: collaboration led to the conjectures of Section 4.3 concerning the equations for table loci. We
166: plan to address these jointly in a sequel.

174: 2.1    Notation and Preliminaries.
175: We fix notation and summarize some concepts and results we will need. Let P = (p1 , . . . , ps ) be
176: a partition of the positive integer n. This means that p1 ≥ · · · ≥ ps > 0 and p1 +p2 +· · ·+ps = n.
278: proofs there will use Equation (2.4) and Theorem 2.8 above and may be read independently
279: of the Definition 2.9 of the poset DP . We will use the poset DQ and its elementary maps in an
280: essential way when we describe the equations for the loci of certain Jordan types in Section 4;
281: and we review P. Oblak’s recursive conjecture in Section 5.1. For further discussion of the
282: poset DP see [BIK, KO, Kh1, Kh2, IKh].

1454: Normal pattern.
1455: We say that T (Q) has normal pattern if there are no partitions of type C but not of type A
1456: or B in T (Q) and if also in the first column of T (Q) the type A rows and type B elements
1457: strictly alternate in the first column of the table T (Q). R. Zhao showed that when u >> r
1458: then T (Q) has normal pattern [Z], and in particular |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r) in this case.
1459: For completeness we include a proof of this normal pattern result.
1460: 
1461: Corollary 3.26 (Normal pattern). When u > max{r + r2 /8, 3r/2} then T (Q) has normal
1462: pattern.

1466: is consistent with the number of B rows needed and with the type A or B/C of entry Pr−1,1
1467: needed for T (Q) to have normal pattern. For normal pattern we now need kt = 2t + 2 for

1873: Remark 4.4. We note that here, and also in general for B = JQ , where Q is any stable
1874: partition, the set {A ∈ UB | Q(PA ) = Q} is constructible, being the finite union of locally
1875: closed subsets of UB corresponding to certain loci Z(P ). The phenomenon just observed is
1876: related to the constructibility of this set for Q = (5, 2).
1877: 
1878: 4.3     Proposed equations for table loci.
1879: The following conjectures were developed in collaboration with M. Boij, using calculations in
1880: Macaulay 2 [GS]. T. Košir pointed out to us that the quadratic equations that occur are
1881: polarizations of the 2 × 2 determinant, as described in [KS2], and studied more generally for
1882: the k × k determinant in [KS1]. By “equations of the locus” of P in this section, we mean the
1883: equations of the closure of the locus in UB .
1884: 
1885: Conjecture 4.5 (Table Locus Conjecture). Let B = JQ , Q = (u, u − r), u > r ≥ 2. The
1886: locus Z(Pk,` ) ⊂ UB (with reduced structure) is an irreducible complete intersection defined by
1887: k + ` − 2 equations, of which min{k + ` − 2, r − 2} are linear in the variables of UB and the
1888: rest are quadrics. Each new quadric appearing on the k + ` = s + r diagonal, s ≥ 1 is the sum
1889: of s determinants of 2 × 2 matrices of variables, each having sl2 weight 2(k + ` − 1).
1890: 
1891: Linear Equations.
1892: We state a precise version of the Locus Conjecture for the left hand column of T (Q), then
1893: we generalize it to those equation sets that are entirely linear (k + ` ≤ r), and for the linear
1894: portions of the equations when k + ` > r.
1895: Notation. Given Q = (u, u − r), r ≥ 2 we denote the vertices of DQ by v1,1 , . . . , v1,u from left
1896: to right in the top, longer row, and v2,1 , . . . , v2,u−r from left to right in the second row. We
1897: denote by ai , 1 ≤ i ≤ u − 1 the map taking each vertex v1,k to v1,k+i for k ≤ u − i, and that is
1898: zero otherwise. We denote by bi , 1 ≤ i ≤ u − r − 1, the map taking each vertex v2,k to v2,k+o
1899: 
1900:                                                    34
1901: 
1902: for k ≤ u − r − i, and that is zero otherwise. The map gi , 1 ≤ i ≤ u − r takes each v2,k to
1903: v1,k+i+r−1 , 1 ≤ k ≤ u + 1 − r − i, and gi0 , 1 ≤ i ≤ u − r takes v1,k tov2,k+i , 1 ≤ k ≤ u − r − i and
1904: each takes all other vertices to zero. See Figure 4.3.
1905:

1974: equations to the spaces of jets over classical determinantal varieties studied in [KS1, KS2]. The

1980: 5     The Box Conjecture.
1981: We first state P. Oblak’s conjectured recursive process for obtaining Q(P ) and summarize
1982: what is known in Section 5.1. We then state a Box Conjecture for Q−1 (Q) (Section 5.2).
1983: In Section 5.4 we prove the analog of Theorem 3.14 – that we can fill the box with distinct
1984: partitions in Q−1 (Q) –in the special case that the stable partition Q = (u + s, u, u − r), with
1985: r ≥ 2 and 2 ≤ s ≤ 4.
1986: 
1987: 5.1     The Recursive Conjecture for Q(P ).
1988: P. Oblak conjectured a recursive process (Definition 5.1) for Q(P ) discussed in ([BKO, Kh2,
1989: BIK]). This greatly influenced further work in the area. We state this here, as it is closely
1990: related to the Box Conjecture, to follow. Recall from Definition 2.13 that a U-chain Ca of
1991: DP has three parts, a maximum chain through all the vertices of parts a, a − 1 and two
1992: chains linking the almost rectangular sub partition (ana , (a − 1)na−1 ) to the source and sink
1993: of the poset DP in the top rows.
1994:                              P      Recall also that the length |Ca | satisfies equation (2.4):
1995: |Ca | = ana + (a − 1)na−1 + 2 i>a ni ..
1996:     Given a partition P and integer a ∈ SP we let P 0 = P 0 (P, a) = P − Ca by which we mean
1997: the unique partition obtained by omitting the vertices of Ca from DP and counting the vertices
1998: 
1999: 
2000: 
2001:                                                           36
2012: that Ob(P 0 ), P 0 = P 0 (P, a) has been chosen. Then we set Ob(P ) = (|Ca |, Ob(P 0 )). When
2013: P ` n is almost rectangular we take Ob(P ) = (n).
2014: 
2015: Conjecture 5.2. (P. Oblak)] The map P → Q(P ) satisfies Q(P ) = Ob(P ).
2016: 
2017:     E.R. Gansner, C Greene, D. Kleitman, S. Poljak, M. Saks, and T. Britz and S. Fomin
2018: [Gans, Gre, GreKl, Pol, Sak, BrFo] associate a partition λ(P) to any finite poset by first
2019: setting ci = # most number of vertices covered by i chains of P, then setting λi (P) = ci −ci−1 ,
2020: with c0 = 0. For the poset DP L. Khatami defined a partition λU (DP ) using U-chains in a
2021: similar way, setting ci,U (DP ) = max# vertices covered by i U-chains. As stated above in
2022: Definition 5.1, the partition Ob(P ) might not be well-defined, and originally, P. Oblak chose
2023: the largest integer a giving a maximum length chain in each step. However the second author
2024: showed
2025: 
2026: Theorem 5.3 (Independence). (L. Khatami [Kh1]) The resulting partition Ob(P ) of the Oblak
2034:   L. Khatami also determined the smallest part of Q(P ) using a study of the antichains of
2035: DP in [Kh2]. This, with P. Oblak’s index Theorem 2.8 implies a result we will use later,
2036: 
2037: Theorem 5.5 (Oblak conjecture for rP ≤ 3). The P. Oblak conjecture is true over any infinite
2038: field k when rP ≤ 3.
2039: 
2040: Remark 5.6 (Summary of results on the Oblak recursive process). Thus, the cases rP = 2
2041: [Obl1, KO, BIK, Z] and rP = 3 [Kh2] of the Oblak recursive conjecture have been known
2042: since 2012 (2008 for rP = 2) and the map Q : P → Q(P ) is explicit for rP ≤ 3. Theorem 5.4
2043: of the first and second authors then showed “half” the P. Oblak recursive conjecture in all
2044: characteristics. R. Basili has proposed a proof of the P. Oblak conjecture in [Bas2]. We
2045: note that even a characteristic zero proof – in fact even a proof of the in principle weaker
2046: statement that λ(DP ) = λU (DP ) – is an entirely combinatorial issue, and in combination with
2047: Theorem 5.4 is enough to show the recursive conjecture over any infinite field.6
2048:    6
2049:    In contrast, which pairs of Jordan types occur for A, B with [A, B] = 0 depends on char k: see [BrWi],[McN,
2050: Example 22], and [BIK, Example 2.18].

2056: 
2057: 5.2     The key of a stable partition Q and the Box Conjecture.
2058: We first define the key of Q, which determines the shape of the conjectural box B(Q) of
2059: partitions.

2193: 5.3    Diagonal hook lengths and the Box Conjecture.
2194: We here compare the count of partitions in the putative box B(Q) with the count of the
2195: partitions DHL(Q) having diagonal hook lengths Q. These counts are the same, implying
2196: that the Box Conjecture is consistent with the count of all partitions of n.
2197:    The rank k of a partition is the side of its Durfee square, the largest square that fits into
2198: the upper left corner of its Ferrer’s graph. Let P be any partition. The principal or diagonal
2199: hook-length partition dhl(P ) of P is the sequence
2200: 
2201:                              dhl(P ) = (h11 , h22 , . . . , hkk ), hkk > 0               (5.13)
2202: 
2203: of lengths of the diagonal hooks of P [Gut, Definition 2.2], and it is readily seen to be stable
2204: – have parts that differ pairwise by at least two. Let Q be a stable partition. We denote
2205: by DHL(Q) = dhl−1 (Q) the set of partitions P of n having dhl(P ) = Q. It is easy to show
2206: that they may be arranged in a box of dimensions SQ , such that the (i1 , . . . , ik ) entry has
2207: i1 + · · · + ik parts.
2246:                                       k=1
2247:                                                (1 − t)k          #S=k       i=1
2248: 
2249: Remark 5.16. The Box Conjecture implies that the parts enumerator of B(Q) = Q−1 (Q) is
2250: the same as the parts enumerator EQ (t) for DHL(Q). Thus, counting partitions P of n by their
2251: maximum commuting Jordan type Q(P ) gives the same sums as above in Proposition 5.15
2252: for p(n) or p(a, n): the Box Conjecture is consistent with these known formulas. An explicit
2253: isomorphism between the two sets Q−1 (Q) and DHL(Q), or even a direct count of partitions
2254: having rP = k fixed, would contribute to showing the Box Conjecture.
2255: 
2256: 5.4    Box Conjecture for certain stable partitions Q with three parts.
2257: We now use a method similar to certain steps in the proof of our main Theorem 3.14 to show
2258: the first part of the Box Conjecture– filling the box – in an infinite series of cases when Q has
2259: three parts.
2260: Theorem 5.17. Let r ≥ 2, 2 ≤ s ≤ 4 and Q = (u + s, u, u − r). Then we can fill a box B(Q)
2261: of dimensions (s − 1) × (r − 1) × (u − r) by partitions having Q(P ) = Q.
2262:    These cases correspond to all keys S = (s − 1, r − 1, u − r) with 1 ≤ s − 1 ≤ 3. We
2263: prove this using Theorem 5.5 (the P. Oblak recursive conjecture is known for rP ≤ 3), and
2264: methods similar to those we used in the proof of Theorem 3.14. We now split the Theorem
2265: up into cases, where, in each, we specify the entries of B(Q). That the number of entries is
2266: (s − 1)(r − 1)(u − r) in each case, as claimed, is easy to check.
2267: Lemma 5.18. Let the key be S = (1, r − 1, u − r). So Q = (u + 2, u, u − r) with r ≥ 2, and
2268: u − r ≥ 1. Then
2269:                      B(Q) = {(u + 2, Pk,` )|Pk,` ∈ T ((u, u − r))}.
2270: Proof. For all Pk,` ∈ T ((u, u − r)), the biggest part of Pk,` is at most u so it differs from u + 2
2271: by at least 2. So we obviously get Q((u + 2, Pk,` )) = Q.
2272:                                                                                                   

2274: Lemma 5.19. Let the key be S = (2, r − 1, u − r). So Q = (u + 3, u, u − r) with r ≥ 2, and
2282:                          {(u + 2, Pk,` )|Pk,` ∈ T ((u + 1, u − r)) and k ≥ 2}.
2283: Remark 5.20. Let Q0 be a stable partition with two parts. Then in T (Q0 ), the first part of
2284: the partitions in the same Type A row, or in the same B/C hook is always the same. Also,
2285: as we descend the first column of T (Q0 ), the biggest part of the partition does not increase
2286: (Lemma 3.21).
2287: Proof of Lemma 5.19. Since the biggest part of every partition in T ((u, u − r)) is at most u,
2288: it is obvious that for all Pk,` ∈ T ((u, u − r)), we get Q((u + 3, Pk,` )) = Q, as desired.

15:                                                 T. Košir and P. Oblak have shown that Q has parts that differ pairwise by at least two.
16:                                                 Such partitions, which are also known as “super distinct” or “Rogers-Ramanujan”, are
17:                                                 exactly those that are stable or “self-large” in the sense that Q(Q) = Q.
18:                                                     In 2012 P. Oblak formulated a conjecture concerning the cardinality of Q−1 (Q) when
19:                                                 Q has two parts, and proved some special cases. R. Zhao refined this to posit that the
20:                                                 partitions in Q−1 (Q) for Q = (u, u−r) with r ≥ 2 could be arranged in an (r−1)×(u−r)
21:                                                 table T (Q) where the entry in the k-th row and `-th column has k + ` parts. We prove
22:                                                 this Table Theorem, and then generalize the statement to propose a Box Conjecture for
23:                                                 the set of partitions Q−1 (Q) for an arbitrary partition Q whose parts differ pairwise by
24:                                                 at least two.

59: 1     Introduction.
60: We fix an infinite field k and denote by Matn (k) the ring of n × n matrices with entries in
61: k acting on the vector space V = kn . Let P be a partition of n and denote by B = JP the
62: nilpotent Jordan block matrix of partition P . Let CB = {A ∈ Matn (k) | AB = BA} be the
63: centralizer of B in Matn (k), and let NB be the subvariety of nilpotent elements in CB .
64:     There has been substantial work in the last ten years studying the map Q that takes P to
65: the Jordan type Q(P ) of a generic element of NB . P. Oblak conjectured a beautiful recursive
66: description of Q(P ). This conjecture appears close to resolved (see Section 5.1, Conjecture 5.3,
67: Remark 5.7, and [6, 37, 27, 28, 24, 3]).
68:     An almost rectangular partition is one whose largest part is at most one larger than its
69: smallest part. R. Basili introduced the invariant rP , which is the smallest number of almost
70: rectangular partitions whose union is P , and showed that Q(P ) has rP parts (Theorem 2.5).
71: T. Košir and P. Oblak showed that if the characteristic of k is 0 then Q(P ) has parts that differ
72: pairwise by at least two (Theorem 2.7). Even in cases where the Oblak recursive conjecture
73: had been shown some time ago, (as rP = 2 [29], or rP = 3 [28]) the set Q−1 (Q) remained
74: mysterious. In 2012 P. Oblak made a second conjecture: when Q = (u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2,
75: then the cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r) [38, Remark 2]. In 2013, R. Zhao noticed
76: an even stronger pattern in Q−1 (Q) for such Q. She conjectured that there is a table T (Q)
77: of partitions Pk,` where the number of parts in Pk,` is k + `: see Theorem 1.1 immediately
78: below. We here prove a precise version, the Table Theorem (Theorems 3.13 and 3.31). We
79: then propose a Box Conjecture 5.11 describing Q−1 (Q) for arbitrary partitions Q whose parts
80: differ pairwise by at least two (Section 5.2) and we study some special cases where Q has three
81: parts (Section 5.4).
82:     The question, which pairs of conjugacy classes can occur for pairs of commuting matrices
83: reduces to the case where both matrices are nilpotent. There is an extensive literature on
84: commuting pairs of nilpotent matrices, including [3, 21, 24, 27, 28, 29, 37, 38, 39, 42] and
85: others, some of whose results we specifically cite. Connections to the Hilbert scheme are
86: made in [1, 2, 4, 11, 42], and commuting nilpotent orbits occur in the study of Artinian
87: algebras [4, 23]. However, the study of the map P → Q(P ) seems to be, surprisingly, very
88: recent, beginning with [1, 2, 4, 27, 29, 37, 39, 42]: apparently, early workers in the area were
89: more drawn to determining vector spaces of commuting matrices of maximum dimension (see
90: [25, 33, 45] and references in the latter). There is further recent work on commuting r-tuples of
96: schemes [15, 35]. There is much study of nilpotent orbits for Lie algebras, as in [10, 12, 17, 40];
97: for generalizations of problems considered here to other Lie algebras than sln , see [39]
98:    Our main result is
99: Theorem 1.1. Let Q = (u, u − r) where u > r ≥ 2.
100:  i. The cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r).
101: 
102:  ii. The set Q−1 (Q) may be arranged as an (r − 1) × (u − r) array T (Q) of partitions
103: 
104:                      Pk,` = Pk,` (Q), where 1 ≤ k ≤ r − 1, and 1 ≤ ` ≤ u − r,                (1.1)
105: 
106:     such that the number of parts of Pk,` is k + `.
107: Remark 1.2. We call this the Table Theorem. Theorem 3.13 below specifies each Pk,` in
108: T (Q), and shows that Q(Pk,` ) = Q; Theorem 3.31 says that T (Q) is all of Q−1 (Q). Some
109: special cases had been shown prior to our work here: P. Oblak had shown Theorem 1.1 for
110: 2 ≤ r ≤ 4 in [38]. R. Zhao in [47] had shown the case (u − r) = 1, 2, 3 and also the case u 
111:  r;
112: the latter is the case where T (Q) has a “normal pattern” (Corollary 3.22).
113:     In the formula for cardinality the proposed value for |Q−1 ((u, u − r))| is the same as that
114: for |Q−1 ((u, r − 1))|. Understanding this symmetry was a goal of R. Zhao in her study of the
115: two sets: it remains obscure to us.
116: 
117: Summary.
118: In Section 2.1 we first review some results we will need; in Section 2.2 we recall the poset DP
119: associated to the nilpotent commutator NB of B = JP and more particularly to a maximal
120: nilpotent subalgebra UB of the centralizer CB .
128: the algebraic subsets of UB parametrizing the matrices A ∈ UB with PA = Pk,` . In Section 4.1
129: we describe the grading on UB determined by the sl2 -triple associated to B, where B = JP
130: for an arbitrary partition P . In Section 4.2 we show that the equations of these loci are sl2 -
131: homogeneous (Proposition 4.12). In Section 4.3, joint with M. Boij, we conjecture that the
132: closure Z(Pk,` ) is an irreducible complete intersection of codimension k + ` − 2 in UB , where
133: again B = JQ and Q = (u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2. We also propose equations for Z(Pk,` ). Note
134: that by Proposition 2.9(c) we have UB = NB for the partitions Q = (u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2.
135:     After reviewing P. Oblak’s recursive conjecture in Section 5.1, we propose in Section 5.2
136: the Box Conjecture 5.11 for Q−1 (Q). The combinatorial part of the Box Conjecture in short
137: states that if Q is a partition with k parts differing pairwise by at least two, then its key S(Q)
138: gives the lengths of the sides of a k-dimensional box B(Q) containing the elements of Q−1 (Q).
139: 
140:                                                 3
141: 
142: In Section 5.3 we note that the number of partitions having a given diagonal hook partition
143: Q, and a given number a of parts is exactly the analogous number for Q−1 (Q), under the Box
144: Conjecture. This shows that the combinatorial part of the Box Conjecture is consistent with
145: known formulas for the number of partitions of n having a parts. In Section 5.4 we show some
146: special cases of the Box Conjecture for Q having three parts.
147:    We believe that this article introduces a new approach to viewing the map Q : P → Q(P ).
148: While our methods are elementary, our results suggest interesting algebraic and geometric
149: explanations and consequences.
150: 
151: 
152: 2     Preliminaries and Background.
153: 2.1     Notation and Preliminaries.
154: We fix notation and summarize some concepts and results we will need. Let P = (p1 , . . . , ps )
155: be a partition of the positive integer n having s parts. This means that p1 ≥ · · · ≥ ps > 0 and
156: p1 + p2 + · · · + ps = n. We denote by SP the set of parts of P , i.e. SP = {p1 , p2 , . . . , ps }. Note
157: that 1 ≤ |SP | ≤ s. Recall that the Ferrers diagram of P has rows whose lengths are the parts
236: union is P .
237: Theorem 2.5. ([2]) The partition Q(P ) has rP parts.
238:     The following result is shown for char k = 0 in [39, Theorem 2.1 and Example 2.5a], and
239: for general infinite k in [4, Theorem 1.12]. We say that a partition P is stable if Q(P ) = P .
240: Theorem 2.6. A partition is stable if and only if its parts differ pairwise by at least two.
241:   A partition whose parts differ pairwise by at least two is termed “super distinct” or “Rogers-
242: Ramanujan” in the literature on partitions.
243: 
244:                                                5
245: 
246: Theorem 2.7. ([29]) Suppose that char k = 0 or char k = p > n. Then the partition Q(P )
247: has parts that differ pairwise by at least two.
248: 
249:     The proof of Theorem 2.7 depends on showing that when B = JP and the matrix A ∈ NB
250: is generic, then the Artinian ring k[A, B] is Gorenstein, so – in height two – is a complete
1284: Examples 3.21, 3.26 and 3.27. Cases (b) and (c) for Pr−1,1 are seen respectively in Example 3.23
1285: and Example 3.24.

1483: Normal pattern.
1484: We say that T (Q) has normal pattern if
1485:     (i) T (Q) contains no partitions that are of type C but not of type A or B, and
1486:     (ii) in the first column of T (Q) the type A and type B partitions strictly alternate.
1487:     R. Zhao showed in [47] that when u >> r then T (Q) has normal pattern, and in particular
1488: that |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r) in this case. For completeness we include a different proof of
1489: this normal pattern result using Theorem 3.13.
1490: Corollary 3.22. When u > max{r + r2 /8, 3r/2} then T (Q) has normal pattern.
1491: Proof. Condition (i) in the definition of normal pattern is immediate from Corollary 3.7 , since
1492: u > r + r2 /8. We now show condition (ii), that is Pk,1 is of type A if k is odd, and of type B
1493: (but not of type A) if k is even, for all k ∈ {1, 2, . . . , r − 1}. First, observe that since u > r/2

2420: statement also holds for the analogous ideals in k[End(V )] and k[CB ].
2421: Remark 4.14. Let N (P ) be the set of nilpotent elements A ∈ Matn (k) having Jordan type
2422: P . By the Gerstenhaber-Hesselink Theorem for sln , the Zariski closure N (P ) of N (P ) in

2426:                                    Z(P ) ⊂ {A ∈ UB | PA ≤ P }.                                (4.28)
2427: 
2428: However in general this is a proper inclusion, as we shall see in Example 4.15, for Q = (5, 2)
2429: and P = P1,2 = (5, 1, 1), see (4.30). Since UB is irreducible, the closure of Z(Q) is all of UB .
2430: Example 4.15 (Equations of table loci for Q = (5, 2)). Let Q = (5, 2), and B = JQ .
2431:    Recall that a generic element A of UB has the form (4.17). In Figure 5 we give the
2432: table T (Q), and we give in the table E(Q) for each partition Pij ∈ T (Q) the corresponding
2433: conditions for A ∈ UB to belong to Z(Pij ). We now show that these are the correct conditions.
2434: Recall that since it commutes with B, an element A ∈ UB is uniquely determined by its action
2481: from Theorem 3.13 to index the entries of T (Q): P1,1 = Q, P1,2 = (5, [2]2 ) = (5, 1, 1), P2,1 =
2482: (4, [3]2 ) = (4, 2, 1) and P2,2 = (4, [3]3 ) = (4, 1, 1, 1). The equations in E(Q) show that Z(P2,1 ) ⊃
2483: Z(P2,2 ). On the other hand Z(P1,2 ) does not contain Z(P2,1 ) or Z(P2,2 ) even though P2,1 is
2484: greater than both P2,1 and P2,2 in the Bruhat order! The relation among the table loci Z(Pk,` )
2485: and, more generally, among the loci Z(P ) in UB seems hard to predict, as the next two claims
2486: demonstrate. In Section 4.3 we conjecture the equations of the loci Z(Pk,` ) in UB .
2487: Claim. We have the following equality in UB :
2488:

2490:

2531: the inclusion is strict.                                                                        
2532: Jordan types of matrices commuting with B = JQ , Q = (5, 2). Since Q(Q) = Q the
2533: Jordan types of elements of UB do not include the partitions (7) or (6, 1) that are greater than
2534: Q in the Bruhat order. We have seen that P = (4, 3) does not occur as a partition PA for A
2535: in UB . It is straightforward to show that all other partitions of 7 occur as Jordan types for
2536: matrices A ∈ UB .
2537:    Let Q0 be (another) stable partition of n. The algebraic set Q−1 (Q0 ) ⊂ UB , being the finite
2538: union of locally closed sets of the form Z(P 0 ) | Q(P 0 ) = Q0 , is constructible. For example,
2539: when Q = (5, 2), Q−1 ((6, 1)) ⊂ UB is the union of Z((3, 3, 1)), Z((3, [4]3 )) and Z((3, [4]4 )).

2541: 4.3    Proposed equations for the table loci when Q = (u, u − r).
2542: In this subsection we fix Q = (u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2, and let B = JQ . The following
2543: conjectures were developed in collaboration with M. Boij, using calculations in Macaulay 2
2544: [20]. T. Košir pointed out to us that the quadratic equations that occur are polarizations of the
2545: 2 × 2 determinant, as described in [31], and studied more generally for the k × k determinant
2546: in [30]. By “equations of the locus” of P we mean a specific minimal set of generators of
2547: I(Z(P )). Recall that I(Z(P )) is the set of functions in k[UB ] vanishing on Z(P ), it is a radical
2548: ideal of k[UB ]. We first give the larger picture, then we will specify the conjectured equations
2549: for each Pk,` in the table T (Q).
2550: 
2551: Conjecture 4.16 (Table Loci Conjecture). Let B = JQ , Q = (u, u − r), u > r ≥ 2. The
2552: ideal I(Z(P )) for P = Pk,` is an irreducible complete intersection generated by k + ` − 2
2553: specific sl2 -homogeneous polynomials of which min{k + ` − 2, r − 2} equations are linear in
2554: the variables of k[UB ], and the rest are quadrics. As k + ` increases along a fixed A row or a
2555: fixed B/C hook, in each step we adjoin a new equation to the set of equations for the locus
2556: of the previous partition. Each new quadric appearing in the locus of a partition Pk,` on the
2557: k + ` = s + r diagonal in T (Q) is the sum of s determinants of 2 × 2 matrices of variables,
2558: and has sl2 weight 2(k + ` − 1).
2559: 
2560:    We state a precise version of the Table Loci Conjecture first for the loci of partitions in
2561: the left hand column of T (Q), which require only linear equations. Then we generalize it for
2562: the other table loci, which, when k + ` > r involve quadratic equations.
2563: We use the coordinates on UB of Definition 4.6. For µ ∈ {0, 1, . . . , u − 1} and ν ∈ {0, 1, . . . u −
2564: 
2565: 
2566:                                                  46
2567: 
2568: r − 1} we denote by E(µ, ν) the set of variables
2569: 
2570:                                        a1 , · · · , aµ ; b1 , · · · , bν .                 (4.33)
2571: 
2572: Note that E(0, 0) = ∅.
2573: 
2574: Conjecture 4.17 (Loci equations for the left hand column of T (Q)). Let k ∈ {1, . . . , r − 1}.

2636: Remark 4.22. We have made some progress in showing these conjectures, which we plan to
2637: report in [7]. The Ljubljana colleagues T. Košir, P. Oblak and K. Šivic have connected these
2638: equations to the spaces of jets over classical determinantal varieties studied in [30, 31]. The
2639: conjectures above would suggest that our division of the tables T (Q) into A rows and B/C
2640: hooks is the combinatorial “shadow” of an underlying geometric structure.

2643: 5     The Box Conjecture.
2644: We first recall P. Oblak’s Recursive Conjecture for Q(P ) and summarize results about it in
2645: Section 5.1. In Section 5.2 we state a Box Conjecture for Q−1 (Q) which is a generalization
2646: of Theorems 3.13 and 3.31. Section 5.3 links the conjectured cardinality of Q−1 (Q) with the
2647: number of partitions for which the lengths of the diagonal hooks are given by Q. In Section 5.4
2648: we prove the analog of Theorem 3.13 in the special case that Q = (u + s, u, u − r), with r ≥ 2
2649: and 2 ≤ s ≤ 4.
2650: 
2651: 5.1     Recursive Conjecture for Q(P ).
2652: P. Oblak conjectured a recursive process for determining Q(P ) from P that greatly influenced
2653: further work in the area ([6, 29, 27, 5, 24]). We state this process below in Definition 5.1.
2654:     Recall from Definition 2.15 that a U-chain Ca of DP is comprised of three parts: first
2655: a chain through all the vertices in the rows of length a, a − 1, corresponding to the almost
2656: rectangular subpartition (ana , (a − 1)na−1 ) of P ; then two chains linking those rows to the
2657: source and to the sink in the top rowPof DP . Recall also that the length |Ca | satisfies equation
2658: (2.4): |Ca | = ana + (a − 1)na−1 + 2 i>a ni .
2659:     Given a partition P of n and an integer a ∈ SP we denote by P 0 (P, a) the unique partition
2660: of (n − |Ca |) obtained by omitting the vertices of the chain Ca from DP and counting the
2661:                                                                                                 0
2662: vertices left in each row. When P = (· · · , ini , · · · ) we have that P 0 (P, a) = (· · · , ini , · · · )
2663: where the multiplicity integers n0i satisfy
2690: of Definition 5.1 is independent of the choices of maximum length U-chains in Definition 5.1
2691: and is equal to λU (P ).
2692: 
2693: Conjecture 5.3 (Oblak Recursive Conjecture). The map P → Q(P ) satisfies Q(P ) = Ob(P ).
2694: 
2695:     Evidently from Definition 2.10, the poset DP is independent of char k. Since Ob(P ) is a
2696: combinatorial invariant of D(P ) the Recursive Conjecture implies that Q(P ) is independent
2697: of char k. Also, by definition λ(DP ) ≥ λU (P ) in the Bruhat order (3.27). The first and second
2698: author showed:
2699: 
2700: Theorem 5.4. [24, Theorem 3.9] Let k be an infinite field. Then Q(P ) ≥ λU (P ).
2701: 
2702:    L. Khatami studied the smallest part of Q(P ) and defined a somewhat subtle combinatorial
2703: invariant µ(P ) [28, Definition 2.6]. Using a study of the antichains of DP she showed
2704: 
2705: Theorem 5.5. [28, Theorem 4.1] Let P be a partition of n and let k be an infinite field. The
2706: three partitions λ(DP ), λU (P ) and Q(P ) have the same smallest part, which is equal to µ(P ).
2707: 
2708:    This, with P. Oblak’s Index Theorem 2.8 implies the following result we will use later.
2709: 
2710: Theorem 5.6 (Oblak conjecture for rP ≤ 3). The Oblak Recursive Conjecture 5.3 is true
2711: over any infinite field k when rP ≤ 3.
2712: 
2713: Remark 5.7 (Summary of results on the Oblak Recursive Conjecture). Thus, the cases rP = 2
2714: [37, 29, 5, 47] and rP = 3 [28] of the Conjecture have been known since 2012 (2008 for rP = 2)
2715: and the map Q : P → Q(P ) is explicit for rP ≤ 3. Theorem 5.4 of the first and second authors
2716: then showed “half” the Conjecture in all characteristics. R. Basili has proposed a proof of the
2717: Oblak Conjecture in [3]. We note that a characteristic zero proof of the Conjecture implies its
2718: truth over any infinite field,4 since Theorem 5.4 implies that it is equivalent to λ(DP ) = λU (DP )
2719: – an entirely combinatorial issue.
2720:   4
2721:    In contrast, which pairs of Jordan types occur for A, B with [A, B] = 0 depends on char k: see [8],[34,
2722: Example 22], and [5, Example 2.18].
2723: 
2724: 
2725: 
2726:                                                    49
2727: 
2728: Lemma 5.8. [27, Proposition 2.7] Let P be a partition of n. Then λU (P ) has parts that differ
2732: has parts that differ pairwise by at least two over any infinite field k when rP ≤ 4. When
2733: rP ≤ 3 this is because λU (P ) = Q(P ) = λ(DP ).

2761: We now state a conjecture generalizing the Table Theorem (i.e. Theorems 3.13, and 3.31) and
2762: Conjecture 4.16.
2763: Conjecture 5.11 (Box Conjecture). Let Q be a partition having k parts that differ pairwise
2764: by at least two, and assume that the key of Q is SQ = (s1 , s2 , . . . , sk ).
2765: (a) There is an s1 × s2 · · · × sk array (“box”) B(Q) of distinct partitions

2781: Remark 5.12. In principle Oblak’s Recursive Conjecture 5.3 for the map P → Q(P ) should
2782: allow to decide the purely combinatorial parts (a)-(b) of the Box Conjecture. We follow this
2783: strategy in Section 5.4 to prove part (a) of the Box Conjecture for certain Q. Of course, a
2784: deeper understanding of B(Q) and Conjecture 5.11 could very well give a new approach to
2785: showing Conjecture 5.3.
2786:     Note that when si = 1 there is no contribution of this part of the key to the conjectured
2787: cardinality |Q−1 (Q)|, a fact that was known at least in the case when SQ = (1, 1, . . . , 1, sk )
2788: (see [38, Theorem 4.1]). Since the cases where k = 3 and s1 , s2 , or s3 is 1 are relatively easy,
2789: we give below several (of many) examples where we have verified Conjecture 5.11 (a),(b) for
2790: a partition Q with k = 3 parts, where no si is equal to 1.

2830: 5.3     Diagonal hook lengths and the Box Conjecture.
2831: Part (b) of the Box Conjecture asserts that |Q−1 (Q)| = |B(Q)| = Πki=1 si for Q a partition with
2832: k parts that differ pairwise by at least two. In Lemma 5.16 we observe that the set DHL(Q)
2833: of partitions having diagonal hook lengths Q has this cardinality. This means that our Box
2834: Conjecture is consistent with the count of all partitions of n, according to their numbers of
2835: parts (see Remark 5.18).
2836:     The rank k = k(P ) of a partition P is the side of its Durfee square, the largest square that
2837: fits into the upper left corner of its Ferrers graph. Let P be any partition. The principal or
2838: diagonal hook-length partition dhl(P ) of P is the sequence
2839:                                dhl(P ) = (h11 , h22 , . . . , hkk ), k = k(P )                   (5.11)
2840: of lengths of the diagonal hooks of P [22, Definition 2.2]. It is readily seen that dhl(P ) has
2841: parts that differ pairwise by at least two. Let Q be such a partition. We denote by DHL(Q)
2842: the set of partitions P of n for which dhl(P ) = Q. We expect that the following lemma is well
2843: known. We provide a proof because we could not find a specific reference.
2844: Lemma 5.16. Let Q = (q1 , q2 , . . . , qk ) with q1 ≥ q2 ≥ · · · ≥ qk > 0 be a partition whose parts
2845: differ pairwise by at least two. Let SQ = (s1 , s2 , . . . , sk ) be the key of Q (see Definition 5.9).
2929: 
2930:                       {Q ` n | the parts of Q differ pairwise by at least two}.

2933:                                                XY

2935:                                                       Q
2936:            Q
2937:     where SQ is the product of the components of the key SQ of Q. Lemma 5.16 and (5.15)
2938:     prove that this consequence of the Box Conjecture is true.

2944:     isomorphism between DHL(Q) and D−1 (Q). Conversely, the existence of a number of
2945:     parts preserving bijection DHL(Q) → Q−1 (Q) would, by Lemma 5.16, imply part (b) of
2946:     the Box Conjecture.
2947:

2950:     page 2).

2952: 5.4    Box Conjecture for certain partitions Q with three parts.
2953: In this section we use a method similar to certain steps in the proof of our main Theorem 3.13
2954: to show part (a) of the Box Conjecture– “filling the box” – for a few (infinite) families of
2955: partitions Q with three parts. Recall from Theorem 5.6 and Lemma 5.8 that if rP ≤ 3 then
2956: Q = Q(P ) has parts that differ pairwise by at least two.
2957: 
2958: Theorem 5.19. Let Q = (u + s, u, u − r) with 2 ≤ s ≤ 4 and 2 ≤ r. There is an array B(Q)
2959: of dimensions (s − 1) × (r − 1) × (u − r) of partitions
2960: 
2961:                   B(Q) = {Pj,k,` , 1 ≤ j ≤ s − 1, 1 ≤ k ≤ r − 1, 1 ≤ ` ≤ u − r}              (5.17)
2962: 
2963: such that Q(Pj,k,` ) = Q and Pj,k,` has j + k + ` parts.
2964: 
2965:    This theorem covers all keys SQ = (s−1, r −1, u−r) with 1 ≤ s−1 ≤ 3. Like Theorem 3.13
2966: we prove it using Oblak’s Recursive Conjecture, which is known for rP ≤ 3 (see Theorem 5.6).
2967: We now split Theorem 5.19 into three cases, s = 2 in Lemma 5.20, s = 3 in Lemma 5.22 and
2968: s = 4 in Proposition 5.23.
2969: 
2970: Lemma 5.20. Let Q = (u + 2, u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2. For k ∈ {1, . . . , r} and ` ∈
2971: {1, . . . , u − r} put
2972:                                P1,k,` = (u + 2, Pk,` ),
2973: where Pk,` is the partition in T ((u, u − 3)) defined in Theorem 3.13. Then Q(P1,k,` ) = Q and
2974: P1,k,` has 1 + k + ` parts for all k ∈ {1, . . . , r} and ` ∈ {1, . . . , u − r}
2975: 
2976: Proof. For all Pk,` ∈ T ((u, u − r)), the largest part of Pk,` is at most u so it differs from u + 2
2977: by at least 2. It follows from Theorem 5.6 that Q((u + 2, Pk,` )) = Q. The claim about the
3136: The assertion about the number of parts of Pj.k,` follows readily by applying Theorem 3.13(d).

3139: Remark 5.24. We note that Theorem 5.19 confirms part (a) of Conjecture 5.11 for the
3140: partitions Q under consideration, but does not show part (b).
3141: Example 5.25. In Figure 7, where we write Q−1 (Q) for T (Q), we specify the box B(Q) for
3142: Q = (11, 7, 3). Since the key of Q is SQ = (3, 3, 3), B(Q) is a 3 × 3 × 3 cube. Observe how the
3143: picture suggests a decomposition of B(Q) into partial rows, and possibly three-dimensional
3144: hooks, generalizing Corollary 3.15.

15:                                                 T. Košir and P. Oblak have shown that Q has parts that differ pairwise by at least two.
16:                                                 Such partitions, which are also known as “super distinct” or “Rogers-Ramanujan”, are
17:                                                 exactly those that are stable or “self-large” in the sense that Q(Q) = Q.
18:                                                     In 2012 P. Oblak formulated a conjecture concerning the cardinality of Q−1 (Q) when
19:                                                 Q has two parts, and proved some special cases. R. Zhao refined this to posit that
20:                                                 the partitions in Q−1 (Q) for Q = (u, u − r) with u > r > 1 could be arranged in an
21:                                                 (r − 1) × (u − r) table T (Q) where the entry in the k-th row and `-th column has k + `
22:                                                 parts. We prove this Table Theorem, and then generalize the statement to propose a
23:                                                 Box Conjecture for the set of partitions Q−1 (Q) for an arbitrary partition Q whose parts
24:                                                 differ pairwise by at least two.

53: 
54: 1     Introduction.
55: We fix an infinite field k and denote by Matn (k) the ring of n × n matrices with entries in
56: k acting on the vector space V = kn . Let P be a partition of n and denote by B = JP the
57: nilpotent Jordan block matrix of partition P . Let CB = {A ∈ Matn (k) | AB = BA} be the
58: centralizer of B in Matn (k), and let NB be the subvariety of nilpotent elements in CB .
59:     There has been substantial work in the last ten years studying the map Q that takes
60: P to the Jordan type Q(P ) of a generic element of NB . P. Oblak conjectured a beautiful
61: recursive description of Q(P ). This conjecture remains open in general (for progress on it see
62: Section 4.1, Conjecture 4.3, Remark 4.7, and [3, 6, 22, 25, 26, 34]).
63:     An almost rectangular partition is one whose largest part is at most one larger than its
64: smallest part. R. Basili introduced the invariant rP , which is the smallest number of almost
65: rectangular partitions whose union is P , and showed that Q(P ) has rP parts (Theorem 2.4).
66: T. Košir and P. Oblak showed that if the characteristic of k is 0 then Q(P ) has parts that differ
67: pairwise by at least two (Theorem 2.6). Even in cases where the Oblak recursive conjecture
68: had been shown some time ago, (as rP = 2 [27], or rP = 3 [26]) the set Q−1 (Q) remained
69: mysterious. In 2012 P. Oblak made a second conjecture: when Q = (u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2,
70: then the cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r) [35, Remark 2]. In 2013, R. Zhao noticed
71: an even stronger pattern in Q−1 (Q) for such Q. She conjectured that there is a table T (Q)
72: of partitions Pk,` where the number of parts in Pk,` is k + `: see Theorem 1.1 immediately
73: below. We here prove a precise version, the Table Theorem (Theorems 3.12 and 3.19). We
74: then propose a Box Conjecture 4.11 describing Q−1 (Q) for arbitrary partitions Q whose parts
75: differ pairwise by at least two (Section 4.2), and we study some special cases where Q has
76: three parts (Section 4.3).
77:     The question, which pairs of conjugacy classes can occur for pairs of commuting matrices
78: reduces to the case where both matrices are nilpotent. There is an extensive literature on
79: commuting pairs of nilpotent matrices, including [3, 19, 22, 25, 26, 27, 34, 35, 36, 39] and
80: others, some of whose results we specifically cite. Connections to the Hilbert scheme are made
81: in [1, 2, 4, 10, 20, 31, 39], and commuting nilpotent orbits occur in the study of Artinian
82: algebras [4, 21]. However, the study of the map P → Q(P ) seems to be, surprisingly, very
83: recent, beginning with [1, 2, 4, 25, 27, 34, 36, 39]: apparently, early workers in the area were
84: more drawn to determining vector spaces of commuting matrices of maximum dimension (see
85: [24, 29, 42] and references in the latter). There is further recent work on commuting r-tuples
92: 
93:                                                 2
94: 
95: Theorem 1.1. Let Q = (u, u − r) where u > r ≥ 2.
96: 
97:  i. The cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = (r − 1)(u − r).
98: 
99:  ii. The set Q−1 (Q) may be arranged as an (r − 1) × (u − r) array T (Q) of partitions
100: 
101:                          Pk,` = Pk,` (Q), where 1 ≤ k ≤ r − 1, and 1 ≤ ` ≤ u − r,                       (1.1)
102: 
103:       such that the number of parts of Pk,` is k + `.
104: 
105: Remark 1.2. We call this the Table Theorem. Theorem 3.12 below specifies each partition
106: Pk,` in the array or table T (Q), and shows that Q(Pk,` ) = Q; the Completeness Theorem 3.19
107: says that T (Q) is all of Q−1 (Q). Our main tool is P. Oblak’s result giving the largest part of
108: Q(P ), see [34] and Theorem 2.8 below. Some special cases had been shown prior to our work
109: here: P. Oblak had shown Theorem 1.1 for 2 ≤ r ≤ 4 in [35]. R. Zhao in [46] had shown the
110: case (u − r) ∈ {1, 2, 3} and also the case u 
111:  r.
112:     In the formula for cardinality the proposed value for |Q−1 ((u, u − r))| is the same as that
113: for |Q−1 ((u, r − 1))|. Understanding this symmetry was a goal of R. Zhao in her study of the
114: two sets: it remains obscure to us.
115: 
116:    We illustrate Theorem 1.1 in Example 1.4 below (see also Example 3.16). We first introduce
117: almost rectangular partitions1 , whose importance for the problem of describing the map P →
118: Q(P ) was first noted by R. Basili [2].
119: 
120: Definition 1.3 (Almost Rectangular). A partition P = (p1 , p2 , . . . , ps ) of n with p1 ≥ p2 ≥
121: · · · ≥ ps > 0 is almost rectangular if p1 − ps ≤ 1. For 1 ≤ k ≤ n we denote by [n]k the unique
122: almost rectangular partition of n that has k parts (see equation (2.1)).

158: Example 1.4. We illustrate Theorem 1.1 by giving the table T (Q) for Q = (13, 3) (Table 1.1)
159: and for Q = (14, 4) (Table 1.2). The entries of the table use the notation [n]k for the almost
160: rectangular partition of n having k parts. The table T (Q) has a decomposition into “A-rows”
161: and “B/C hooks” (see Definition 3.13, and Remark 3.15): both Table 1.1) and Table 1.2)
162: show horizontal rows or partial rows comprised of type A partitions (these cells of the table
163: are unshaded), and type B/C hooks (each hook is shaded or colored). For the definition of
164: type A,B,C partitions see Definition 3.1. For Q = (13, 3) the table T (Q) has three B/C hooks:
165: of the form {(5, [11]k ), 4 ≤ k ≤ 11}, {([7]2 , [9]k ), 5 ≤ k ≤ 9}, and {([9]3 , [7]k ), 6 ≤ k ≤ 7}. For
166: Q = (14, 4) the table T (Q) has four B/C hooks: the fourth B/C “hook” is comprised of a
167: single cell P = ([12]4 , [6]6 ).

169: Overview.
177: alternating pattern case first shown by R. Zhao [46], which occurs for u 
178:  r.2 In Section 3.4 we
179: show that the table T (Q) is the complete inverse image of Q under the map Q (Theorem 3.19).
180:     After reviewing P. Oblak’s recursive conjecture in Section 4.1, we propose in Section 4.2
181: the Box Conjecture 4.11 for Q−1 (Q). The combinatorial part of the Box Conjecture in short
182: states that if Q is a partition with k parts differing pairwise by at least two, then its key SQ
183: gives the lengths of the sides of a k-dimensional box B(Q) containing the elements of Q−1 (Q).
184: In Section 4.3 we show elements of the Box Conjecture for some partitions Q having three
185: parts.
186:     This article is a shortening and revision of [23]. We focus here on just the Table Theorem
187:   2
188:       These are called normal patterns in [23] and [46].

193: and Box Conjecture. We also flipped the B/C hooks of the table: this is useful for an article
194: in preparation with M. Boij, in which we will study the equations of loci ZP ⊂ NB , where
195: B = JQ with Q = (u, u − r) and P is a partition in the table T (Q). Here ZP is the Zariski
196: closure in NB of
197:                             {A ∈ NB | A has Jordan type P }.                           (1.2)
198: We believe that the present article and its sequel yield a new approach to viewing the map
199: Q : P → Q(P ). While our methods are elementary, our results suggest that there might be
200: interesting algebraic and geometric explanations and consequences.

203: 2     Preliminaries and Background.
204: 2.1     Notation and Preliminaries.
279: Theorem 2.4. (R. Basili [2]) The partition Q(P ) has rP parts.
280: 
281:     The following result is shown for char k = 0 in [36, Theorem 2.1 and Example 2.5a], and
282: for general infinite k in [4, Theorem 1.12]. We say that a partition P is stable if Q(P ) = P .
283: 
284: Theorem 2.5. A partition is stable if and only if its parts differ pairwise by at least two.
285: 
286:   A partition whose parts differ pairwise by at least two is termed “super distinct” or “Rogers-
287: Ramanujan” in the literature on partitions. T. Košir and P. Oblak showed that Q(P ) is stable.
288: 
289: Theorem 2.6. (T. Košir and P. Oblak) [27]) Suppose that char k = 0 or char k = p > n and
290: k is infinite. Then the partition Q(P ) has parts that differ pairwise by at least two.
291: 
292: Remark 2.7. In [27], Theorem 2.6 is stated for algebraically closed fields. In this somewhat
293: technical remark, we review the Theorem’s proof and explain why this hypothesis can be

1551: 3.4    Completeness of the table T (Q).
1552: In this section we will prove Theorem 3.19, which asserts that T (Q) is all of Q−1 (Q). This
1553: will complete the proof of the Table Theorem 1.1. R. Zhao proved this for u 
1554:  r in [46].
1555: Lemma 3.18. Fix Q = (u, u − r), u > r ≥ 2. All type C partitions P that satisfy Q(P ) = Q
1556: and that are not of type A or B, occur in the table T (Q) of Definition 3.13.
1557: Proof. From Lemma 3.6 we know that
1558:              {PC : C = (c1 , c2 , s1 , s2 ; a) with c1 , c2 , s1 , s2 ∈ Z>0 and a ≥ 4} where
1559:               PC = (ac1 , (a − 1)c2 +s2 , (a − 2)c1 +s1 , (a − 3)c2 )                          (3.35)
1560: is the set of all partitions that are of type C but not of type A or B.
1561:     Let C = (c1 , c2 , s1 , s2 ; a) with c1 , c2 , s1 , s2 ∈ Z>0 and a ≥ 4. Assume that Q(PC ) =
1562: (u, u − r). We need to prove that the partition PC is in the table T (Q). By Lemma 3.6
1563: Q(PC ) = (u, u − r) implies that the following equalities hold:

1966: 4     The Box Conjecture.
1967: We first recall P. Oblak’s Recursive Conjecture for Q(P ) and summarize results about it in
1968: Section 4.1. In Section 4.2 we state a Box Conjecture for Q−1 (Q) which is a generalization of
1969: Theorems 3.12 and 3.19.
1970:     In Section 4.3 we prove the analog of Theorem 3.12 in the special case that Q = (u +
1971: s, u, u − r), with r ≥ 2 and 2 ≤ s ≤ 4.
1972: 
1973: 4.1     Recursive Conjecture for Q(P ).
1974: P. Oblak conjectured a recursive process for determining Q(P ) from P that greatly influenced
1975: further work in the area ([5, 6, 22, 25, 27]).
1976:     Recall from Definition 2.15 that a U-chain Ca of DP is comprised of three parts: first
1977: a chain through all the vertices in the rows of length a, a − 1, corresponding to the almost
1978: rectangular subpartition (ana , (a − 1)na−1 ) of P ; then two chains linking those rows to the
1979: source and to the sink in the top rowPof DP . Recall also that the length |Ca | satisfies equation
1980: (2.3): |Ca | = ana + (a − 1)na−1 + 2 i>a ni .
1981:     Given a partition P of n and an integer a ∈ SP we denote by P 0 (P, a) the unique partition
1982: of (n − |Ca |) obtained by omitting the vertices of the chain Ca from DP and counting the
1983:                                                                                                 0
1984: vertices left in each row. When P = (· · · , ini , · · · ) we have that P 0 (P, a) = (· · · , ini , · · · )
1985: where the multiplicity integers n0i satisfy
2010: 
2011:                                                     36
2012: 
2013: Conjecture 4.3 (Oblak Recursive Conjecture). The map P → Q(P ) satisfies Q(P ) = Ob(P ).
2014:     It follows from Definition 2.10 that the poset DP is independent of char k. Since Ob(P ) is
2015: a combinatorial invariant of DP the Recursive Conjecture implies that Q(P ) is independent
2016: of char k. Also, by definition λ(DP ) ≥ λU (P ) in the Bruhat order.7 A general result due to
2017: E.R. Gansner and M. Saks ([15, 40], see [8, Theorem 6.1]) shows that λ(DP ) ≥ Q(P ). The
2018: first and second author showed:
2019: Theorem 4.4. [22, Theorem 3.9] Let k be an infinite field. Then Q(P ) ≥ λU (P ).
2020:    L. Khatami studied the smallest part of Q(P ) and defined a somewhat subtle combinatorial
2021: invariant µ(P ) [26, Definition 2.6]. Using a study of the antichains of DP she showed
2022: Theorem 4.5. [26, Theorem 4.1] Let P be a partition of n and let k be an infinite field. The
2023: three partitions λ(DP ), λU (P ) and Q(P ) have the same smallest part, which is equal to µ(P ).
2024:    Together with P. Oblak’s Index Theorem 2.8, this implies the following fact we will use in
2025: Section 4.3.
2026: Theorem 4.6 (Oblak conjecture for rP ≤ 3). The Oblak Recursive Conjecture 4.3 is true
2027: over any infinite field k when rP ≤ 3.
2028: Remark 4.7 (Summary of results on the Oblak Recursive Conjecture). Thus, the cases rP = 2
2029: [5, 27, 34, 46] and rP = 3 [26] of the Conjecture have been known since 2008 and 2012,
2030: respectively. Theorem 4.4 of the first and second authors then showed “half” the Conjecture
2031: in all characteristics. Since λ(DP ) ≥ Q(P ) ≥ λU (P ) a proof of the purely combinatorial
2032: statement λ(DP ) = λU (DP ) would show the Oblak conjecture for P in all characteristics. In
2033: contrast, which pairs of Jordan types occur for A, B with [A, B] = 0 depends on char k: see
2034: [5, Example 2.18], [7], and [30, Example 22].
2035: Lemma 4.8. [25, Proposition 2.7] Let P be a partition of n. Then λU (P ) has parts that differ
2036: pairwise by at least two.
2037:    Recall that Q(P ) has parts that differ pairwise by at least two when char k = 0 or
2038: char k = p > n and k is infinite by Theorem 2.6. It follows from Theorems 2.8 and 4.5, and
2039: Lemma 4.8 that Q(P ) has parts that differ pairwise by at least two over any infinite field k
2040: when rP ≤ 3 (since then λ(DP ) = Q(P ) = λU (DP )).
2041:

2077: We now state a conjecture generalizing the Table Theorem (i.e. Theorems 3.12 and 3.19).
2078: Conjecture 4.11 (Box Conjecture). Let Q be a partition having k parts that differ pairwise
2079: by at least two, and assume that the key of Q is SQ = (s1 , s2 , . . . , sk ).
2080: (a) There is an s1 × s2 · · · × sk array (“box”) B(Q) of distinct partitions
2081: 
2082:                                           B(Q) = {Pi1 ,i2 ,...,ik | 1 ≤ iu ≤ su },                (4.5)
2083:                                     P
2084:     such that Pi1 ,i2 ,...,ik has       1≤u≤k iu parts and satisfies Q(Pi1 ,i2 ,...,ik ) = Q.
2085: 
2086: (b) The cardinality |Q−1 (Q)| = 1≤i≤k si . Equivalently, given (a), the set of partitions in
2087:                                  Q
2088:     B(Q) is the complete set Q−1 (Q).
2089: Remark 4.12. In principle Oblak’s Recursive Conjecture 4.3 for the map P → Q(P ) should
2090: allow us to decide the Box Conjecture. We follow this strategy in Section 4.3 to prove part
2091: (a) of the Box Conjecture for certain Q. Of course, a deeper understanding of B(Q) and
2092: Conjecture 4.11 could very well give a new approach to showing Oblak’s Recursive Conjecture.
2093: In [23, Section 5.3] we show that the count of partitions in the box B(Q) is the same as that
2094: for partitions of diagonal hook lengths given by Q: this shows that the Box Conjecture is
2095: consistent with a count of the number of partitions of n. It would be of interest to show that
2096: there is a bijection – preferably explicit – between the set of partitions in Q−1 (Q) and the set
2097: of partitions having diagonal hook lengths Q.
2098: Remark 4.13. Let Q be a partition as in Conjecture 4.11 and B = JQ , the Jordan matrix of
2099: partition Q (notation before Lemma 2.1). Recall from equation (1.2) that by the locus ZP of a
2100: Jordan type P ∈ B(Q) we mean the Zariski closure of the set {A ∈ NB | PA = P } of matrices
2101: commuting with B = JQ and having Jordan type P . Recall that here NB = UB is an affine
2102: space (Proposition 2.9 (b)). Together       Pkwith M. Boij we conjecture that the codimension of the
2103: locus ZP , P = (Pi1 ,i2 ,...,ik ) in NB is ( u=1 iu ) − k, and that the locus is a complete intersection
2104: defined by explicit irreducible equations of degree at most k in the coordinates of NB . In a
2105: sequel in progress joint with M. Boij, we plan to show this conjecture for k = 2. For some
2106: more information about these loci, see [23, §4].

2111: cardinality |Q−1 (Q)|, a fact that was known at least in the case when SQ = (1, 1, . . . , 1, sk ) (see
2112: [35, Theorem 4.1]). Since the cases where k = 3 and s1 , s2 , or s3 is equal to 1 are relatively
2113: easy, we give below several examples of stable partitions Q with three parts for which we have
2114: verified8 Conjecture 4.11 and where no si is equal to 1.

2164: 4.3     Box Conjecture for certain partitions Q with three parts.
2165: In this section we use a method similar to certain steps in the proof of our main Theorem 3.12
2166: to show part (a) of the Box Conjecture– “filling the box” – for a few (infinite) families of
2167: partitions Q with three parts. Recall from Theorem 4.6 and Lemma 4.8 that if rP ≤ 3 then
2168: Q = Q(P ) has parts that differ pairwise by at least two and are obtained from P by the Oblak
2169: recursive process.
2170: Theorem 4.17. Let Q = (u + s, u, u − r) with 2 ≤ s ≤ 4 and 2 ≤ r. There is an array B(Q)
2171: of dimensions (s − 1) × (r − 1) × (u − r) of partitions
2172:                   B(Q) = {Pj,k,` | 1 ≤ j ≤ s − 1, 1 ≤ k ≤ r − 1, 1 ≤ ` ≤ u − r}                   (4.11)
2173: such that Q(Pj,k,` ) = Q and Pj,k,` has j + k + ` parts.
2174:     This theorem covers all keys SQ = (s−1, r −1, u−r) with 1 ≤ s−1 ≤ 3. Like Theorem 3.12
2175: we prove it using Oblak’s recursive process. We note that Theorem 4.17 confirms part (a)
2176: of Conjecture 4.11 for the partitions Q under consideration, but does not show part (b). We
2177: split Theorem 4.17 into three cases, s = 2 in Lemma 4.18, s = 3 in Lemma 4.20, and s = 4 in
2178: Proposition 4.21.
2179: Lemma 4.18. Let Q = (u + 2, u, u − r) with u > r ≥ 2. For k ∈ {1, . . . , r − 1} and
2180: ` ∈ {1, . . . , u − r} put
2181:                                P1,k,` = (u + 2, Pk,` ),
2182: where Pk,` is the partition in T ((u, u − r)) defined in Theorem 3.12. Then Q(P1,k,` ) = Q and
2183: P1,k,` has 1 + k + ` parts for all k ∈ {1, . . . , r − 1} and ` ∈ {1, . . . , u − r}.
2184: Proof. For all Pk,` ∈ T ((u, u − r)), the largest part of Pk,` is at most u so it differs from u + 2
2185: by at least 2. Thus, the only almost rectangular subpartition of P1,k,` that includes u + 2 is
2186: (u + 2) itself. On the other hand, since Pk,` ∈ T ((u, u − r)), we have Q((Pk,` )) = (u, u − r).
2187: Thus, the longest U-chain in the poset of P1,k,` has length u + 2, and can be obtained from the
2385: grant number DMS-1407394.

35:                                               If G is a graph and P a path in G, we say that P is a nonseparating (separating) path if G−P is connected
36:                                           (respectively, disconnected). A path P between u and v is called a uv-path.
37:                                               Tutte [1] proved that, for every 3-connected graph G and vertices u and v, there exists a nonseparating
38:                                           uv-path. In 1975, Lovász [2] made the following conjecture which is related to this result of Tutte.
39:                                           Conjecture 1. For every positive integer k, there exists a positive integer f (k) such that, for every f (k)-
40:                                           connected graph G and vertices u and v, there exists a uv-path P such that G − P is k-connected.
41:                                               It is easy to see that f (1) ≥ 3. So, Tutte’s result implies that f (1) = 3. Chen, Gould, and Yu [3], and
42:                                           independently Kriesell [4], proved that f (2) = 5. Recently, Kawarabayashi, Lee, and Yu [5] proved that
43:                                           f (2) = 4 except for double wheels. The conjecture is open for k ≥ 3.
44:                                               Some related questions have been settled yielding new conjectures. One of them is the following due to
45:                                           Kawarabayashi and Ozeki [6].
46:

64: Conjecture 2. For all positive integers k and `, there exists a positive integer g(k, `) such that the following
65: holds. For every g(k, `)-connected graph G and vertices u and v, there exist internally disjoint uv-paths
66:                              S`
67: P1 , . . . , P` such that G − i=1 Pi is k-connected.
68:     For ` = 1 this conjecture corresponds to Lovász Conjecture. Kawarabayashi and Ozeki showed that
69: g(1, `) = 2` + 1 and g(2, `) ≤ 3` + 2.
70:     Other related question was raised by Hong and Lai [7], who considered the problem of connecting a subset
71: of vertices by a tree instead of just two vertices as in Tutte’s theorem. They made the conjecture below.
72: Conjecture 3. For all positive integers k and r, there exists a positive integer h(k, r) such that, for every
73: h(k, r)-connected graph G and subset X with r vertices, there exists a tree T connecting X such that G − T
74: is k-connected.
75:     Hong and Lai proved that h(1, r) = r + 1 and h(2, r) ≤ 2r + 1.
76:     By Tutte, in a 3-connected graph, every pair of vertices is connected by a nonseparating path. Is it possible
77: to get a spanning tree where all paths are nonseparating? Inspired by Tutte’s result on nonseparating paths,
78: we call Tutte tree any spanning tree of a graph such that every path in the tree is nonseparating. In this
79: paper we deal with the following question.
80: Question 4. When does a graph have a Tutte tree?
81:    For planar graphs we prove the following.
82: Theorem 5. A planar graph has a Tutte tree if and only if it is Hamiltonian or it has a spanning tree whose
83: leaves induce a triangle.
271: articulation vertex of a nontrivial block of G has degree two in the nontrivial block.
272: Proof. (⇒) (i) If a nontrivial block is not a leaf in the block tree, then it has at least two articulation vertices,
273: say u and v. Since it is 2-connected, it contains a cycle with u and v. It is known that the fundamental
274: cycles form a basis of the cycle space [10, Theorem 1.9.5]. It follows that any spanning tree of G has a
275: fundamental cycle containing u inside the block. Such a cycle separates the blocks attached to u from the
276: blocks attached to v. Thus every nontrivial block must be a leaf in the block tree of G.
277:    (ii) Let u be an articulation point of a nontrivial block of G. Then u is contained in some fundamental
278: cycle on the tree in this block. If such a cycle does not contain all vertices in the block, then it separates
279: the blocks attached to u from the rest of the block. Hence this fundamental cycle containing u also contains
280: 
281: 
282:                                                          6
283: 
284: all vertices in the block. It follows that the block is Hamiltonian and the fundamental Tutte tree restricted
285: to the block is a Hamiltonian path of the block.
305: fundamental cycle in two {u, v}-bridges contains it and the remaining two {u, v}-bridges are nontrivial.)
306: Thus, every fundamental cycle of T is completely contained in a {u, v}-bridge. Therefore, the fundamental
307: cycles of T do not generate the cycles containing edges of two {u, v}-bridges. This contradicts the fact that
308: the fundamental cycles of a spanning tree form a basis for the cycle space [10, Theorem 1.9.5]. Hence such
309: a tree T cannot exist.
310: Corollary 19. Let G be a 2-connected graph and {u, v} be a 2-vertex cut. If G has a fundamental Tutte
311: tree, then every {u, v}-bridge has a Hamiltonian uv-path, except possibly one. Moreover, if there are exactly
312: three {u, v}-bridges, then every {u, v}-bridge has a Hamiltonian uv-path.
313: Proof. Let T be a fundamental Tutte tree in G. Since the fundamental cycles of T induce a basis of the
314: cycle space, there exists at least one fundamental cycle contained in the union of two {u, v}-bridges, B1 and
315: B2 , say C12 . If there are exactly two {u, v}-bridges, then C12 must contain all vertices of at least one of B1
316: or B2 , and the corollary follows. If there are exactly three {u, v}-bridges, let B3 be the other one. Similarly,
317: there exist fundamental cycles Cij contained in the union of Bi and Bj . It follows that {Cij } induces a
318: Hamiltonian uv-path in each {u, v}-bridge.
319:     A series extension of a graph is the subdivision of an edge; a parallel extension is the addition of a new
363: of G without all the leaves in the same face. Let u and v be two leaves in different faces of D. Then there
364: exist a cycle C that separates u from v [12, Theorem 3.1]; that is, a cycle containing neither u nor v and
365: such that they are in distinct regions of the Jordan curve D[C]. Since the fundamental cycles form a basis of
366: the cycle space [10, Theorem 1.9.5], it follows that there exists a fundamental cycle Ce of T that separates u
367: from v. Because G is planar, Ce is a separating fundamental cycle, and so we have a contradiction.
368: Corollary 21. There are infinitely many planar 3-connected graphs with no fundamental Tutte tree.
369: Proof. Let n ≥ 5 be an integer and Gn be as defined in the proof of Lemma 13. It follows that every spanning
370: tree of Gn has at least two leaves in different faces and therefore Gn has no fundamental Tutte tree.
371:    If a graph has a Tutte tree, then such tree is also a fundamental Tutte tree. However, there exist (planar)
372: 3-connected graphs with a fundamental Tutte tree, but without a Tutte tree, as for instance the Herschel
373: graph (Figure 5), which is nonhamiltonian and has no triangles.
374:    In the case of planar graphs, traceability is a necessary condition (Corollary 11) but not sufficient (Herschel
375: graph) for the existence of a Tutte tree. On the other hand, there are nontraceable (and thus nonhamiltonian)
376: cubic planar 3-connected graphs [13] with a fundamental Tutte tree, e.g., the Zamfirescu graph (Figure 6).

13:                                                  Abstract. This paper is motivated by the following question in sieve theory. Given a
14:                                                  subset X ⊂ [N ] and α ∈ (0, 1/2). Suppose that |X (mod p)| ≤ (α + o(1))p for every prime
15:                                                  p. How large can X be? On the one hand, we have the bound |X| ≪α N α from Gallagher’s
16:                                                  larger sieve. On the other hand, we prove, assuming the truth of an inverse sieve conjecture,
17:                                                                                                                          2014
18:                                                  that the bound above can be improved (for example, to |X| ≪α N O(α ) for small α). The
19:                                                  result follows from studying the average size of |X (mod p)| as p varies, when X = f (Z)∩[N ]
20:                                                  is the value set of a polynomial f (x) ∈ Z[x].
21: 
22: 
23:                                                                                     1. Introduction
24:                                             For a positive integer N, denote by [N] the set {1, 2, · · · , N}. The letter p is always used
25:                                           to denote a prime. To primary goal of this paper is to study upper bounds for the sizes
26:                                           of subsets X ⊂ [N] occupying a small fraction of residue classes modulo many primes p.
27:                                           Gallagher’s larger sieve [12] provides such an upper bound.
28:                                           Theorem 1.1 (Larger sieve). Let X ⊂ [N] be a subset and P be a set of primes. We have
29:                                                                                  P
30:                                                                                    p∈P log p
31:                                                                |A| ≤ P                                   ,
32:                                                                         p∈P |X  (mod p)|−1 log p − log N
33:                                           whenever the denominator is positive.
34:                                              See [6] for some variants of it and references therein for applications. We are particularly
35:                                           interested in the situation when |X (mod p)| ≤ αp for some fixed α ∈ (0, 1), and whether
36:                                           the bound provided by the larger sieve is best possible.
37:                                           Corollary 1.2 (Larger sieve, special case). Let X ⊂ [N] be a subset and α ∈ (0, 1/2]. If |X
38:                                           (mod p)| ≤ (α + o(1))p for every prime p, then |X| ≪ N α+o(1) .
39:                                              This is easily deduced from Theorem 1.1 by taking P to be the set of primes up to N α+o(1) .
40:                                           When α > 1/2, the statement still holds, but is beaten by the bound |X| ≪α N 1/2 following
41:                                           from the large sieve [21]. When α ≤ 1/2, is the bound |X| ≪ N α+o(1) sharp? If X is the set
42:                                           of perfect squares up to N, then |X| ∼ N 1/2 and X occupies (p + 1)/2 residue classes (the
43:                                           quadratic residues) modulo any odd prime p. The question of whether this is the only type
44:                                           of sharp example is usually referred to as the inverse sieve conjecture, informally stated as
45:                                           follows.
46:                                                                                               1

50: Conjecture 1.3 (Inverse sieve conjecture, rough form). Let X ⊂ [N] be a subset. If |X
51: (mod p)| ≤ 0.9p for every prime p, then either one of the following two statements holds:
52:     (1) the cardinality of X is extremely small;
53:     (2) the set X possesses algebraic structure.
54:    See Conjecture 4.1 below for one precise formulation of it. See also [7, 14, 17, 24] for more
55: discussions and evidences towards it.
56:    Now assume that α < 1/2 is fixed. Motivated by the inverse sieve conjecture, we consider
57: the sizes of X (mod p) when X is the value set of a polynomial. For a polynomial f (x) ∈ Z[x]
58: of degree d ≥ 1, denote by fp ∈ Fp [x] the reduction f (mod p). Let αp (f ) = p−1 |fp (Fp )|,
59: the relative size of the value set of f (mod p). Define α(f ) to be the average of αp (f ) as p

76: Corollary 1.2 is not sharp whenever α is smaller than (and bounded away from) 1/2. See
77: the last section in [23] for a preliminary discussion on the simplest case d = 3.

80: |X (mod p)| ≤ (α + o(1))p for every prime p, then |X| ≪α,ǫ N 1/d where d is the smallest
81: positive integer with τ (d) ≥ (1 − ǫ)α−1 .

116:    Since deg fn = 2n , we have α(fn ) ≪ (log(deg fn ))−1 . We do not know whether this is the
117: best example or whether the bound for α(f ) in Theorem 1.4 is sharp. See Section 6.2 below
118: for more discussions on this.
119:    The investigation of αp (f ) for a fixed prime p has a long history (see [3, 5]), and explicit
120: formulae for αp (f ) are known in terms of the proportion of fixed point free elements in a
121: certain Galois group (see Lemma 5.1 below and the remark afterwards). Not surprisingly, the
122: quantity α(f ) can also be evaluated in terms of a certain Galois group, and this is recorded
123: in Proposition 6.1 below. Due to a lack of understanding of the relevant Galois groups, our
124: lower bound for α(f ) is instead obtained by studying the number of solutions to f (x) ≡ f (y)
125: (mod p) on average as p varies (see Section 2), and it is for this reason that the average of
126: αp (f )−1 naturally shows up.
127:    A related line of work is on classifying those polynomials f ∈ Fp [x] for which αp (f )
128: is close to the lower bound d−1 (for a fixed p). In particular, results in [13] imply that
129: αp (f ) ≥ 2d−1 + o(1) whenever p 6≡ ±1 (mod d).
130:    The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we state a general and quanti-
131: tative version of Theorem 1.4 for polynomials over arbitrary number fields, and outline the
132: proof strategy, with the details given in Section 3. In Section 4 we state a precise form of
133: the inverse sieve conjecture and deduce Theorem 1.5. In Section 5, Theorem 1.6 is proved
134: by computing relevant Galois groups. Finally in Section 6, we make some further remarks
135: concerning the larger sieve as well as the quantity α(f ).
136:    Acknowlegements. Thanks to Brian Conrad for help with proofs, to Kannan
137: Soundararajan for helpful discussions, and to Akshay Venkatesh for asking a question that
138: leads to this paper.

174: homogeneous part of degree d of f (x) − f (y) is a(xd − y d ) for some nonzero a and it factors
