419:             4. Inverse sieve conjecture implies improved larger sieve
420:   In this section we state a precise version of the inverse sieve conjecture and then prove
421: Theorem 1.5. The implied constants here are always allowed to depend on α, ǫ.
422: Conjecture 4.1 (Inverse sieve conjecture). Let X ⊂ [N] be a subset and let ǫ > 0 be real.
423: Assume that for each parameter Q ≥ N ǫ , we have
424:                               X |X      (mod p)|
425:                                                  ≤ (1 − ǫ)π(Q).
426:                               p≤Q
427:                                          p
428: 
429: Then at least one of the following two situations happens:
430:      (1) (very small size) |X| ≪ǫ N ǫ ;
431:      (2) (algebraic structure) there exists a polynomial f (x) ∈ Q[x] of degree d ∈ [2, C] and
432:          height at most N C such that |X ∩ f ([N])| ≥ C −1 |X|, where C = C(ǫ) is a constant.
433:   Here, we say that a polynomial f (x) ∈ Q[x] has height at most H if f (x) = A−1 f ∗ (x) for
436: 2.1, in that h(f ) is invariant under scalar multiplication but the notion here is not. Note
437: that if a polynomial f (x) ∈ Q[x] has height at most H, then h(f ) ≪ log H.
438: Remark 4.2. We make a few remarks concerning why some quantitative aspects of this
439: conjecture are reasonable.
440:       • The condition on X essentially says that X misses a positive proportion of residue
441:         classes modulo primes p on average, as soon as p exceeds a small positive power of
442:         N. With this assumption we know from the large sieve that |X| ≪ N 1/2 and from
443:         the larger sieve that |X| ≪ N α+O(ǫ) if the upper bound (1 − ǫ)π(Q) is replaced by
444:         απ(Q). Without the knowledge about X (mod p) for p ≤ N ǫ , one can essentially
445:         add to X any N ǫ extra elements without violating the assumption, but one should
446:         still expect to see algebraic structure apart from these extra elements.
447:       • The conclusion |X ∩ f ([N])| ≥ C −1 |X| is equivalent to the seemingly weaker one
448:         |X ∩ f (Q)| ≥ C −1 |X|, after a suitable modification of the polynomial f which does
449:         not increase its height too much. To see that the interval [N] can be replaced by Z,
450:         note that the set J = {n ∈ Z : 1 ≤ f (n) ≤ N} is the union of at most d intervals
459:

462:    If |X (mod p)| ≤ αp for small α, repeated applications of Conjecture 4.1 allows us to
463: strengthen it by requiring the degree d to be fairly large.

465: Proposition 4.3 (Inverse sieve conjecture in the larger sieve regime). Assume the truth of
466: Conjecture 4.1. Let X ⊂ [N] be a subset. Let α ∈ (0, 1) and ǫ ∈ (0, α) be real. Assume
467: that |X (mod p)| ≤ (α + o(1))p for each prime p. Then at least one of the following two
468: situations happens:
469:    (1) (very small size) |X| ≪ǫ N ǫ ;
470:    (2) (algebraic structure) there exists a polynomial f (x) ∈ Q[x] of degree d ∈ [2, C] and
471:        height at most N C such that |X ∩ f (Z)| ≥ C −1 |X|, where C = C(ǫ) is a constant.
472:        Moreover, we may ensure that τ (d) ≥ (1 − ǫ)α−1 .
473: 
474: Proof. Suppose that |X| ≫ N ǫ . We will apply Conjecture 4.1 iteratively to construct a
475: sequence of polynomials f1 , f2 , · · · , fk and a sequence of sets X0 = X, X1 , X2 , · · · , Xk with
476: k = O(1) such that the following conditions hold:
477:    (1) deg fi = di ∈ [2, C], and τ (d1 d2 · · · dk ) ≥ (1 − ǫ)α−1 ;
478:    (2) the height of fi is at most N O(1) for each 1 ≤ i ≤ k;
479:    (3) Xi ⊂ [N] and |Xi | ≫ |Xi−1 | for each 1 ≤ i ≤ k;
480:    (4) fi (Xi ) ⊂ Xi−1 for each 1 ≤ i ≤ k.
481:   Suppose first that these objects are constructed. Let f = f1 ◦ f2 ◦ · · · ◦ fk . By property (1),
482: the degree d of f is O(1) and satisfies τ (d) ≥ (1 − ǫ)α−1 . By property (2), the height of f is
483: N O(1) . By property (3), we have |Xk | ≫ |X|. By property (4), we have f (Xk ) ⊂ X ∩ f ([N]).
484: Hence

539:                  5. Polynomials with small value sets modulo primes
540:   In this section we prove Theorem 1.6. First we state a result connecting the quantity
541: αp (f ) to a Galois group. For a polynomial f (x) ∈ Fp [x] of degree d, denote by Rf the set of
542: roots in Fp (t) of the polynomial f (x) − t. Define
543: 
544:                        Gf = Gal(Fp (Rf )/Fp (t)), G∗f = Gal(Fp (Rf )/Fp (t)).
545: In other words, Gf and G∗f are the Galois groups of the splitting field of f (x) − t over Fp (t)
546: and Fp (t), respectively. It is easy to see that G∗f is a normal subgroup of Gf with Gf /G∗f
547: cyclic. In fact, Gf /G∗f is isomorphic to Gal(Fp (Rf ) ∩ Fp /Fp ). For any subset Ξ ⊂ Gf , we
548: use α(Ξ) to denote the proportion of elements in Ξ with at least one fixed point, under the
549: natural action on Rf .
550: 
551:                                 SHARPNESS OF THE LARGER SIEVE                                    13
562: version of Chebotarev density theorem and related equidistribution results play an important
563: role in proving function field analogues of certain classical analytic number theory conjec-
564: tures [1, 2, 10].
565: Proof of Theorem 1.6. Recall that the sequence of polynomials {fn } is defined by
566:                               f1 (x) = x2 , fn+1 (x) = (fn (x) + 1)2 .

629: 
630:                                   6. Further remarks
631: 6.1. More on the sharpness of Gallagher’s larger sieve. We point out here that Gal-
632: lagher’s larger sieve in its general form as stated in Theorem 1.1 has the optimal bound.
633: Indeed, if we take A ⊂ [N] to be any subset with cardinality Q and take P be the set of all
634: primes between Q and N, the general form of the larger sieve gives the sharp bound |A| ≪ Q,
635: because the numerator is about N and the denominator is about N/Q. This shows that any
636: potential improvement to Corollary 1.2 must incorporate the ill distribution modulo many
637: small primes.
638:    Under the assumption of Corollary 1.2, one may go over the argument in the proof of the
639: larger sieve to find out what happens if |A| is close to N α . Indeed, in the typical proof of
640: Gallagher’s larger sieve, one uses the upper and lower bounds
641:                       |X|2          X X
642: (6.1)                      log Q ≤             log p ≤ |X|2 log N + |X|Q,
643:                        α
669: where Φℓ is the cyclotomic polynomial of degree φ(ℓ), we cannot expect (6.2) to be true for
670: n = ad − bd . However, it is still reasonable to expect that each factor Φℓ (a, b) satisfies (6.2).
671: If so, then we obtain an upper bound in (6.1) with log N there replaced by τ (d) log Q, which
672: in turn implies that τ (d) ≥ α−1 . This is consistent with the conclusion of Theorem 1.5.
673:    On the other hand, making this heuristic rigorous could be extremely hard. For example,
674: it is an open problem to obtain a bound better than |X| ≪ N 1/2 for X ⊂ [N] with all
675: nonzero differences x − x′ (x, x′ ∈ X) N κ -smooth, where κ > 0 is sufficiently small (see [9]).
676:    We also point out that there are versions of Gallagher’s larger sieve over arbitrary number
677: field [8, 26]. One can ask similar questions about its sharpness in this general setting, and
678: use Theorem 2.1 to formulate an improved larger sieve conjecture. We will not do so here
679: since the case over Z is already quite interesting.
680: 
681: 6.2. Computing α(f ) via Galois groups. The main result of this paper computes the
682: average of mp (f ) as p varies, as a consequence of Chebotarev density theorem. It is natural
683: to ask if one can compute α(f ), the average of αp (f ) as p varies, directly, especially since we
684: do have such a formula for each individual αp (f ) as in Lemma 5.1.
685: Proposition 6.1. Let K be a number field and f (x) ∈ OK [x] be a monic polynomial of
686: degree d. Let G = Gal(K(R)/K(t)), where R is the set of roots of f (x) − t. Let α(G) be
687: the proportion of elements in G with at least one fixed point, under the natural action on R.
688: Then
689:                                      1     X

17:                                                  can be equitably partitioned into k induced forests.
18:                                                     Each of these results implies the existence of a constant c such that
19:                                                  for any k ⩾ c, any planar graph has an equitable partition into k induced
20:                                                  forests. This was conjectured by Wu, Zhang, and Li in 2013.
21: 
22: 
23: 
24:                                             An equitable partition of a graph G is a partition of the vertex-set of
25:                                          G such that the sizes of any two parts differ by at most one. Hajnal and
26:                                          Szemerédi [4] proved the following result, which was conjectured by Erdős
27:                                          (see also [5] for a shorter proof).
28:                                          Theorem 1. For any integers ∆ and k ⩾ ∆ + 1, any graph with maximum
29:                                          degree ∆ has an equitable partition into k stable sets.
30:                                             Note that there is no constant c, such that for any k ⩾ c, any star can
31:                                          be equitably partitioned into k stable sets. Wu, Zhang, and Li made the
32:                                          following two conjectures [9].
33:                                          Conjecture 2. There is a constant c such that for any k ⩾ c, any planar
34:                                          graph can be equitably partitioned into k induced forests.
35:                                          Conjecture 3. For any integers ∆ and k ⩾ ⌈ ∆+1  2 ⌉, any graph of maximum
36:                                          degree ∆ can be equitably partitioned into k induced forests.
37:                                             A proper coloring of a graph G is acyclic if any cycle of G contains at
38:                                          least 3 colors. We first prove the following result.
39:                                          Theorem 4. Let k ⩾ 2. If a graph G has an acyclic coloring with at most
40:                                          k colors, then G can be equitably partitioned into k − 1 induced forests.
41:                                          Proof. The proof proceeds by induction on k ⩾ 2. If k = 2 then G itself
42:                                          is a forest and the result trivially holds, so we can assume that k ⩾ 3. Let
43:                                          V1 , . . . , Vk be the color classes in some acyclic k-coloring of G (note that some
44:                                          sets Vi might be empty). Let n be the number of vertices of G. Without
45:                                                                                                                   n
46:                                          loss of generality, we can assume that V1 contains at most nk ⩽ k−1          vertices.
63: and therefore G has an equitable partition into k − 1 induced forests.        
64:   It was proved by Borodin [2] that any planar graph has an acyclic coloring
65: with at most 5 colors. Therefore, Theorem 9 implies Corollary 5, which is a
66: positive answer to Conjecture 2.
67: Corollary 5. For any k ⩾ 4, any planar graph can be equitably partitioned
68: into k induced forests.
69:    We now prove stronger results in two different ways. We first show the
70: induced forests can be chosen to be very specific. We then show that graphs
71: from a class that is much wider than the class of planar graphs can also be
72: equitably partitioned into constantly many induced forests.
73:    A star coloring of a graph G is a proper coloring of the vertices of G such
74: that any two color classes induce a star forest. Using the same proof as that
75: of Theorem 4, it easy to show the following result.
76: Theorem 6. Let k ⩾ 2. If a graph G has a star coloring with at most k
77: colors, then G can be equitably partitioned into k − 1 induced star forests.
111: Theorem 9. For any integers d ⩾ 1 and k ⩾ 3d−1 , any d-degenerate graph
112: can be equitably partitioned into k induced forests.
113:    It follows from Euler’s formula that every planar graph is 5-degenerate.
114: Therefore, Theorem 9 also implies Conjecture 2 (with c = 81 instead of c = 4
115: in Corollary 5). For a graph G, let χa (G) denote the least integer k such that
116: G has an acyclic coloring with k colors. It is known that there is a function
117: f such that every graph G is f (χa (G))-degenerate [3]. However there exist
118: families of 2-degenerate graphs with unbounded acyclic chromatic number.
119: It follows that Theorem 9 can be applied to wider classes of graphs than
120: Theorems 4, 6, and 8.
121:    A class of graphs is hereditary if it is closed under taking induced sub-
122: graphs.
123: Lemma 10. Let ℓ be an integer and C be a hereditary class of graphs such
124: that every graph in C can be equitably partitioned into ℓ induced forests. Then
125: for any k ⩾ ℓ, any graph in C can be equitably partitioned into k induced
156: 
157: Open problems. It remains to determine whether every planar graph has
158: an equitable partition into three induced forests. By Theorems 4 and 9, a
159: possible counterexample must have acyclic chromatic number equal to 5 and
160: cannot be 2-degenerate.
161:   It was proved by Poh [7] that every planar graph has a partition into three
162: induced linear forests (i.e. graphs in which each connected component is a
163: path). A natural question is the following.
164: Question 12. Is there a constant c such that for any k ⩾ c, any planar
165: graph has an equitable partition into k induced linear forests?
166:

52: hcf (H) as follows. When χ(H) 6= 2 and hcfχ (H) = 1, we say hcf (H) = 1. If χ(H) = 2, we say

55: Theorem 1.1 ([15]). For every graph H on h vertices, there exist integers C and m0 such that for
56: all integers m ≥ m0 , if G is a graph on n = mh vertices then the following holds. If
57:                                   
58:                                     (1 − 1/χcr (H))n + C if hcf (H) = 1
59:                           δ(G) ≥
60:                                       (1 − 1/χ(H))n + C otherwise,
61: 
62: then G contains an H-factor.
63: 
64:    It was also shown in [15] that Theorem 1.1 is best possible up to the constant C. Other results
65: and methods for tiling problems can be found in a recent survey of Kühn and Osthus [16].
66: 
67:      Rather than working with an arbitrary graph G, one may restrict G to be r-partite and tile it
68: with some r-partite graph H. Although it sounds like a special case, multipartite tiling is stronger
69: than general tiling in the following sense. First, a result on multipartite tiling does not follow
70: from the corresponding general result. For example, an arbitrary graph G of order n contains a
71: perfect matching if δ(G) ≥ n/2 (Dirac [6]), while a bipartite graph B with two partition sets of size
72: n/2 contains a perfect matching if δ(B) ≥ n/4 (König-Hall [9]). Second, a result on multipartite
73: tiling often implies one for general tiling. For example, suppose we know that every bipartite graph
74: with two partition sets of size n/2 and minimum degree at least n/4 contains a perfect matching
75: (assumed that n is even). Let G be an arbitrary graph G with δ(G) ≥ n/2 + ǫn for some ǫ > 0.
82: DeBasio [5] later determined the minimum degree threshold for a Ks,t -factor). Given any bipartite
83: H of order h, since Kh,h contains an H-factor, this gives a sufficient condition for an H-factor.
84: 
85: Theorem 1.2 ([22]). Let H be a bipartite graph of order h. Suppose that n is suﬃciently large and
86: divisible by h. If G is a balanced bipartite graph on 2n vertices such that δ(G) ≥ n2 + 3h
87:                                                                                          2 − 2, then
88: G contains an H-factor.
89: 
90:    We first show that Theorem 1.2 is best possible (up to an additive constant) when hcf (H) 6= 1.
91:

95:   1. If hcf (H) 6= 1, then there exists a G such that δ(G) = ⌈ n2 ⌉ − 1 and G does not contain an
96:      H-factor.

98:   2. If hcf (H) = 1, then there exists a G such that
99:                                                            

110:     Zhao [22] asked about the minimum degree threshold for H-factors in bipartite graphs and
111: suggested using either χ(H)(= 2) or χcr (H), where the indicator function hcf (H) determines
112: which one is relevant. The main result of this paper answers this affirmatively; it can be viewed as
113: a bipartite analog of Theorem 1.1.
114: Theorem 1.4. Let H be a bipartite graph on h vertices such that hcf (H) = 1. If G is a balanced
115: bipartite graph on 2n = mh vertices, then there exist positive integers m0 and c1 (H) ≤ 4h3 such
116: that if m ≥ m0 and                                  
117:                                                 1
118:                                δ(G) ≥ 1 −              n + c1 (H)
119:                                              χcr (H)
120: then G contains an H-factor.
121:      Proposition 1.3, Part 2, shows that Theorem 1.4 is best possible up to the value of c1 (H). Our
122: constant c1 (H) is on the order of h3 , and its exact value is specified in (8) of Theorem 4.9. Unlike the
123: constant C in Theorem 1.1 which depends on the Regularity Lemma, our c1 (H) is comparatively
124: small. Nevertheless, we are unable to determine the best possible value of c1 (H) as in [22].
125:      Zhao [22] also asked for the minimum degree threshold for an almost perfect H-tiling. Komlós
126: [11] showed that for any graph H, every graph G with n vertices and δ(G) ≥ (1−1/χcr (H))n contains
127: an H-tiling that covers all but at most o(n) vertices. Shokoufandeh and Zhao [20] improved o(n) to
128: a constant, O(h2 ), where h is the order of H. In this paper we prove a similar result for bipartite
129: tiling.
130: Theorem 1.5. Let H be a bipartite graph of order h. There exist integers n0 and c2 (H) < 8h2
131: such that every bipartite graph G with n ≥ n0 vertices in each partition set contains an H-tiling
132: that covers all but at most c2 (H) vertices if δ(G) ≥ (1 − 1/χcr (H))n.
133:     It is important to note that Kühn and Osthus [15] started their proof of Theorem 1.1 with the
134: result of Komlós (or the one of Shokoufandeh and Zhao), which gives an almost tiling of G, and then
135: modified it into a perfect tiling under the assumption that hcf (H) = 1. While proving Theorem 1.4,
136: we first find an almost-tiling (which leaves o(n) vertices uncovered) from scratch. If hcf (H) = 1,
137: then we modify it into a perfect H-tiling, otherwise we modify it into an H-tiling that leaves only
138: O(h2 ) vertices uncovered.
139:     The structure of the paper is as follows. We prove Proposition 1.3 in Section 2. In Section 3, we
140: lay some groundwork for our proofs: we state bipartite versions of the Regularity Lemma and Blow-
141: up Lemma. Section 4 provides the proof of Theorem 1.4, which is divided into the nonextremal case
142: and the extremal case. Section 5 gives the proof of Theorem 1.5 based on the one of Theorem 1.4.
143: In the last section we give concluding remarks, including a conjecture on r-partite tiling.
144:      Notation. Fix a graph. For two vertices x, y, we write x ∼ y if x is adjacent to y. Let Γ(x)
145: denote the set of neighbors of x and d(x) = |Γ(x)|. For a vertex set S, let Γ(x, S) = Γ(x) ∩ S. A
146: bipartite graph G[X, Y ] means a bipartite graph with partition sets X and Y . Given two disjoint
147: subsets A, B of V (G), G[A, B] is the bipartite subgraph induced on A ∪ B and its size is denoted
148: by e(A, B). The density of A and B is the ratio d(A, B) = e(A, B)/(|A| · |B|). When A = {x}, we
149: simply write d(x, B) instead of d({x}, B). Note that d({x}, B) is a density instead of a degree.
150:      Throughout this paper we assume that H is a bipartite graph on h vertices such that σ(H) = u
151: and h − σ(H) = w. Let C1 , . . . , Ckc be its connected components. Then each component Ci has a
152: unique 2-coloring {Ui , Wi } with |Wi | ≥ |Ui |. Let ci = |Ci | = |Wi | + |Ui | and di = |Wi | − |Ui |. Recall
153: that hcfc (H) = hcf (c1 , . . . , ckc ). We now define
154:                                                    P      hcfχ,c (H) as hcf (d1 , . . . , dkc ). When hcfc (H) = 1,
359: m(w − u) ≥ (w − u)rβ + t, which gives (i).

1222: vertices because at most 6h copies of H in H may contain the vertices of G′ − G, and each copy of
1223: H may contain at most h − 1 vertices of G.

1271: Proof of Theorem 1.5. First note what is different here from Theorem 1.4: (1). we do not assume
1272: that hcf (H) = 1; (2) the δ(G) condition has no extra constant c1 (H); (3) at most c2 (H) vertices
1273: may be left outside the desired H-tiling. Below we closely follow the proof of Theorem 1.4 but focus
1274: on the impact of these differences.
1275:     Non-extremal Case: We assume G is not in the extremal case, which is defined exactly as
1276: in Theorem 1.4. First note that Theorem 4.1 has no c1 (H) in the minimum degree condition, and
1277: Lemma 4.3 does not assume that hcf (H) = 1. We thus apply Lemma 4.3 to get a decomposition of
1278: G into super-regular cluster pairs (P1 , Q1 ), . . . , (Pk , Qk ), and exceptional sets X0 , Y0 . We can not
1279: apply Lemma 4.4 directly because it assumes that hcf (H) = 1. If we follow the proof of Lemma 4.4,
1280: we can apply Claim 4.7 to get rid of the exceptional sets but we can not use Claim 4.8 because we
1281: do not have hcfc (H) = 1. Actually even if h divides |Pi | + |Qi |, we can not use Corollary 3.7 to
1282: obtain an H-factor on Pi ∪ Qi because we do not have hcfχ,c (H) = 1. Instead we can only apply
1283: Lemma 5.1 to obtain an H-tiling that omits at most h + (w − u) − 2 vertices of Pi ∪ Qi . If we apply
1284: Lemma 5.1 to each (Pi , Qi ), then we obtain an H-tiling of G that omits at most 2hk vertices, where
1285: k ≤ 2pM (ǫ) is a large constant depending on the large constant M (ǫ) defined in the Regularity
1286: Lemma.

1416: 
1417:    As explained before, Theorem 1.4 implies an approximate version of Theorem 1.1 for bipartite
1418: H, in which the constant C is replaced by o(n). In fact, if the following conjecture of Bollobás and
1419: Scott [3] is true, we can even get Theorem 1.1 exactly.
1420: 
1421: Conjecture 6.1 ([3]). If G is a graph of even order, then G contains a spanning, balanced bipartite
1422: subgraph B such that for every vertex v in G, dB (v) ≥ dG2(v) − 12 .
1423: 
1424:     In fact, for this purpose, it suffices to have a weaker form of Conjecture 6.1: every graph G
1425: contains a spanning, balanced, bipartite subgraph B such that δ(B) ≥ δ(G)   2 − c, where c is some
1426: absolute constant.
1427:     After seeing the similarity between Theorem 1.1 and Theorems 1.4, it is reasonable to expect
1428: such a result for r-partite tiling. In an r-partite graph G, we define the pairwise minimum degree
1429: δ̄(G) as the minimum degree from a vertex in one partition set to any other partition set.
1430: 
1431: Conjecture 6.2. Let H be a graph with order h and chromatic number r. There exist integers C
1432: and m0 such that for all m ≥ m0 , if G is a balanced r-partite graph with n = mh vertices in each
1433: partition set such that
1434:                                 
1435:                                   (1 − 1/χcr (H))n + C if hcf (H) = 1
1436:                         δ̄(G) ≥
1437:                                     (1 − 1/χ(H))n + C otherwise,
1438: 
1439: then G contains an H-factor.
1440: 
1441:     At present Conjecture 6.2 is out of reach as it has not been confirmed for H = Kr with r > 4.
1442: In other words, we do not have the multipartite version of the Hajnal-Szemerédi theorem. This
1443: problem was studied by Fischer [7], who obtained an almost perfect tiling for the case of K3 and
1444: K4 . Magyar and Martin [17] proved Conjecture 6.2 for K3 with C = 1; Martin and Szemerédi [18]
1445: proved Conjecture 6.2 for K4 with C = 0. Csaba and Mydlarz [4] recently proved an approximate
1446: version of Conjecture 6.2 for H = Kr in which they assume δ̄(G) ≥ krk+1r
1447:                                                                          n, where kr = r + O(log r).
1448: Furthermore, Martin and Zhao [19] proved Conjecture 6.2 for all complete tripartite graphs Ks,s,s .
1449: Given the success on the tiling of K3 and K4 , it may not be very hard to prove Conjecture 6.2 for
1450: all 3-chromatic or 4-chromatic H.

96: Then we apply a linear transformation of variables and obtain an ideal generated

98: G. We call these ideals, which are of interest for their own sake, permanental edge
99: ideals. Permanental ideals have first been studied by Laubenbacher and Swanson in
100: [6].

143:    For weakly star-shaped polygons, Cabello et al. [6] showed that the above example is
144: essentially optimal, providing the following linear upper bound on b(S).
145: Theorem 1.1 ([6, Theorem 5]). For every weakly star-shaped simple polygon P , we have
146: b(P ) ⩽ 18 c(P ).
147:   For polygons that are not weakly star-shaped, Cabello et al. [6] gave a superlinear bound.
148: Theorem 1.2 ([6, Theorem 6]). Every simple polygon P satisfies
149:                                                          
150:                                                       1
151:                           b(P ) ⩽ 12 c(P ) 1 + log2         .
152:                                                     c(P )
153:     Moreover, Cabello et al. [6] conjectured that even for a general simple polygon P , b(P )
154: can be bounded from above by a linear function of c(P ). The next theorem, which is the
155: first main result of this paper, confirms this conjecture. Recall that b(S) is defined for a set
156: S if and only if S has finite positive measure and Seg(S) is measurable. Recall also that a
157: set is p-componentwise simply connected if its polygonally-connected components are simply
158: connected. In particular, every simply connected set is p-componentwise simply connected.
159: Theorem 1.3. Every p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined
160: satisfies b(S) ⩽ 180 c(S).
161:    It is clear that every simple polygon satisfies the assumptions of Theorem 1.3, hence we
162: directly obtain the following, which confirms the conjecture of Cabello et al. [6].
163: Corollary 1.4. Every simple polygon P ⊆ R2 satisfies b(P ) ⩽ 180 c(P ).
164:    The main restriction in Theorem 1.3 is the assumption that S is p-componentwise simply
165: connected. This assumption cannot be omitted, as shown by the set S = [0, 1]2 r Q2 , where it
166: is easy to verify that c(S) = 0 and b(S) = 1. See Example 3.1 in Section 3.
167:    A related construction shows that Theorem 1.3 fails in higher dimensions. To see this,
168: consider again the set S = [0, 1]2 r Q2 , and define a set S 0 ⊆ R3 by
169:                             S 0 := {(tx, ty, t) : t ∈ [0, 1] and (x, y) ∈ S}.
170: 
171: 4                  MARTIN BALKO, VÍT JELÍNEK, PAVEL VALTR, AND BARTOSZ WALCZAK
172: 
173: 
174: Again, it is easy to verify that c(S 0 ) = 0 and b(S 0 ) = 1, although S 0 is simply connected, even
175: star-shaped.
176:     Despite these examples, we will show that meaningful analogues of Theorem 1.3 for higher
177: dimensions and for sets that are not p-componentwise simply connected are possible. The key
178: is to use higher-order generalizations of the Beer index, which we introduce now.
179:     For a set S ⊆ Rd , we define the set Simpk (S) ⊆ (Rd )k+1 by
180:                     Simpk (S) := {(A0 , . . . , Ak ) ∈ S k+1 : Conv({A0 , . . . , Ak }) ⊆ S},
181: where the operator Conv denotes the convex hull of a set of points. We call Simpk (S) the
182: k-simplex set of S. Note that Simp1 (S) = Seg(S).
183:    For an integer k ∈ {1, 2, . . . , d} and a set S ⊆ Rd with finite positive measure and with
184: measurable Simpk (S), we define bk (S) by
185:                                                    λ(k+1)d (Simpk (S))
186:                                        bk (S) :=                       .
187:                                                        λd (S)k+1
195: of convexity can conceivably admit a nontrivial upper bound in terms of c(S). Our next result
196: shows that such an upper bound on bd (S) exists and is linear in c(S).
197: 
198: Theorem 1.5. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant β = β(d) > 0 such that every set S ⊆ Rd
199: with defined bd (S) satisfies bd (S) ⩽ β c(S).
200: 
201:    We do not know if the linear upper bound in Theorem 1.5 is best possible. We can, however,
202: construct examples showing that the bound is optimal up to a logarithmic factor. This is our
203: last main result.
204: 
205: Theorem 1.6. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant γ = γ(d) > 0 such that for every ε ∈ (0, 1],
206: there is a set S ⊆ Rd satisfying c(S) ⩽ ε and bd (S) ⩾ γ log ε1/ε , and in particular, we have
207:                                                                         2
208: 
209: bd (S) ⩾ γ log c(S)

214:                         2. Bounding the mutual visibility in the plane
215:    The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 1.3. Since the proof is rather long and
216: complicated, let us first present a high-level overview of its main ideas.
217:    We first show that it is sufficient to prove the estimate from Theorem 1.3 for bounded open
218: simply connected sets. This is formalized by the next lemma, whose proof is presented in
219: Appendix A.
220: 
221: Lemma 2.1. Let α > 0 be a constant such that every open bounded simply connected set
222: T ⊆ R2 satisfies b(T ) ⩽ α c(T ). It follows that every p-componentwise simply connected set
223: S ⊆ R2 with defined b(S) satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S).
224: 
225:    Suppose now that S is a bounded open simply connected set. We seek a bound of the form
226: b(S) = O(c(S)). This is equivalent to a bound of the form λ4 (Seg(S)) = O(smc(S)λ2 (S)).
227: We therefore need a suitable upper bound on λ4 (Seg(S)).
228:    We first choose in S a diagonal ` (i.e., an inclusion-maximal line segment in S), and show
243:    We will show that each p-component of Li ∪ Li+1 is a rooted set, with the extra property
244: that all its points are reachable from its root by a polygonal line with at most two segments
245: (Lemma 2.5). To handle such sets, we will generalize the techniques that Cabello et al. [6]
246: have used to handle weakly star-shaped sets in their proof of Theorem 1.1. We will assign to
247: every point A ∈ R a set T(A) of measure O(smc(R)), such that for every (A, B) ∈ Seg(R), we
248: have either B ∈ T(A) or A ∈ T(B) (Lemma 2.7). From this, Theorem 1.3 will follow easily.
249:    To proceed with the proof of Theorem 1.3 for open sets, we need a few auxiliary lemmas.
250: 
251: Lemma 2.2. For every positive integer d, if S is an open subset of Rd , then the set Seg(S)
252: is open and the set Vis(A, S) is open for every point A ∈ S.
253: 
254: Proof. Choose a pair of points (A, B) ∈ Seg(S). Since S is open and AB is compact, there is
255: ε > 0 such that Nε (AB) ⊆ S. Consequently, for any A0 ∈ Nε (A) and B 0 ∈ Nε (B), we have
256: A0 B 0 ⊆ S, that is, (A0 , B 0 ) ∈ Seg(S). This shows that the set Seg(S) is open. If we fix A0 = A,
257: then it follows that the set Vis(A, S) is open.                                                   
258: 
259: Lemma 2.3. Let S be a simply connected subset of R2 and let ` and `0 be line segments in S.
260: It follows that the set Vis(`0 , S) ∩ ` is a (possibly empty) subsegment of `.
267: common, and every point C ∈ AB is visible in R from the point C 0 ∈ A0 B 0 such that D ∈ CC 0 .
268: This shows that Vis(`0 , S) ∩ ` is a convex subset and hence a subsegment of `.             
269:    Now, we define rooted sets and their tree-structured decomposition, and we explain how
270: they arise in the proof of Theorem 1.3.
271:    A set S ⊆ R2 is half-open if every point A ∈ S has a neighborhood Nε (A) that satisfies one
272: of the following two conditions:
273: (1) Nε (A) ⊆ S,
274: (2) Nε (A) ∩ ∂S is a diameter of Nε (A) splitting it into two subsets, one of which (including
275:     the diameter) is Nε (A) ∩ S and the other (excluding the diameter) is Nε (A) r S.
276: The condition (1) holds for points A ∈ S ◦ , while the condition (2) holds for points A ∈ ∂S. A
277: set R ⊆ R2 is a rooted set if the following conditions are satisfied:
278: (1) R is bounded,
279: (2) R is p-connected and simply connected,
280: (3) R is half-open,
281: (4) R ∩ ∂R is an open line segment.

360: Nε (D) ∩ (P r r)} are open and partition the entire r, hence one of them must be empty. This
361: implies that the segments connecting r to P r r lie all in h− or all in h+ . Since P = Vis(r, P ),

652:                                        4. General dimension
653:    This section is devoted to the proofs of Theorem 1.5 and Theorem 1.6. In both proofs, we
654: use the operator Aff to denote the affine hull of a set of points. First, we present the proof of
655: Theorem 1.5.
656:    Let T = (A0 , A1 , . . . , Ad ) be a (d + 1)-tuple of distinct affinely independent points in Rd .
657: We say that a permutation B0 , B1 , . . . , Bd of T is a regular permutation of T if the following
658: two conditions hold:
659: (1) the segment B0 B1 is the diameter of T , and
660: (2) for i = 2, . . . , d − 1, the point Bi has the maximum distance to Aff({B0 , . . . , Bi−1 }) among
661:     the points Bi , Bi+1 , . . . , Bd .
662: Obviously, T has at least two regular permutations due to the interchangeability of B0
663: and B1 . The regular permutation Ai0 , Ai1 , . . . , Aid with the lexicografically minimal vector
664: (i0 , i1 , . . . , id ) is called the canonical permutation of T .
665:     Let T be a (d + 1)-tuple of distinct affinely independent points in Rd , and let B0 , B1 , . . . , Bd
666: be the canonical permutation of T . For i = 1, . . . , d − 1, we define Boxi (T ) inductively as
705: (2) the sides of Boxd (T ) have lengths d1 , 2d2 , 2d3 , . . . , 2dd−1 , and d1 d2 ···d d−1

728:    Now, we present the proof of Theorem 1.6. In the following, we make no serious effort to
729: optimize the constants. As the first step towards the proof of Theorem 1.6, we show that if

733: Lemma 4.3. For every positive integer n and every n-tuple N of points from (0, 1)d , the set
734: S := (0, 1)d r N satisfies bd (S) ⩾ 1/2n.
735: 
736: Proof. Let S and N = {B1 , . . . , Bn } be the sets from the statement and let 0 be the origin. We
737: use S∗d−1 to denote the set of (d−1)-tuples (A1 , . . . , Ad−1 ) ∈ S d−1 that satisfy the following: for

822:     The next step in the proof of Theorem 1.6 is to find a convenient n-tuple N of points from
823: (0, 1)d whose removal produces a set with sufficiently small convexity ratio. To are going to
824: find N using a continuous version of the well-known Epsilon Net Theorem [9]. Before stating
825: this result, we need some definitions.
826:     Let X be a subset of Rd and let U be a set system on X. We say that a set T ⊆ X is
827: shattered by U if every subset of T can be obtained as the intersection of some U ∈ U with T .
828: The Vapnik-Chervonenkis dimension (or VC-dimension) of U, denoted by dim(U), is the
829: maximum n (or ∞ if no such maximum exists) for which some subset of X of cardinality n is
830: shattered by U.
831:     Let U be a system of measurable subsets of a set X ⊆ Rd with λd (X) = 1, and let ε ∈ (0, 1)
832: be a real number. A set N ⊆ X is called an ε-net for (X, U) if N ∩ U 6= ∅ for every U ∈ U
833: with λd (U ) ⩾ ε.
849:                                     E ⊆ K ⊆ C + d(E − C).
850: In particular, we have λd (K)/dd ⩽ λd (E).

904:     It is a natural question whether the bound for bd (S) in Theorem 1.6 can be improved
905: to bd (S) = Ω(c(S)). In the plane, this is related to the famous problem of Danzer and
906: Rogers (see [5, 14] and Problem E14 in [7]) which asks whether for given ε > 0 there is a set
907: N 0 ⊆ (0, 1)2 of size O( 1ε ) with the property that every convex set of area ε within the unit
908: square contains at least one point from N .
909:     If this problem was to be answered affirmatively, then we could use such a set N 0 to stab
910: (0, 1)2 in our proof of Theorem 1.6 which would yield the desired bound for b2 (S). However it
911: is generally believed that the answer is likely to be nonlinear in 1ε .
912: 
913:                          5. Other variants and open problems
914:    We have seen in Theorem 1.3 that a p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2 whose
915: b(S) is defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S), for an absolute constant α ⩽ 180. Equivalently, such a
916: set S satisfies smc(S) ⩾ b(S)λ2 (S)/180.
917:    By a result of Blaschke
918:                        √
919:                             [4] (see also Sas [18]), every convex set K ⊆ R2 contains a triangle
920:                      3 3
921: of measure at least 4π λ2 (K). In view of this, Theorem 1.3 yields the following consequence.
922: Corollary 5.1. There is a constant α > 0 such that every p-componentwise simply connected
923: set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined contains a triangle T ⊆ S of measure at least α b(S)λ2 (S).
924:    A similar argument works in higher dimensions as well. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant
925: β = β(d) such that every convex set K ⊆ Rd contains a simplex of measure at least βλd (K)
926: (see e.g. Lassak [12]). Therefore, Theorem 1.5 can be rephrased in the following equivalent
927: form.
928: Corollary 5.2. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant α = α(d) > 0 such that every set S ⊆ Rd
929: whose bd (S) is defined contains a simplex T of measure at least α bd (S)λd (S).
930:    What can we say about sets S ⊆ R2 that are not p-componentwise simply connected? First
931: of all, we can consider a weaker form of simple connectivity: we call a set S p-componentwise
932: simply ∆-connected if for every triangle T such that ∂T ⊆ S we have T ⊆ S. We conjecture
933: that Theorem 1.3 can be extended to p-componentwise simply ∆-connected sets.
934: Conjecture 5.3. There is an absolute constant α > 0 such that every p-componentwise simply
935: ∆-connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S).
936: 
937:                     ON THE BEER INDEX OF CONVEXITY AND ITS VARIANTS                             17
938: 
939: 
940:    What does the value of b(S) say about a planar set S that does not satisfy even a weak
941: form of simple connectivity? As Example 3.1 shows, such a set may not contain any convex
942: subset of positive measure, even when b(S) is equal to 1. However, we conjecture that a large
943: b(S) implies the existence of a large convex set whose boundary belongs to S.
944: 
945: Conjecture 5.4. For every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ R2 is a set with b(S) ⩾ ε,
946: then there is a bounded convex set C ⊆ R2 with λ(C) ⩾ δλ(S) and ∂C ⊆ S.
947: 
948:    Theorem 1.3 shows that Conjecture 5.4 holds for p-componentwise simply connected sets,
949: with δ being a constant multiple of ε. It is possible that even in the general setting of
950: Conjecture 5.4, δ may be taken as a constant multiple of ε.
951:    Motivated by Corollary 5.1, we propose a stronger version of Conjecture 5.4, where the
952: convex set C is required to be a triangle.
953: 
954: Conjecture 5.5. For every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ R2 is a set with b(S) ⩾ ε,
955: then there is a triangle T ⊆ R2 with λ(T ) ⩾ δλ(S) and ∂T ⊆ S.
956: 
957:    Note that Conjecture 5.5 holds when restricted to p-componentwise simply connected sets,
958: as implied by Corollary 5.1.
959:    We may generalise Conjecture 5.5 to higher dimensions and to higher-order indices of
960: convexity. To state the general conjecture, we introduce the following notation: for a set
961:                
962: X ⊆ Rd , let Xk be the set of k-element subsets of X, and let the set Skelk (X) be defined by
963:                                                [
964:                                Skelk (X) :=          Conv(Y ).
965:                                                  X
966:                                             Y ∈(k+1)
967: 
968: If X is the vertex set of a d-dimensional simplex T = Conv(X), then Skelk (X) is often called
969: the k-dimensional skeleton of T . Our general conjecture states, roughly speaking, that sets
970: with large k-index of convexity should contain the k-dimensional skeleton of a large simplex.
971: Here is the precise statement.
972: 
973: Conjecture 5.6. For every k, d ∈ N such that 1 ⩽ k ⩽ d and every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0
974: such that if S ⊆ Rd is a set with bk (S) ⩾ ε, then there is a simplex T with vertex set X such
975: that λd (T ) ⩾ δλd (S) and Skelk (X) ⊆ S.
976: 
977:    Corollary 5.2 asserts that this conjecture holds in the special case of k = d, since Skeld (X) =
978: Conv(X) = T . Corollary 5.1 shows that the conjecture holds for k = d = 1 if S is further
979: assumed to be p-componentwise simply connected. In all these cases, δ can be taken as a
980: constant multiple of ε, with the constant depending on k and d.
981:    Finally, we may ask whether there is a way to generalize Theorem 1.3 to higher dimensions,
982: by replacing simple connectivity with another topological property. Here is an example of one
983: such possible generalization.
984: 
985: Conjecture 5.7. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant α = α(d) > 0 such that if S ⊆ Rd is a
986: set with defined bd−1 (S) whose every p-component is contractible, then bd−1 (S) ⩽ α c(S).
987: 
988:    We can prove this conjecture in the special case when S is an appropriate cone of a subset R
989: of Rd−1 for which bd−1 (R) is defined. Here, a cone of R is the set {tA : t ∈ [0, 1], A ∈ R × {d}}
990: in Rd . It is not difficult to show that for d ⩾ 2 and R ⊆ Rd−1 taking a cone of R preserves
991: c(R) and bk (R) for every k ∈ {1, . . . , d − 1}. The rest then follows from Theorem 1.5.

1039:                                 Appendix A. Proof of Lemma 2.1
1040:    In this section, we prove Lemma 2.1, which reduces the general setting of Theorem 1.3 to
1041: the case that S is a bounded open simply connected subset of R2 .
1042: 
1043: Lemma A.1. Let S ⊆ R2 be a set whose b(S) is defined. For every ε > 0, there is a
1044: bounded set S 0 ⊆ S such that λ(S 0 ) ⩾ (1 − ε)λ(S) and b(S 0 ) ⩾ b(S) − ε. Moreover, if S is
1045: p-componentwise simply connected, then so is S 0 .
1046: 
1047: Proof. Let B be an open ball centered at origin. Consider the sets S 0 = S ∩ B and S0 = S r B
1048: partitioning the set S. Fix the radius of B large enough, so that S0 has measure at most ελ(S)/2.
1049: We claim that S 0 has the properties stated in the lemma. Clearly λ(S 0 ) ⩾ (1 − ε/2)λ(S) >
1050: (1 − ε)λ(S). Moreover, Seg(S 0 ) = Seg(S) r ((S0 × S) ∪ (S × S0 )), and hence Seg(S 0 ) is
1051: measurable and λ4 (Seg(S 0 )) ⩾ λ4 (Seg(S)) − ελ(S)2 . Therefore,

19:                                                        We also consider higher-order generalizations of b(S). For 1 ⩽ k ⩽ d, the k-index of convexity
20:                                                    bk (S) of a set S ⊆ Rd is the probability that the convex hull of a (k + 1)-tuple of points chosen
21:                                                    uniformly independently at random from S is contained in S. We show that for every d ⩾ 2
22:                                                    there is a constant β(d) > 0 such that every set S ⊆ Rd satisfies bd (S) ⩽ β c(S), provided
23:                                                    bd (S) exists. We provide an almost matching lower bound by showing that there is a constant

140: b(S) cannot be bounded from above by a sublinear function of c(S), even for simple polygons S.
141:    For weakly star-shaped polygons, Cabello et al. [6] showed that the above example is essentially
142: optimal, providing the following linear upper bound on b(S).
143: Theorem 1.1 ([6, Theorem 5]). For every weakly star-shaped simple polygon P , we have
144: b(P ) ⩽ 18 c(P ).
145:   For polygons that are not weakly star-shaped, Cabello et al. [6] gave a superlinear bound.
146: Theorem 1.2 ([6, Theorem 6]). Every simple polygon P satisfies
147:                                                        1
148:                                                           
149:                            b(P ) ⩽ 12 c(P ) 1 + log2         .
150:                                                      c(P )
151:    Moreover, Cabello et al. [6] conjectured that even for a general simple polygon P , b(P ) can
152: be bounded from above by a linear function of c(P ). (The question whether b(P ) = O(c(P )) for
153: simple polygons P was originally asked by Cabello and Saumell [7].) The next theorem, which
154: is the first main result of this paper, verifies this conjecture. Recall that b(S) is defined for a set
155: S if and only if S has finite positive measure and Seg(S) is measurable. Recall also that a set
156: is p-componentwise simply connected if its p-components are simply connected. In particular,
157: every simply connected set is p-componentwise simply connected.
158: Theorem 1.3. Every p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined
159: satisfies b(S) ⩽ 180 c(S).
160:   Clearly, every simple polygon satisfies the assumptions of Theorem 1.3. Hence we directly
161: obtain the following, which verifies the conjecture of Cabello et al. [6].
162: Corollary 1.4. Every simple polygon P ⊆ R2 satisfies b(P ) ⩽ 180 c(P ).
163:

166: 
167:    The main restriction in Theorem 1.3 is the assumption that S is p-componentwise simply
168: connected. This assumption cannot be omitted, as shown by the set S := [0, 1]2 r Q2 , where it
169: is easy to verify that c(S) = 0 and b(S) = 1, see Proposition 3.7.
170:    A related construction shows that Theorem 1.3 fails in higher dimensions. To see this, consider
171: again the set S := [0, 1]2 r Q2 , and define a set S 0 ⊆ R3 by
172:                             S 0 := {(tx, ty, t) : t ∈ [0, 1] and (x, y) ∈ S}.
173: Again, it is easy to verify that c(S 0 ) = 0 and b(S 0 ) = 1, although S 0 is simply connected, even
174: star-shaped.
175:    Despite these examples, we will show that meaningful analogues of Theorem 1.3 for higher
176: dimensions and for sets that are not p-componentwise simply connected are possible. The key is
177: to use higher-order generalizations of the Beer index, which we introduce now.
178:    For k ∈ {1, . . . , d} and a set S ⊆ Rd , we define the set Simpk (S) ⊆ (Rd )k+1 by
179:                  Simpk (S) := {(A0 , . . . , Ak ) ∈ S k+1 : Conv({A0 , . . . , Ak }) ⊆ S},
180: where the operator Conv denotes the convex hull of a set of points. We call Simpk (S) the
181: k-simplex set of S. Note that Simp1 (S) = Seg(S).
182:   For k ∈ {1, . . . , d} and a set S ⊆ Rd with finite positive measure and with measurable
183: Simpk (S), we define bk (S) by
184:                                                 λ(k+1)d (Simpk (S))
185:                                     bk (S) :=                       .
186:                                                     λd (S)k+1
194: can conceivably admit a nontrivial upper bound in terms of c(S). Our next result shows that
195: such an upper bound on bd (S) exists and is linear in c(S).
196: 
197: Theorem 1.5. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant β = β(d) > 0 such that every set S ⊆ Rd
198: with bd (S) defined satisfies bd (S) ⩽ β c(S).
199: 
200:    We do not know if the linear upper bound in Theorem 1.5 is best possible. We can, however,
201: construct examples showing that the bound is optimal up to a logarithmic factor. This is our
202: last main result.
203: 
204: Theorem 1.6. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant γ = γ(d) > 0 such that for every ε ∈ (0, 1),
205: there is a set S ⊆ Rd satisfying c(S) ⩽ ε and bd (S) ⩾ γ log ε1/ε , and in particular, we have

214:                      2. Bounding the mutual visibility in the plane
215:   The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 1.3. Since the proof is rather long and complicated,
216: we first present a high-level overview of its main ideas.
217: 
218:                      ON THE BEER INDEX OF CONVEXITY AND ITS VARIANTS                               5

220: 
221:   We first show that it is sufficient to prove the estimate from Theorem 1.3 for bounded open
222: simply connected sets. This is formalized by the next lemma, whose proof can be found in
223: Section 2.2.
224: Lemma 2.1. Let α > 0 be a constant such that every bounded open simply connected set T ⊆ R2
225: satisfies b(T ) ⩽ α c(T ). It follows that every p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2 with
226: b(S) defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S).
227:    In the proof of Lemma 2.1, we first show that the set S can be reduced to a bounded open
228: set S 00 whose Beer index b(S 00 ) can be arbitrarily close to b(S) from below. This is done by
229: considering a part S 0 of S that is contained in a sufficiently large disc and by showing that all
230: segments in S 0 are in fact contained in the interior of S 0 , except for a set of measure zero. The
231: proof is then finished by choosing S 00 as the interior of S 0 and by applying the assumption of the
232: lemma to every p-component of S 00 .
247:    We will show that each p-component of Li ∪ Li+1 is a rooted set, with the extra property
248: that all its points are reachable from its root by a polygonal line with at most two segments
249: (Lemma 2.5). To handle such sets, we will generalize the techniques that Cabello et al. [6] have
250: used to handle weakly star-shaped sets in their proof of Theorem 1.1. We will assign to every
251: point A ∈ R a set T(A) of measure O(smc(R)), such that for every (A, B) ∈ Seg(R), we have
252: either B ∈ T(A) or A ∈ T(B) (Lemma 2.7). From this, Theorem 1.3 will follow easily.
253: 2.1. Proof of Theorem 1.3 for bounded open simply connected sets. First, we need a
254: few auxiliary lemmas.
255: Lemma 2.2. For every positive integer d, if S is an open subset of Rd , then the set Seg(S) is
256: open and the set Vis(A, S) is open for every point A ∈ S.
257: Proof. Choose a pair of points (A, B) ∈ Seg(S). Since S is open and AB is compact, there is
258: ε > 0 such that Nε (AB) ⊆ S. Consequently, for any A0 ∈ Nε (A) and B 0 ∈ Nε (B), we have
259: A0 B 0 ⊆ S, that is, (A0 , B 0 ) ∈ Seg(S). This shows that the set Seg(S) is open. If we fix A0 = A,
260: then it follows that the set Vis(A, S) is open.                                                   
261: Lemma 2.3. Let S be a simply connected subset of R2 and let ` and `0 be line segments in S.
262: It follows that the set Vis(`0 , S) ∩ ` is a (possibly empty) subsegment of `.
263: Proof. The statement is trivially true if ` and `0 intersect or have the same supporting line, or if
264: Vis(`0 , S) ∩ ` is empty. Suppose that these situations do not occur. Let A, B ∈ ` and A0 , B 0 ∈ `0
272: point D in common, and every point C ∈ AB is visible in R from the point C 0 ∈ A0 B 0 such that
273: D ∈ CC 0 . This shows that Vis(`0 , S) ∩ ` is a convex subset and hence a subsegment of `.         
274:    Now, we define rooted sets and their tree-structured decomposition, and we explain how they
275: arise in the proof of Theorem 1.3.
276:    A set S ⊆ R2 is half-open if every point A ∈ S has a neighborhood Nε (A) that satisfies one of
277: the following two conditions:
278: (1) Nε (A) ⊆ S,
279: (2) Nε (A) ∩ ∂S is a diameter of Nε (A) splitting it into two subsets, one of which (including the
280:     diameter) is Nε (A) ∩ S and the other (excluding the diameter) is Nε (A) r S.
281: The condition (1) holds for points A ∈ S ◦ , while the condition (2) holds for points A ∈ ∂S.
282:   A set R ⊆ R2 is a rooted set if the following conditions are satisfied:
283: (1) R is bounded,
284: (2) R is p-connected and simply connected,
285: (3) R is half-open,
286: (4) R ∩ ∂R is an open line segment.

1363:    The next step in the proof of Theorem 1.6 is to find a convenient n-tuple N of points from

1367:    Let X be a subset of Rd and let U be a set system on X. We say that a set T ⊆ X is shattered
1368: by U if every subset of T can be obtained as the intersection of some U ∈ U with T . The Vapnik-
1369: Chervonenkis dimension (or VC-dimension) of U, denoted by dim(U), is the maximum n (or ∞
1370: if no such maximum exists) for which some subset of X of cardinality n is shattered by U.

1441:    It is a natural question whether the bound for bd (S) in Theorem 1.6 can be improved to
1442: bd (S) = Ω(c(S)). In the plane, this is related to the famous problem of Danzer and Rogers (see
1443: [5, 15] and Problem E14 in [8]) which asks whether for given ε > 0 there is a set N 0 ⊆ (0, 1)2 of
1444: size O( 1ε ) with the property that every convex set of area ε within the unit square contains at
1445: least one point from N 0 .
1446:    If this problem was to be answered affirmatively, then we could use such a set N 0 to stab
1447: (0, 1)2 in our proof of Theorem 1.6 which would yield the desired bound for b2 (S). However it is
1448: generally believed that the answer is likely to be nonlinear in 1ε .

1450: 3.3. A set with large k-index of convexity and small convexity ratio.
1451: 
1452: Proposition 3.7. For every integer d ⩾ 2, the set S := [0, 1]d r Qd satisfies c(S) = 0 and
1453: bk (S) = 1 for every positive integer k < d.
1454: 
1455: Proof. Since Qd is countable and λd ([0, 1]d ) = 1, we have λd (S) = 1. Every convex subset K of
1456: [0, 1]d with positive d-dimensional measure contains an open d-dimensional ball B with positive
1457: diameter, as there is a (d + 1)-tuple of affinely independent points of K. Since Qd is a dense
1458: subset of Rd , we see that B ∩ Qd 6= ∅ and thus c(S) = 0.
1474: and we already know that λd (S) = 1 = λd ([0, 1]d ), hence bk (S) = 1.                     

1476:                                4. Other variants and open problems
1477:    We have seen in Theorem 1.3 that a p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2 whose
1478: b(S) is defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S), for an absolute constant α ⩽ 180. Equivalently, such a
1479: set S satisfies smc(S) ⩾ b(S)λ2 (S)/180.
1480:    By a result of Blaschke
1481:                    √
1482:                             [4] (see also Sas [19]), every convex set K ⊆ R2 contains a triangle of
1483: measure at least 34π3 λ2 (K). In view of this, Theorem 1.3 yields the following consequence.
1484: 
1485: Corollary 4.1. There is a constant α > 0 such that every p-componentwise simply connected
1486: set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined contains a triangle T ⊆ S of measure at least α b(S)λ2 (S).
1487: 
1488:    A similar argument works in higher dimensions as well. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant
1489: β = β(d) such that every convex set K ⊆ Rd contains a simplex of measure at least βλd (K) (see
1490: e.g. Lassak [13]). Therefore, Theorem 1.5 can be rephrased in the following equivalent form.
1491: 
1492: Corollary 4.2. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant α = α(d) > 0 such that every set S ⊆ Rd
1493: whose bd (S) is defined contains a simplex T of measure at least α bd (S)λd (S).
1494: 
1495: 28             MARTIN BALKO, VÍT JELÍNEK, PAVEL VALTR, AND BARTOSZ WALCZAK
1496: 
1497: 
1498:    What can we say about sets S ⊆ R2 that are not p-componentwise simply connected? First
1499: of all, we can consider a weaker form of simple connectivity: we call a set S p-componentwise
1500: simply 4-connected if for every triangle T such that ∂T ⊆ S we have T ⊆ S. We conjecture
1501: that Theorem 1.3 can be extended to p-componentwise simply 4-connected sets.
1502: Conjecture 4.3. There is an absolute constant α > 0 such that every p-componentwise simply
1503: 4-connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S).
1504:    What does the value of b(S) say about a planar set S that does not satisfy even a weak form
1505: of simple connectivity? As Proposition 3.7 shows, such a set may not contain any convex subset
1506: of positive measure, even when b(S) is equal to 1. However, we conjecture that a large b(S)
1507: implies the existence of a large convex set whose boundary belongs to S.
1508: Conjecture 4.4. For every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ R2 is a set with b(S) ⩾ ε,
1509: then there is a bounded convex set C ⊆ R2 with λ(C) ⩾ δλ(S) and ∂C ⊆ S.
1510:    Theorem 1.3 shows that Conjecture 4.4 holds for p-componentwise simply connected sets,
1511: with δ being a constant multiple of ε. It is possible that even in the general setting of Conjecture
1512: 4.4, δ can be taken as a constant multiple of ε.
1513:    Motivated by Corollary 4.1, we propose a stronger version of Conjecture 4.4, where the convex
1514: set C is required to be a triangle.
1515: Conjecture 4.5. For every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ R2 is a set with b(S) ⩾ ε,
1516: then there is a triangle T ⊆ R2 with λ(T ) ⩾ δλ(S) and ∂T ⊆ S.
1517:   Note that Conjecture 4.5 holds when restricted to p-componentwise simply connected sets, as
1518: implied by Corollary 4.1.
1519:   We can generalize Conjecture 4.5 to higher dimensions and to higher-order indices of convexity.
1520: To state the general conjecture, we introduce the following notation: for a set X ⊆ Rd , let X
1521:                                                                                                 
1522:                                                                                               k
1523: be the set of k-element subsets of X, and let the set Skelk (X) be defined by
1524:                                                    [
1525:                                   Skelk (X) :=                Conv(Y ).
1526:                                                          X
1527:                                                  Y ∈(     )
1528:                                                         k+1
1529: 
1530: If X is the vertex set of a d-dimensional simplex T = Conv(X), then Skelk (X) is often called
1531: the k-dimensional skeleton of T . Our general conjecture states, roughly speaking, that sets with
1532: large k-index of convexity should contain the k-dimensional skeleton of a large simplex. Here is
1533: the precise statement.
1534: Conjecture 4.6. For every k, d ∈ N such that 1 ⩽ k ⩽ d and every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0
1535: such that if S ⊆ Rd is a set with bk (S) ⩾ ε, then there is a simplex T with vertex set X such
1536: that λd (T ) ⩾ δλd (S) and Skelk (X) ⊆ S.
1537:    Corollary 4.2 asserts that this conjecture holds in the special case of k = d ⩾ 2, since
1538: Skeld (X) = Conv(X) = T . Corollary 4.1 shows that the conjecture holds for k = 1 and d = 2
1539: if S is further assumed to be p-componentwise simply connected. In all these cases, δ can be
1540: taken as a constant multiple of ε, with the constant depending on k and d.
1541:    Finally, we can ask whether there is a way to generalize Theorem 1.3 to higher dimensions,
1542: by replacing simple connectivity with another topological property. Here is an example of one
1543: such possible generalization.
1544: Conjecture 4.7. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant α = α(d) > 0 such that if S ⊆ Rd is a
1545: set with bd−1 (S) defined whose every p-component is contractible, then bd−1 (S) ⩽ α c(S).
1546: 
1547:                         ON THE BEER INDEX OF CONVEXITY AND ITS VARIANTS                                       29
1548: 
1549: 
1550:    A modification of the proof of Theorem 1.5 implies that Conjecture 4.7 is true for star-shaped
1551: sets S.

575:     In this section we prove formulas for |SMλ −1 (λ)| and |SMλ −2 (λ)| in the rectangular case, generalizing
576: our observations about the three-row tableaux in Appendix A. We then prove a special case of the
577: “tail-end” bijection and conjecture as to how this result extends to the general rectangular case. For
578: this entire section, we restrict our attention to rectangular tableaux, although the authors suspect that
579: there may exist generalizations to non-rectangular tableaux that mirror Subsection 3.2.
580: 
581: 4.1    Calculating |SMλ −1 (λ)| and |SMλ −2 (λ)|, Rectangular Case
582: Directly enumerating inverted tableaux becomes increasingly tractable as one approaches the maximum
583: inversion number Mλ . This is because having large numbers of inversions in a fixed column increases
584: the likelihood that the tableaux splits after that column. In the (Mλ − 1)- and (Mλ − 2)-inversion
585: cases, non-split tableaux are so rare that they may be easily enumerated. As split tableaux are easily
586: counted, this allows for relatively straightforward closed formulas for |SMλ −1 (λ)| and |SMλ −2 (λ)|.
587:

604:    In case #1, there is a unique ordering of the elements in the leftmost column (relative to the column
605: immediately on its right) and the tableaux necessarily splits after the first column. It follows that the
606: number of tableaux satisfying this case equals |SM(n−1) −1 (n − 1)|.
607:

612:     In case #2, there are two sub-cases depending upon whether the largest entry am or the second
613: largest entry am−1 in the leftmost column is directly to the left of the smallest entry b1 in the column
614: immediately on its right. For an illustration of these two sub-cases when m = 4, see Figure 9 below.
615:     In the first sub-case, the tableaux must split after the first column, so that the first column must
616: contain the entries 1, 2, . . . , m. Since there is a unique way to produce M(n−1) inversions across the
617: latter columns, the placement of m + 1, m + 2, . . . , mn is predetermined. In the first column, there are
618: m − 1 inversions involving am and we must have Tm−1 − 1 − (m − 1) = Tm−2 − 1 inversions among the
619: remaining m − 1 entries in that column. There are M (m − 2, Tm−2 − 1) = m − 2 distinct arrangements
620: that produce these Tm−2 − 1 inversions. This means that there are precisely m − 2 inverted tableaux
621: satisfying this sub-case.
622:     In the second sub-case, the tableaux need not split after the first column, as the largest entry am
623: in the leftmost column may or may not be greater than the smallest entry b1 in the column directly
624: to its right. No matter the relationship between these two entries, there is a unique arrangement
625: that produces the requisite M(n−1) inversions across the latter columns. In the first column, there are
626: always m − 2 inversions involving the top entry am−1 , leaving Tm−2 inversions among the remaining
627: m − 1 entries in the column. There is only M (m − 2, Tm−2 ) = 1 arrangement of the remaining entries in
628: the leftmost column that produces these Tm−2 inversions. Also notice that the required arrangement
629: in the leftmost column ensures that am is directly to the left of the second-smallest entry b2 of the
630: next column, and that the tableaux always splits after the second column. This leaves the relationship
631: between am and b1 as the only open question about such a tableaux. It follows that there are precisely
632: 2 inverted tableaux in this sub-case (one for am < b1 , one for am > b1 ).
633:     Collecting results from all sub-cases and then applying the inductive assumption gives the desired
634: result: |SMn (n)| = |SMn−1 (n − 1)| + (m − 2) + 2 = (m(n − 1) − 1) + (m − 2) + 2 = mn − 1.

699: such tableaux must split after the second column.

851:                                                                                 e
852: Conjecture 4.4. Let n ≥ 1, m ≥ 2, and take the m-row shapes λ = (n, . . . , n), λ(n+1, n, . . . , n, n−1).

856:     It is the authors’ belief that it may be possible to prove Conjecture 4.4 via a “recursive bumping”
857: algorithm superficially similar to the method of proof in Theorem 3.1. In this modified algorithm, the
858:                        e map would begin in the rightmost column that contains greater than nTm−2
859: φ1 : Si (λ) ֒→ Si−m+1 (λ)
860: inversions. The first rightward “bump” would entail a reordering of the initial column in a manner
861: that is uniquely determine by the bijection of Lemma 4.3. Subsequent rightward “bumps” would then
862: be required the conserve inversion number in a manner that can be uniquely undone by some inverse
863: algorithm φ2 : Si−m+1 (eλ) ֒→ Si (λ).
864:     The difficulty in proving Conjecture 4.4 derives from these later “bumps”. In particular, if a
865: tableaux possesses additional inversions to the right of the starting column, those inversions make the
866: preservation of inversion number very complicated when attempting to bump a distinguished element
867: past their column. Resolving the difficulties in this proof, as well as developing tractable methods
868: for dealing with |Si (λ)| when i is greater than 1 but too small to be in the “tail-end”, are the most
869: significant remaining questions about enumerating tableaux inversions.

138: 1, n, . . . , n, n − 1). Then |S1 (λ)| = |S0 (λ)|.
139: 
140:    We then turn to tableaux with more than one inversion. Working downward from the
141: maximum inversion number Mλ , Theorem 4.1 and 4.2 combine to prove the following closed
142: formulas enumerating the number of m × n rectangular tableaux with precisely Mλ − 1 and
143: Mλ − 2 inversions:

147: Theorem 1.4. Take n ≥ 1, m ≥ 3 and consider the m-row rectangular shape λ = (n, . . . , n).
148: Then |SMλ −1 (λ)| = mn − 1 and |SMλ −2 (λ)| = (mn−2)(mn+1)
149:                                                    2
150: 
151:     Closing the paper are a series of more specific results and conjectures enumerating inverted
152: tableaux whose inversion number is “sufficiently large” (a realm that we refer to as the “tail end”
153: of a shape’s inversion distribution). All results and conjectures in this paper were developed
154: with the help of a computer program developed by the authors. Details about this program, as
155: well as several tables of generated results, may be found in Appendix A.
156: 
157: 
158: 2     Basic Enumerative Results About Tableau Inversions
159: In this section, we present a number of foundational enumerative results involving tableau
160: inversions that are independent from the more involved methods of Sections 3 and 4.
161: 
162: 2.1     Total Number of Inverted Young Tableaux of Shape λ
163: Let λ = (λ1 , λ2S  , . . . , λm ) be a non-increasing partition of the positive integer N, and consider
164: the set I(λ) = i Si (λ) of inverted Young tableaux of shape λ (with any number of inversions
291: after its j th column.
292:     It is well-known that the number of rectangular standard Young tableaux of size 2 × n
293: equals the nth Catalan number Cn , a fact that can be directly calculated from the Hook-Length
294: Formula of Theorem 1.1 or by setting up a bijection with noncrossing matchings. This means
295: that |S0 (n, n)| = Cn .
296: 
297:                                                     6
298: 
299:     To find |Si (n, n)| for i > 0, we begin by noting that each column in a two-row inverted Young
300: tableau has either 0 or 1 inversion pair. If a column admits an inversion pair, this means that
301: the larger entry in that column containing the inversion much be smaller than the smallest of the
302: two entries in the column directly to the right. It follows that a two-row inverted Young tableau
303: must split after any column in which it has an inversion. For an example of this phenomenon
304: see Figure 3, where larger subscripts correspond to larger entries. In that example, b2 < c1
305: ensures that the tableau must split.
349: 
350:     Readers in search of additional combinatorial identities should note that, when successively
351: ranging over all n > 0, the sequence formed by the |Si (n, n)| is A039599 on OEIS [1].
352:     In light of Theorem 1.2, Theorem 2.3 applies to give a closed formula for all Betti numbers
353: of the Springer variety Fλ when λ = (n, n). Also note that, as (1, . . . , 1) is the only partition of
354: n of length at least n, then Mn,n = n and |Sn (n, n)| = 1 as predicted by Proposition 2.2.
355: 
356: 
357: 3     Enumerating 1-Inverted Young Tableaux
358: For the remainder of this paper, we introduce a number of techniques that allow us to calculate
359: the number of inverted Young tableaux |Si (λ)| with precisely i inversions for a variety of shapes
360: λ. One of our primary techniques will be to develop bijections between i-inverted tableaux
361: of shape λ and j-inverted tableaux of some other shape e    λ. In some cases, this second set
362: of inverted Young tableaux will then be directly enumerable via the Hook-length formula or
363: the methods of Section 2. In this particular section we begin by restricting our attention to
593: rectangular tableaux with λ = (n, n, n), this tail-end consistently begins at i = n + 1.
594:     In this section we prove formulas for |SMλ −1 (λ)| and |SMλ −2 (λ)| in the rectangular case,
595: generalizing our observations about the three-row tableaux in Appendix A. We then prove a
596: special case of the “tail-end” bijection and conjecture as to how this result extends to the general
597: rectangular case. For this entire section, we restrict our attention to rectangular tableaux,
598: although the authors suspect that there may exist generalizations to non-rectangular tableaux
599: that mirror Subsection 3.2.
600: 
601: 4.1    Calculating |SMλ −1(λ)| and |SMλ −2(λ)|, Rectangular Case
602: Directly enumerating inverted tableaux becomes increasingly tractable as one approaches the
603: maximum inversion number Mλ . This is because having large numbers of inversions in a fixed
604: column increases the likelihood that the tableau splits after that column. In the (Mλ − 1)- and
605: (Mλ − 2)-inversion cases, non-split tableaux are so rare that they may be easily enumerated.
606: 
607:                                                 12
623:   2. The leftmost column has precisely M1 − 1 inversions and the remaining (n − 1) columns
624:      have a total of precisely M(n−1) inversions.
625:     In case #1, there is a unique ordering of the elements in the leftmost column (relative to
626: the column immediately on its right) and the tableau necessarily splits after the first column.
627: It follows that the number of tableaux satisfying this case equals |SM(n−1) −1 (n − 1)|.
628:     In case #2, there are two sub-cases depending upon whether the largest entry am or the
629: second largest entry am−1 in the leftmost column is directly to the left of the smallest entry b1
630: in the column immediately on its right. For an illustration of these two sub-cases when m = 4,
631: see Figure 9 below.
632:     In the first sub-case, the tableau must split after the first column, so that the first column
633: must contain the entries 1, 2, . . . , m. Since there is a unique way to produce M(n−1) inversions
634: across the latter columns, the placement of m + 1, m + 2, . . . , mn is predetermined. In the first
635: column, there are m−1 inversions involving am and we must have Tm−1 −1−(m−1) = Tm−2 −1
636: inversions among the remaining m − 1 entries in that column. There are M(m − 2, Tm−2 − 1) =
637: m − 2 distinct arrangements that produce these Tm−2 − 1 inversions. This means that there are
638: precisely m − 2 inverted tableaux satisfying this sub-case.
639:     In the second sub-case, the tableau need not split after the first column, as the largest entry
640: am in the leftmost column may or may not be greater than the smallest entry b1 in the column
641: directly to its right. No matter the relationship between these two entries, there is a unique
642: arrangement that produces the requisite M(n−1) inversions across the latter columns. In the first
643: column, there are always m − 2 inversions involving the top entry am−1 , leaving Tm−2 inversions
644: among the remaining m−1 entries in the column. There is only M(m−2, Tm−2 ) = 1 arrangement
645: of the remaining entries in the leftmost column that produces these Tm−2 inversions. Also notice
646: that the required arrangement in the leftmost column ensures that am is directly to the left of
647: the second-smallest entry b2 of the next column, and that the tableau always splits after the
648: second column. This leaves the relationship between am and b1 as the only open question about
649: such a tableau. It follows that there are precisely 2 inverted tableaux in this sub-case (one for
650: am < b1 , one for am > b1 ).
692:      columns have precisely M(n−1) inversions.

694:     In case #1, there is a unique ordering of the leftmost column to obtain the maximal number
695: of M1 inversions and the tableau necessarily splits after the first column. It follows that the
696: number of inverted tableaux in this case equals |SM(n−1) −2 (n − 1)|.
697:     In case #2, there are two sub-cases depending upon whether the largest entry am or the
698: second largest entry am−1 in the leftmost column is directly left of the smallest entry b1 in the
699: second column. For an illustration of these sub-cases when m = 4, see Figure 10.
700:     In the first sub-case of case #2, the tableau must split after the first column. In the first
701: column, am participates in m − 1 inversions and thus the remaining elements must account for
702: Tm−1 − 1 − (m − 1) = Tm−2 − 1 total inversions. By Theorem 4.1, there are m − 2 ways to
703: achieve these Tm−2 − 1 inversions. It follows that there are precisely (m − 2)|SM(n−1) −1 (n − 1)|
704: tableaux in this sub-case.
705:     In the second sub-case of case #2, the tableau needn’t split after the first column. In the
706: first column, am−1 accounts for m − 2 inversions and hence the remaining elements in that
707:

709: 
710: column must account for Tm−2 inversions. This implies that the rest of the first column must
711: be “fully inverted”, and thus that am is directly to the left of the second-smallest entry b2 in
712: the second column. We then have am−1 < b1 and am < b2 , leaving the relationship between am
713: and b1 as the only question about the first two columns. Also noticing that b1 is directly to the
714: left of either cm or cm−1 in the third column, as m ≥ 3 we know that am < b2 < c2 ≤ cm−1 /cm .
715: This means we needn’t worry about the relationship between am and any elements of the third
716: column. For each possible arrangement of the tableau’s final n − 1 columns, it follows that there
717: are precisely two inverted tableaux fitting this sub-case (one for am < b1 , one for am > b1 ).
718: Thus there are precisely 2|SM(n−1) −1 (n − 1)| tableaux in this sub-case.
719:     In case #3 there are four sub-cases, each depending upon which elements in the first column
720: are directly to the left of the smallest two entries b1 , b2 in the second column. Once again,
721: reference Figure 10 for an illustration of these sub-cases in the situation where m = 4. Notice
722: that in all of these sub-cases, the M(n−1) inversions needed over the final n − 1 columns ensures
723: that there is precisely one arrangement of those columns for each arrangement of the first
724: column. It also guarantees that any such tableau must split after the second column.

728: case of this theorem, there are (m−1−2)(m−1+1)

751: sub-case.

753: is involved in m − 3 inversions, leaving Tm−1 − 2 − (m − 3) = Tm−2 inversions for the remaining
754: m−1 entries of that column. There is precisely one arrangement of those entries that gives Tm−2
755: inversions, but in this situation we still need to determine the relationship of both am and am−1
756: to b1 . Here there are possible orderings am−1 < am < b1 , am−1 < b1 < am , and b1 < am−1 < am .
757: As this is the only undetermined aspect of these tableaux, there are precisely 3 tableaux in this
758: sub-case.

892:     It is the authors’ belief that it may be possible to prove Conjecture 4.4 via a “recursive
893: bumping” algorithm superficially similar to the method of proof in Theorem 3.1. In this modified
894:                                       e map would begin in the rightmost column that contains
895: algorithm, the φ1 : Si (λ) ֒→ Si−m+1 (λ)
896: greater than nTm−2 inversions. The first rightward “bump” would entail a reordering of the
897: initial column in a manner that is uniquely determine by the bijection of Lemma 4.3. Subsequent
898: rightward “bumps” would then be required the conserve inversion number in a manner that can
899: be uniquely undone by some inverse algorithm φ2 : Si−m+1 (λ)   e ֒→ Si (λ).
900:     The difficulty in proving Conjecture 4.4 derives from these later “bumps”. In particular, if a
901: tableau possesses additional inversions to the right of the starting column, those inversions make
902: the preservation of inversion number very complicated when attempting to bump a distinguished
903: element past their column. Resolving the difficulties in this proof, as well as developing tractable
904: methods for dealing with |Si (λ)| when i is greater than 1 but too small to be in the “tail-end”,
905: are the most significant remaining questions about enumerating tableaux inversions.

57: 1    Introduction

104: The above constraint is indeed satisfied under the Kirchhoff boundary conditions in (1.2).
105: It further follows (see, e.g., Theorem 1.4.4 in [4]) that the operator ∆ is in fact self-adjoint
