110:    The linear Schrödinger equation on the tadpole graph can be written in the compact
111: form
112:                                         ∂
113:                                        i Ψ = ∆Ψ,                                      (1.3)
114:                                         ∂t
115: where Ψ = Ψ(t, x). We are interested in the nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the tadpole
116: graph, which is the natural generalization of the linear Schrödinger equation (1.3),
240: the standing waves along the primary branch and the spectral instability of the higher
241: branches with nonvanishing localized tails remain true for all negative ω. We note that
242: orbital stability of the nodeless primary branch supports the idea that it represents the
243: ground state of the system, that is, it minimizes energy at constant mass, as conjectured
244: in [7].
245:      The paper is organized as follows. In Section 2 we prove existence of a countable set
246: of standing waves which vanish on the tail of the tadpole graph. This set is the basis for
247: the subsequent analysis of bifurcation and stability of new standing waves.
248:      In Section 3 we show the existence of the primary branch of standing waves, which
249: bifurcates from the trivial solution at ω = 0. In Section 4 we construct the higher branches
250: of standing waves, which bifurcate at ω = 0 from each solution in the countable set
251: constructed in Section 2, the bifurcating solutions have non-vanishing localized tail outside
252: the ring. Note that both perturbative results are based on a non-trivial adaptation of the
253: Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction method, which holds near the edge bifurcation.
254:      In Section 5 we consider orbital stability of primary branch near the bifurcation point,
262: and it involves eigenvalue problems with nonlinear dependence of the spectral problem
263: on the spectral parameter. We also show that the eigenvalue count is inconclusive for
264: the higher branches with vanishing tails outside the ring. Using heuristic arguments, we
265: conjecture that these higher branches are also unstable at least for small values of ω.
266:      Finally, in Section 7, we develop numerical approximations for the cubic case p = 1.
267: Numerical findings illustrate our analytical results near ω = 0 and give a complete picture
268: on existence and stability of standing waves on the tadpole graphs for larger values of the
269: parameter ω.

1930: case p = 1, which corresponds to the cubic NLS equation on the tadpole.

2216: the spectral gap away from the continuous spectrum and then split into two pairs of purely
2217: imaginary eigenvalues of opposite Krein signatures. This can be explained from the fact
2218: that the standing wave solution (u+ n,ω , 0) shown on Figure 1 look like a sequence of 2n NLS
2219: solitary waves of opposite polarity as ω → −∞. As is well known from the qualitative
2220: theory of soliton interactions [19], pairs of NLS solitary waves of opposite polarity repel

27:                                            Generalized pseudostandard words were introduced by de Luca and De Luca in [9].
28:                                            In comparison to the palindromic and pseudopalindromic closure, only little is
29:                                            known about the generalized pseudopalindromic closure and the associated gen-
30:                                            eralized pseudostandard words. We present a counterexample to Conjecture 43
31:                                            from a paper by Blondin Massé et al. [2] that estimated the complexity of binary
32:                                            generalized pseudostandard words as C(n) ≤ 4n for all sufficiently large n. We
33:                                            conjecture that C(n) < 6n for all n ∈ N.
34: 
35: 
36: 
37:                                            1. Introduction
38: 
39:                                            This paper focuses on generalized pseudostandard words. Such words were defined
40:                                            by de Luca and De Luca in 2006 [9] who studied generalized standard episturmian
41:                                            words, called generalized pseudostandard words, by considering pseudopalindromic
42:                                            closure of an infinite sequence of involutory antimorphisms. While standard epis-
43:                                            turmian and pseudostandard words have been studied intensively and a lot of their
44:                                            properties are known (see for instance [3, 5, 8, 9]), only little has been shown so
68:      and the infinite sequence of antimorphisms that generates such words is stud-
69:      ied.
70: 
71:    • A conjecture is stated saying that the complexity of an infinite binary gener-
72:      alized pseudostandard word u, i.e., the map C : N → N defined by C(n) = the
73:      number of factors of length n of the infinite word u, satisfies:
74: 
75:                             C(n) ≤ 4n    for sufficiently large n.
76: 
77:    In this paper, we provide a counterexample to the above conjecture by construc-
78: tion of a generalized pseudostandard word satisfying C(n) > 4n for all n ≥ 10. We
79: moreover show that C(n) > 4.5 n for infinitely many n ∈ N.
80:    The work is organized as follows. In Section 2 we introduce basics from combi-
81: natorics on words. Section 3 deals with the palindromic closure and summarizes
82: known results. Similarly, Section 4 is devoted to the pseudopalindromic closure and
83: its properties. In Section 5, the generalized pseudopalindromic closure is defined
84: and the normalization process is described. A counterexample to Conjecture 43
85: from [2] is constructed and its complexity is estimated in Section 6. In Section 7 we
86: summarize known facts about the complexity of binary generalized pseudostandard
87: words and state a new conjecture: C(n) < 6n for all n ∈ N.
88: 
89: 
90: 
91: 2. Basics from combinatorics on words
92: 
93: We restrict ourselves to the binary alphabet {0, 1}, we call 0 and 1 letters. A (finite)
94: word w over {0, 1} is any finite binary sequence. Its length |w| is the number of
95: letters w contains. The empty word – the neutral element for concatenation of
96: words – is denoted by ε and its length is set |ε| = 0. The set of all finite binary
97:                              ∗
98: words is denoted by {0, 1} . An infinite word u over {0, 1} is any binary infinite

432: Conjecture 43 in [2]):
433:

435: u there exists n0 ∈ N such that Cu (n) ≤ 4n for all n > n0 .

1072: 6.2. Complexity of up is significantly larger than 4n
1073: Let us prove that the infinite word up from the counterexample to Conjecture 4n
1074: not only satisfies Cup (n) > 4n for all n ≥ 10, but its complexity is significantly
1075: larger than 4n.
1076:    We start with a simple observation concerning the lengths of weak bispecial
1077: factors.
1078: Observation 6. For all i ∈ N, i ≥ 1, we have:
1079:            |s4i+5 | − |w4i+3 | = 2|w4i+1 | − 2|w4i |   = 2|w4i | + 4,
1080:            |s4i+3 | − |w4i+1 | = 2|w4i−1 | − 2|w4i−2 | = 2|w4i−2 | − 6.
1081: Proof. It follows from the form of weak bispecials described in Lemma 3 that
1082:                     |s4i+5 | = 2|w4i+1 | + |w4i+3 | − 2|w4i |,
1083:                                                                                   (2)
1084:                     |s4i+3 | = 2|w4i−1 | + |w4i+1 | − 2|w4i−2 |.
1234: only one of two infinite left special branches. Hence, the total first difference of
1235: complexity has to be doubled.

1237:    • Let us state a new conjecture based on our computer experiments:
1238: 
1239:          Conjecture 2 (Conjecture 6n). Let u be a binary generalized pseudostandard
1240: 
1241: INTEGERS: 16 (2016)                                                               27
1242: 
1243:      word, then its complexity satisfies
1244: 
1245:                                Cu (n) < 6n   for all n ∈ N.
1246: 
1247:      The arguments supporting our conjecture are as follows:
1248: 
1249:         1. On one hand, in all our examples the first difference of complexity satis-
1250:            fies ∆C(n) ≤ 6.
1251:         2. On the other hand, we have checked for u = u(1ω , (RRRRREEEEE)ω )
1252:            that Cu (n) > 5n for some n ∈ N.

11:                                                        The anti-van der Waerden number, denoted by aw([n], k), is the smallest r such that
12:                                                    every exact r-coloring of [n] contains a rainbow k-term arithmetic progression. Butler et.
13:                                                    al. showed that ⌈log3 n⌉ + 2 ≤ aw([n], 3) ≤ ⌈log2 n⌉ + 1, and conjectured that there exists
14:                                                    a constant C such that aw([n], 3) ≤ ⌈log3 n⌉ + C. In this paper, we show this conjecture is
15:                                                    true by determining aw([n], 3) for all n. We prove that for 7 · 3m−2 + 1 ≤ n ≤ 21 · 3m−2 ,

19:                                                                                                m + 3, otherwise.

15:                                                Abstract
16:           The anti-van der Waerden number, denoted by aw([n], k), is the smallest r such
17:       that every exact r-coloring of [n] contains a rainbow k-term arithmetic progression.
18:       Butler et al. showed that dlog3 ne + 2 ⩽ aw([n], 3) ⩽ dlog2 ne + 1, and conjectured
19:       that there exists a constant C such that aw([n], 3) ⩽ dlog3 ne + C. In this paper,
20:       we show this conjecture is true by determining aw([n], 3) for all n. We prove that
21:       for 7 · 3m−2 + 1 ⩽ n ⩽ 21 · 3m−2 ,
22: 
23:                                                m + 2, if n = 3m
24:                                             
25:                               aw([n], 3) =
26:                                                m + 3, otherwise.
27: 
28: 
29:       Keywords: arithmetic progression; rainbow coloring; Behrend construction; uni-
30:       tary coloring
31: 
67: finite abelian groups in [7]. Butler et al. also provides bounds for aw([n], 3), as well as
68: many exact values (see Table 1).
69:     In this paper, we determine the exact value of aw([n], 3), which answers questions
70: posed in [4] and confirms the following conjecture:
71: Conjecture 1. [4] There exists a constant C such that aw([n], 3) ⩽ dlog3 ne + C, for all
72: n ⩾ 3.
73:    Our main result, Theorem 2, also determines awu ([n], 3) which shows the existence of
74: extremal colorings of [n] that are unitary.
75: Theorem 2. For all integers n ⩾ 2,
76:                               

79:                                 m + 3, if n 6= 3m and 7 · 3m−2 + 1 ⩽ n ⩽ 21 · 3m−2 .
80: 
81:    In Section 2, we provide lemmas that are useful in proving Theorem 2 and Section 3
82: contains the proof of Theorem 2.
83: 
84: 2    Main Tools
85: In [4, Theorem 1.6] it is shown that 3 ⩽ aw(Zp , 3) ⩽ 4 for every prime number p and
86: that if aw(Zp , 3) = 4 then p ⩾ 17. Furthermore, it is shown that the value of aw(Zn , 3) is
87: determined by the values of aw(Zp , 3) for the prime factors p of n. We have included this
88: theorem below with some notation change.

402: 
403: 4    Concluding Remarks
404: We have determined the exact value of the anti-van der Waerden number aw([n], 3) for
405: all n, which confirms a conjecture in [4]. Bounds on aw([n, ], k) for k ⩾ 4 are given in [4],
406: however the exact values are not known in general. These bounds can be improved and
407: it would be interesting to know exact values aw([n], k) for k ⩾ 4.
408:     The values of aw(G, k) has been recently studied for finite abelian groups [7]. It would
409: be interesting to investigate this number for other groups.

224: 
225:    Moreover, Cabello et al. [6] conjectured that even for a general simple
226: polygon P, b(P) can be bounded from above by a linear function of c(P).
227: (The question whether b(P) = O(c(P)) for simple polygons P was originally asked
228: by S. Cabello and M. Saumell, personal communication to G. Rote.) The next theorem,
229: which is the first main result of this paper, verifies this conjecture. Recall that b(S) is
230: defined for a set S if and only if S has finite positive measure and Seg(S) is measur-
231: able. Recall also that a set is p-componentwise simply connected if its p-components
232: are simply connected. In particular, every simply connected set is p-componentwise
233: simply connected.
234: Theorem 1.3 Every p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is
235: defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ 180 c(S).
236:    Clearly, every simple polygon satisfies the assumptions of Theorem 1.3. Hence we
237: directly obtain the following, which verifies the conjecture of Cabello et al. [6].
238: Corollary 1.4 Every simple polygon P ⊆ R2 satisfies b(P) ⩽ 180 c(P).
239:    The main restriction in Theorem 1.3 is the assumption that S is p-componentwise
240: simply connected. This assumption cannot be omitted, as shown by the set
241: S := [0, 1]2  Q2 , where it is easy to verify that c(S) = 0 and b(S) = 1, see
242: Proposition 3.7.
243:    A related construction shows that Theorem 1.3 fails in higher dimensions. To see
244: this, consider again the set S := [0, 1]2  Q2 , and define a set S  ⊆ R3 by
245: 
246:                      S  := {(t x, t y, t) : t ∈ [0, 1] and (x, y) ∈ S}.
247: 
248: Again, it is easy to verify that c(S  ) = 0 and b(S  ) = 1, although S  is simply
249: connected, even star-shaped.
250:    Despite these examples, we will show that meaningful analogues of Theorem 1.3
251: for higher dimensions and for sets that are not p-componentwise simply connected are
252: possible. The key is to use higher-order generalizations of the Beer index, which we
253: introduce now.
254:    For k ∈ {1, . . . , d} and a set S ⊆ Rd , we define the set Simpk (S) ⊆ (Rd )k+1 by
255: 
256:          Simpk (S) := {(A0 , . . . , Ak ) ∈ S k+1 : Conv({A0 , . . . , Ak }) ⊆ S},
257: 
258: where the operator Conv denotes the convex hull of a set of points. We call Simpk (S)
259: the k-simplex set of S. Note that Simp1 (S) = Seg(S).
260:    For k ∈ {1, . . . , d} and a set S ⊆ Rd with finite positive measure and with measur-
261: able Simpk (S), we define bk (S) by
280: d-index of convexity can conceivably admit a nontrivial upper bound in terms of c(S).
281: Our next result shows that such an upper bound on bd (S) exists and is linear in c(S).
282: 
283: Theorem 1.5 For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant β = β(d) > 0 such that every set
284: S ⊆ Rd with bd (S) defined satisfies bd (S) ⩽ β c(S).
285: 
286:    We do not know if the linear upper bound in Theorem 1.5 is best possible. We can,
287: however, construct examples showing that the bound is optimal up to a logarithmic
288: factor. This is our last main result.
289:

291: ε ∈ (0, 1), there is a set S ⊆ Rd satisfying c(S) ⩽ ε and bd (S) ⩾ γ log ε1/ε , and in

302: 2 Bounding the Mutual Visibility in the Plane
303: 
304: The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 1.3. Since the proof is rather long and
305: complicated, we first present a high-level overview of its main ideas.
306:    We first show that it is sufficient to prove the estimate from Theorem 1.3 for bounded
307: open simply connected sets. This is formalized by the next lemma, whose proof can
308: be found in Sect. 2.2.
309: 
310: Lemma 2.1 Let α > 0 be a constant such that every bounded open simply connected
311: set T ⊆ R2 satisfies b(T ) ⩽ α c(T ). It follows that every p-componentwise simply
312: connected set S ⊆ R2 with b(S) defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S).
313: 
314:    In the proof of Lemma 2.1, we first show that the set S can be reduced to a bounded
315: open set S  whose Beer index b(S  ) can be arbitrarily close to b(S) from below. This
316: is done by considering a part S  of S that is contained in a sufficiently large disc and
317: by showing that all segments in S  are in fact contained in the interior of S  , except for
343: property that all its points are reachable from its root by a polygonal line with at most
344: two segments (Lemma 2.5). To handle such sets, we will generalize the techniques
345: that Cabello et al. [6] have used to handle weakly star-shaped sets in their proof of
346: Theorem 1.1. We will assign to every point A ∈ R a set T(A) of measure O(smc(R)),
347: such that for every (A, B) ∈ Seg(R), we have either B ∈ T(A) or A ∈ T(B)
348: (Lemma 2.7). From this, Theorem 1.3 will follow easily.
349:

1494: 3.1 Proof of Theorem 1.5
1495: 
1496: Let T = (B0 , B1 , . . . , Bd ) be a (d + 1)-tuple of distinct affinely independent points
1497: in Rd . We say that a permutation B0 , B1 , . . . , Bd of T is a regular permutation of T
1498: if the following two conditions hold:
1499: 1. the segment B0 B1 is the diameter of T ,
1500: 2. for i = 2, . . . , d−1, the point Bi has the maximum distance to Aff({B0 , . . . , Bi−1 })
1501:    among the points Bi , Bi+1 , . . . , Bd .
1502: Obviously, T has at least two regular permutations due to the interchangeability of B0
1503: and B1 . The regular permutation Bi0 , Bi1 , . . . , Bid with the lexicographically minimal
1504: vector (i 0 , i 1 , . . . , i d ) is called the canonical permutation of T .

1590: 3.2 Proof of Theorem 1.6
1591: 
1592: In the following, we make no serious effort to optimize the constants. As the first step
1593: towards the proof of Theorem 1.6, we show that if we remove an arbitrary n-tuple of
1594: points from the open d-dimensional box (0, 1)d , then the d-index of convexity of the
1595: resulting set is of order Ω( n1 ).

1602: Lemma 3.3 For every positive integer n and every n-tuple N of points from (0, 1)d ,

1702:    The next step in the proof of Theorem 1.6 is to find a convenient n-tuple N of
1703: points from (0, 1)d whose removal produces a set with sufficiently small convexity
1704: ratio. We are going to find N using a continuous version of the well-known Epsilon
1705: Net Theorem [9]. Before stating this result, we need some definitions.
1706:    Let X be a subset of Rd and let U be a set system on X . We say that a set
1707: T ⊆ X is shattered by U if every subset of T can be obtained as the intersection of
1708: some U ∈ U with T . The Vapnik-Chervonenkis dimension (or VC-dimension) of U ,
1709: denoted by dim(U ), is the maximum n (or ∞ if no such maximum exists) for which
1710: some subset of X of cardinality n is shattered by U .
1711:    Let U be a system of measurable subsets of a set X ⊆ Rd with λd (X ) = 1, and let
1712: ε ∈ (0, 1) be a real number. A set N ⊆ X is called an ε-net for (X, U ) if N ∩ U = ∅
1713: for every U ∈ U with λd (U ) ⩾ ε.

1798:    It is a natural question whether the bound for bd (S) in Theorem 1.6 can be improved
1799: to bd (S) = Ω(c(S)). In the plane, this is related to the famous problem of Danzer and
1800: Rogers (see [5,14] and Problem E14 in [7]) which asks whether for given ε > 0 there
1801: is a set N  ⊆ (0, 1)2 of size O( 1ε ) with the property that every convex set of area ε
1802: within the unit square contains at least one point from N  .
1803:    If this problem was to be answered affirmatively, then we could use such a set N 
1804: to stab (0, 1)2 in our proof of Theorem 1.6 which would yield the desired bound for
1805: b2 (S). However it is generally believed that the answer is likely to be nonlinear in 1ε .

1808: 3.3 A Set with Large k-Index of Convexity and Small Convexity Ratio
1809: 
1810: Proposition 3.7 For every integer d ⩾ 2, the set S := [0, 1]d  Qd satisfies c(S) = 0
1811: and bk (S) = 1 for every positive integer k < d.
1812: Proof Since Qd is countable and λd ([0, 1]d ) = 1, we have λd (S) = 1. Every
1813: convex subset K of [0, 1]d with positive d-dimensional measure contains an open
1814: d-dimensional ball B with positive diameter, as there is a (d + 1)-tuple of affinely
1815: independent points of K . Since Qd is a dense subset of Rd , we see that B ∩ Qd = ∅
1840: 
1841: 4 Other Variants and Open Problems
1842: 
1843: We have seen in Theorem 1.3 that a p-componentwise simply connected set S ⊆ R2
1844: whose b(S) is defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S), for an absolute constant α ⩽ 180.
1845: Equivalently, such a set S satisfies smc(S) ⩾ b(S)λ2 (S)/180.
1846:    By a result of Blaschke [4] (see
1847:                                   √
1848:                                      also Sas [18]), every convex set K ⊆ R2 contains
1849: a triangle of measure at least 34π3 λ2 (K ). In view of this, Theorem 1.3 yields the
1850: following consequence.
1851: 
1852: Corollary 4.1 There is a constant α > 0 such that every p-componentwise simply
1853: connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined contains a triangle T ⊆ S of measure at
1854: least α b(S)λ2 (S).
1855: 
1856:    A similar argument works in higher dimensions as well. For every d ⩾ 2, there is a
1857: constant β = β(d) such that every convex set K ⊆ Rd contains a simplex of measure
1858: at least βλd (K ) (see e.g. Lassak [12]). Therefore, Theorem 1.5 can be rephrased in
1859: the following equivalent form.
1860: 
1861: Corollary 4.2 For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant α = α(d) > 0 such that every set
1862: S ⊆ Rd whose bd (S) is defined contains a simplex T of measure at least α bd (S)λd (S).
1863: 
1864:    What can we say about sets S ⊆ R2 that are not p-componentwise simply con-
1865: nected? First of all, we can consider a weaker form of simple connectivity: we call a
1866: set S p-componentwise simply -connected if for every triangle T such that ∂ T ⊆ S
1867: we have T ⊆ S. We conjecture that Theorem 1.3 can be extended to p-componentwise
1868: simply -connected sets.
1869: 
1870: Conjecture 4.3 There is an absolute constant α > 0 such that every p-componentwise
1871: simply -connected set S ⊆ R2 whose b(S) is defined satisfies b(S) ⩽ α c(S).
1872: 
1873:    What does the value of b(S) say about a planar set S that does not satisfy even a weak
1874: form of simple connectivity? As Proposition 3.7 shows, such a set may not contain
1875: any convex subset of positive measure, even when b(S) is equal to 1. However, we
1876: conjecture that a large b(S) implies the existence of a large convex set whose boundary
1877: belongs to S.
1878: 
1879: Conjecture 4.4 For every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ R2 is a set
1880: with b(S) ⩾ ε, then there is a bounded convex set C ⊆ R2 with λ(C) ⩾ δλ(S) and
1881: ∂C ⊆ S.
1882: 
1883:    Theorem 1.3 shows that Conjecture 4.4 holds for p-componentwise simply con-
1884: nected sets, with δ being a constant multiple of ε. It is possible that even in the general
1885: setting of Conjecture 4.4, δ can be taken as a constant multiple of ε.
1886:    Motivated by Corollary 4.1, we propose a stronger version of Conjecture 4.4, where
1887: the convex set C is required to be a triangle.
1888: 
1889: Conjecture 4.5 For every ε > 0, there is a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ R2 is a set with
1890: b(S) ⩾ ε, then there is a triangle T ⊆ R2 with λ(T ) ⩾ δλ(S) and ∂ T ⊆ S.
1891: 
1892: 123
1893: 
1894: Discrete Comput Geom (2017) 57:179–214                                                                 213
1895: 
1896: 
1897:    Note that Conjecture 4.5 holds when restricted to p-componentwise simply con-
1898: nected sets, as implied by Corollary 4.1.
1899:    We can generalize Conjecture 4.5 to higher dimensions and to higher-order indices
1900: of convexity. To state
1901:                     the general conjecture, we introduce the following notation: for
1902: a set X ⊆ Rd , let Xk be the set of k-element subsets of X , and let the set Skelk (X )
1903: be defined by
1904: 
1905:                                  Skelk (X ) :=               Conv(Y ).
1906:                                                      ( X
1907:                                                   Y ∈ k+1)
1908: 
1909: If X is the vertex set of a d-dimensional simplex T = Conv(X ), then Skelk (X ) is
1910: often called the k-dimensional skeleton of T . Our general conjecture states, roughly
1911: speaking, that sets with large k-index of convexity should contain the k-dimensional
1912: skeleton of a large simplex. Here is the precise statement.
1913: Conjecture 4.6 For every k, d ∈ N such that 1 ⩽ k ⩽ d and every ε > 0, there is
1914: a δ > 0 such that if S ⊆ Rd is a set with bk (S) ⩾ ε, then there is a simplex T with
1915: vertex set X such that λd (T ) ⩾ δλd (S) and Skelk (X ) ⊆ S.
1916:     Corollary 4.2 asserts that this conjecture holds in the special case of k = d ⩾ 2,
1917: since Skeld (X ) = Conv(X ) = T . Corollary 4.1 shows that the conjecture holds for
1918: k = 1 and d = 2 if S is further assumed to be p-componentwise simply connected. In
1919: all these cases, δ can be taken as a constant multiple of ε, with the constant depending
1920: on k and d.
1921:     Finally, we can ask whether there is a way to generalize Theorem 1.3 to higher
1922: dimensions, by replacing simple connectivity with another topological property. Here
1923: is an example of one such possible generalization.
1924: Conjecture 4.7 For every d ⩾ 2, there is a constant α = α(d) > 0 such that if
1925: S ⊆ Rd is a set with bd−1 (S) defined whose every p-component is contractible, then
1926: bd−1 (S) ⩽ α c(S).
1927:    A modification of the proof of Theorem 1.5 implies that Conjecture 4.7 is true for
1928: star-shaped sets S.

217:           2. Results and counterexamples for Lovász-Saks-Schrijver ideals
218:    For the first part of this section let G = (V, E) be a graph. We start by studying radicality of
219: LkG (d). As mentioned in the Introduction LkG (1) is always radical for trivial reasons. For d = 2
220: the following result from [22] gives a complete answer.
221: Theorem 2.1 (Thm. 1.1 [22]). Let G = ([n], E) be a graph. If char k 6= 2 then the ideal LkG (2) is
222: radical. If char k = 2 then LkG (2) is radical if and only if G is bipartite.
223:    The next examples show that LkG (3) need not be radical. In the examples we assume that k
224: has characteristic 0 but we consider it very likely that the ideals are not radical over any field.
225:    A quick criterion implying that an ideal J in a polynomial ring S is not radical is to identify
226: an element g ∈ S such that J : g 6= J : g 2 . We call such a g a witness (of the fact that J is not
227: radical). Of course the potential witnesses must be sought among the elements that are somehow
228: “closely related” to J. Alternatively, one can try to compute the radical of J or even its primary
1497: fact that the property is inherited by subgraphs supports hope for the classifications asked for in
1498: Question 8.2. After Theorem 2.9 d = 3 for complete intersection and d = 4 for primeness are the
1499: first parameters for which the classification is open.
1500:     For d = 3 we do not even have a conjecture when LkG (d) is a complete intersection. The graph
1501: G from Figure 1(1) gives a graph G for which it can be checked that LkG (3) is not a complete
1502:

1509: carries the essential obstacles for LkG (4) being prime.

1511: isomorphic to Ka,b for a + b = 5 and no subgraph isomorphic to B4 ?

1535:   For radicality we have a concrete conjecture in the case G = Kn .

1537: words, given a matrix of variables X of size n×d we conjecture the ideal of the off-diagonal entries
1538: of XX T is radical for all n, d.

1544: pmd(H) + 1 only in the case that H is a graph.
1545: Question 8.8. Is it true that for a hypergraph H = (V, E), where E is a clutter, we have LkH (d)
1546: is prime for d ≥ pmd(H) + 1?

75: 1.1. The main theorems. In this section we briefly outline the main results of
76: the paper. The definitions of all terms used in the statement of these results can

78:    In order to construct new infinite families of Conway groupoids we study the

80: make use of a bijection between Ω and the vector space V ∼= F2m
81:                                                               2 on which Sp2m (2)

227: transitive subgroup of Sym(n) with π∞ (D) the stabilizer of the point ∞ in L∞ (D).
228: Since, by supposition, π∞ (D) is primitive, this implies that L∞ (D) is a 2-primitive
229: permutation group (i.e. a primitive group with a stabilizer primitive on its non-
230: trivial orbit). We conjecture that this behaviour is general.
231: Conjecture 1. Suppose that D is a supersimple 2 − (n, 4, λ) design other than
232: P3 , that ∞ is a point in D and that the hole stabilizer π∞ (D) is primitive. Then
233: L∞ (D) coincides with a 2-primitive subgroup H of Sym(n) and π∞ (D) is equal to
234: the stabilizer in L∞ (D) of the point ∞.
235:    We remark that all 2-primitive permutation groups are known thanks to CFSG
236: and the list is rather short (see [25] for some discussion). Thus this conjecture im-
237: plies a very strong restriction on the structure of a Conway groupoid with primitive
238: hole stabilizer and a proof would be a very significant step towards a classification.
239:    One could push the conjecture a little further. Let us operate under the supposi-
240: tions of Conjecture 1 and assume, moreover, that L∞ (D) does not contain Alt(n).
241: Now all known examples satisfy two further properties:
242:    Firstly, the elements of L∞ (D) are automorphisms of the design D. Secondly,
243: the group L∞ (D) is a 3-transposition group with associated class of transpositions
244: coinciding with the set
245:                                    {[a, b] | a, b ∈ Ω}.
246: (Here Ω is the point set of D; the elements [a, b] are defined in Section 2.1.)
247:    In forthcoming work the authors prove Conjecture 1 and the two additional
248: statements just mentioned, provided the design D satisfies two mild combinatorial
249: suppositions. It is expected that, by exploiting Fischer’s famous theorem on 3-
250: transposition groups, this will lead to a full classification of Conway groupoids in
251: this restricted situtation [20].

1225: garded as a special case of Mihăilescu’s theorem, formerly the Catalan conjecture
1226: [28].
1227: Lemma 6.3. Suppose that a, b, p are positive integers, that a, b > 1 and that pa ±1 =
1228: 2b . Then either a = 1 or p = 3, a = 2.
1229: Proof. If a is odd then the second factor in pa ± 1 = (p ± 1)(pa−1 ∓ . . . + 1) is
1230: odd. Hence a − 1 = 0 in this case. If a = 2t for some t > 0 then there are two
1231: possibilities: firstly, we could have 2b = (p2t − 1) = (pt − 1)(pt + 1) and we obtain
1232: immediately that (p, t, a) = (3, 1, 2). Secondly, we could have 2b = p2t + 1; but
1233: since p2t + 1 ≡ 2 mod 4, this yields no solutions.                                 

54: Theorem 1.1 (The Hook-Length Formula). Let λ = (λ1 , λ2 , . . . , λm ) be a non-increasing
55: partition of N , and let hij denote the hook-length of the (i, j)-entry in a Young diagram

61:     Now take a permutation σ ∈ Sn . An inversion of σ is a pair of integers 1 ⩽ i, j ⩽ n
62: such that i < j yet σ(i) > σ(j). We write (i, j)σ or simply (i, j) if i and j form an
63: inversion pair of σ with i < j. We denote the total number of inversions of σ by ninv (σ).
64:     In [3], Fresse generalized this notion of inversion to multi-column tableaux. Let τ be
65: a row-standard tableau of shape λ. A pair of entries 1 ⩽ i, j ⩽ N from the same column
111: d − m inversions, where d is the dimension of the entire Springer variety. Ranging over
112: all standard Young tableaux T then gives:
113: 
114: Theorem 1.2 (Fresse). Let λ be a partition of N and consider the Springer variety Fλ .
115: If dim(Fλ ) = d, then the mth Betti number bm = dim(H 2m (Fλ , Q)) equals the number of
116: inverted Young tableaux τ of shape λ with ninv (τ ) = d − m.
117: 
118:     For a given shape λ = (λ1 , . . . , λm ), let Si (λ) = Si (λ1 , . . . , λm ) denote the set of row-
119: standard tableaux τ of shape λ with ninv (τ ) = i. Theorem 1.2 then states that bm =
120: |Sd−m (λ)|. We henceforth refer to elements of Si (λ) as i-inverted (standard) Young
121: tableau of shape λ. Note that standard Young tableaux correspond to S0 (λ) = S(λ),
122: and that the size of the set S0 (λ) is explicitly determined by the Hook-Length formula.
123:     This paper won’t assume detailed knowledge of Springer varieties. We present Fresse’s
124: results primarily to motivate our study of tableaux inversions. Our approach is purely
125: combinatorial, and is concerned with developing tractable methods for enumerating ele-
126: ments in the sets Si (λ) for arbitrary λ and any i ⩾ 0.
127:     The primary difficulty in adapting Fresse’s approach to these goals is that his enu-
128: merative results require knowledge of specific standard Young tableau as a starting point.
129: Even if one can easily produce a generating function that gives the sizes of the sets
130: Si (λ) ∩ inv(T ) for a fixed standard tableau T , there is no workable way for determining
146: to non-rectangular tableaux in Theorem 3.2), relates the number of rectangular tableaux
147: with precisely one inversion to the number of standard Young tableaux of a related “stair-
148: step” shape:
149: Theorem 1.3. Let n, m ⩾ 1, and consider the m-row shapes λ = (n, . . . , n), λ

162:     Closing the paper are a series of more specific results and conjectures enumerating
163: inverted tableaux whose inversion number is “sufficiently large” (a realm that we refer to
164: as the “tail end” of a shape’s inversion distribution). All results and conjectures in this
165: paper were developed with the help of a computer program developed by the authors.
166: Details about this program, as well as several tables of generated results, may be found
167: in Appendix A.
168: 
169: 2     Basic Enumerative Results About Tableau Inversions
170: In this section, we present a number of foundational enumerative results involving tableau
171: inversions that are independent from the more involved methods of Sections 3 and 4.
172: 
173: 2.1    Total Number of Inverted Young Tableaux of Shape λ
174: Let λ = (λ1 , λ2 , . . . , λmS) be a non-increasing partition of the positive integer N , and
175: consider the set I(λ) = i Si (λ) of inverted Young tableaux of shape λ (with any number
316: iff its standardization st(τ ) splits after its j th column.
317:      It is well-known that the number of rectangular standard Young tableaux of size 2 × n
318: equals the nth Catalan number Cn , a fact that can be directly calculated from the Hook-
319: Length Formula of Theorem 1.1 or by setting up a bijection with noncrossing matchings.
320: This means that |S0 (n, n)| = Cn .
321:      To find |Si (n, n)| for i > 0, we begin by noting that each column in a two-row inverted
322: Young tableau has either 0 or 1 inversion pair. If a column admits an inversion pair,
323: this means that the larger entry in that column containing the inversion much be smaller
324: than the smallest of the two entries in the column directly to the right. It follows that a
325: two-row inverted Young tableau must split after any column in which it has an inversion.
326: For an example of this phenomenon see Figure 3, where larger subscripts correspond to
327: larger entries. In that example, b2 < c1 ensures that the tableau must split.
328: Theorem 2.3. Let λ = (n, n) for some n ⩾ 1. Then the number of inverted Young
329: tableaux of shape λ with precisely i inversions is:
330:                                                  !                         
371:      Readers in search of additional combinatorial identities should note that, when succes-
372: sively ranging over all n > 0, the sequence formed by the |Si (n, n)| is A039599 on OEIS
373: [1].
374:      In light of Theorem 1.2, Theorem 2.3 applies to give a closed formula for all Betti
375: numbers of the Springer variety Fλ when λ = (n, n). Also note that, as (1, . . . , 1) is the
376: only partition of n of length at least n, then Mn,n = n and |Sn (n, n)| = 1 as predicted by
377: Proposition 2.2.

619:     In this section we prove formulas for |SMλ −1 (λ)| and |SMλ −2 (λ)| in the rectangular
620: case, generalizing our observations about the three-row tableaux in Appendix A. We
621: then prove a special case of the “tail-end” bijection and conjecture as to how this result

628: to non-rectangular tableaux that mirror Subsection 3.2.
629: 
630: 4.1    Calculating |SMλ −1 (λ)| and |SMλ −2 (λ)|, Rectangular Case
631: Directly enumerating inverted tableaux becomes increasingly tractable as one approaches
632: the maximum inversion number Mλ . This is because having large numbers of inversions
656: the second largest entry am−1 in the leftmost column is directly to the left of the smallest
657: entry b1 in the column immediately on its right. For an illustration of these two sub-cases
658: when m = 4, see Figure 9 below.
659:      In the first sub-case, the tableau must split after the first column, so that the first
660: column must contain the entries 1, 2, . . . , m. Since there is a unique way to produce
661: M(n−1) inversions across the latter columns, the placement of m + 1, m + 2, . . . , mn is
662: predetermined. In the first column, there are m − 1 inversions involving am and we must
663: have Tm−1 − 1 − (m − 1) = Tm−2 − 1 inversions among the remaining m − 1 entries in
664: that column. There are M (m − 2, Tm−2 − 1) = m − 2 distinct arrangements that produce
665: these Tm−2 − 1 inversions. This means that there are precisely m − 2 inverted tableaux
666: satisfying this sub-case.
667: 
668: the electronic journal of combinatorics 22(2) (2015), #P2.44                                14
669: 
670:     In the second sub-case, the tableau need not split after the first column, as the largest
671: entry am in the leftmost column may or may not be greater than the smallest entry b1
672: in the column directly to its right. No matter the relationship between these two entries,
673: there is a unique arrangement that produces the requisite M(n−1) inversions across the
674: latter columns. In the first column, there are always m − 2 inversions involving the top
675: entry am−1 , leaving Tm−2 inversions among the remaining m − 1 entries in the column.
676: There is only M (m − 2, Tm−2 ) = 1 arrangement of the remaining entries in the leftmost
677: column that produces these Tm−2 inversions. Also notice that the required arrangement
678: in the leftmost column ensures that am is directly to the left of the second-smallest entry
679: b2 of the next column, and that the tableau always splits after the second column. This
680: leaves the relationship between am and b1 as the only open question about such a tableau.
681: It follows that there are precisely 2 inverted tableaux in this sub-case (one for am < b1 ,
682: one for am > b1 ).
683:     Collecting results from all sub-cases and then applying the inductive assumption gives
684: the desired result: |SMn (n)| = |SMn−1 (n−1)|+(m−2)+2 = (m(n−1)−1)+(m−2)+2 =
685: mn − 1.
722:       columns have precisely M(n−1) inversions.
723: 
724:     In case #1, there is a unique ordering of the leftmost column to obtain the maximal
725: number of M1 inversions and the tableau necessarily splits after the first column. It follows
726: that the number of inverted tableaux in this case equals |SM(n−1) −2 (n − 1)|.
727:     In case #2, there are two sub-cases depending upon whether the largest entry am or
728: the second largest entry am−1 in the leftmost column is directly left of the smallest entry
729: b1 in the second column. For an illustration of these sub-cases when m = 4, see Figure
730: 10.
731:     In the first sub-case of case #2, the tableau must split after the first column. In the
732: first column, am participates in m − 1 inversions and thus the remaining elements must
733: account for Tm−1 − 1 − (m − 1) = Tm−2 − 1 total inversions. By Theorem 4.1, there
734: are m − 2 ways to achieve these Tm−2 − 1 inversions. It follows that there are precisely
735: (m − 2)|SM(n−1) −1 (n − 1)| tableaux in this sub-case.
736:     In the second sub-case of case #2, the tableau needn’t split after the first column. In
737: the first column, am−1 accounts for m − 2 inversions and hence the remaining elements
738: in that column must account for Tm−2 inversions. This implies that the rest of the first
739: column must be “fully inverted”, and thus that am is directly to the left of the second-
740: smallest entry b2 in the second column. We then have am−1 < b1 and am < b2 , leaving
741: the relationship between am and b1 as the only question about the first two columns.
742: Also noticing that b1 is directly to the left of either cm or cm−1 in the third column, as
743: m ⩾ 3 we know that am < b2 < c2 ⩽ cm−1 /cm . This means we needn’t worry about
744: the relationship between am and any elements of the third column. For each possible
745: arrangement of the tableau’s final n − 1 columns, it follows that there are precisely two
746: inverted tableaux fitting this sub-case (one for am < b1 , one for am > b1 ). Thus there are
747: precisely 2|SM(n−1) −1 (n − 1)| tableaux in this sub-case.
748:     In case #3 there are four sub-cases, each depending upon which elements in the first
750: Once again, reference Figure 10 for an illustration of these sub-cases in the situation
751: where m = 4. Notice that in all of these sub-cases, the M(n−1) inversions needed over the
752: final n − 1 columns ensures that there is precisely one arrangement of those columns for
753: each arrangement of the first column. It also guarantees that any such tableau must split
754: after the second column.
755:     In the first sub-case of case #3, am is directly to the left of b1 and the tableau must
756: split after the first column. In the first column, am participates in m − 1 inversions and
757: thus the remaining elements account for Tm−1 − 2 − (m − 1) = Tm−2 − 2 total inversions.
758: By the base case of this theorem, there are (m−1−2)(m−1+1)

767: to the left of b2 . As am−1 is involved in m − 2 inversions and am is involved in m − 2
777:      In the third sub-case of case #3, am−1 still lies directly to the left of b1 but now am−2
778: lies directly to the left of b2 . In this situation, am−1 is involved in m − 2 inversions and am
779: is involved in m−3 inversions, which leaves Tm−1 −2−(m−2)−(m−3) = Tm−3 inversions
780: to be accounted for over the remaining m−2 entries of the first column. There is precisely
781: one way to account for these remaining inversions, with am necessarily appearing directly
782: to the left of b3 . With am−1 to the left of b1 and am to the left of b3 , the relationship of
783: am to both b1 and b2 is an open question. Here the possible orderings are am < b1 < b2 ,
784: b1 < am < b2 , and b1 < b2 < am . As this is the only undetermined aspect of these
785: tableaux, there are precisely 3 tableaux in this sub-case.
786:      In the final sub-case of case #3, am−2 lies directly to the left of b1 . In the first column,
787: am−2 is involved in m − 3 inversions, leaving Tm−1 − 2 − (m − 3) = Tm−2 inversions
788: for the remaining m − 1 entries of that column. There is precisely one arrangement of
789: those entries that gives Tm−2 inversions, but in this situation we still need to determine the
790: relationship of both am and am−1 to b1 . Here there are possible orderings am−1 < am < b1 ,
791: am−1 < b1 < am , and b1 < am−1 < am . As this is the only undetermined aspect of these
792: tableaux, there are precisely 3 tableaux in this sub-case.
793:      Collecting all sub-cases and citing Theorem 4.1 along with the inductive assumption
794: gives:

936: Conjecture 4.4. Let n ⩾ 1, m ⩾ 2, and take the m-row shapes λ = (n, . . . , n), λ(ne +
937: 1, n, . . . , n, n − 1). Then |Si (λ)| = |Si−m+1 (λ)|

939: triangular number.
940: 
941:     It is the authors’ belief that it may be possible to prove Conjecture 4.4 via a “recursive
942: bumping” algorithm superficially similar to the method of proof in Theorem 3.1. In this
943: modified algorithm, the φ1 : Si (λ) ,→ Si−m+1 (λ)
944:                                                 e map would begin in the rightmost column
945: that contains greater than nTm−2 inversions. The first rightward “bump” would entail a
946: reordering of the initial column in a manner that is uniquely determine by the bijection
947: of Lemma 4.3. Subsequent rightward “bumps” would then be required the conserve
948: inversion number in a manner that can be uniquely undone by some inverse algorithm
949: φ2 : Si−m+1 (λ)
950:               e ,→ Si (λ).
951:     The difficulty in proving Conjecture 4.4 derives from these later “bumps”. In particu-
952: lar, if a tableau possesses additional inversions to the right of the starting column, those
953: inversions make the preservation of inversion number very complicated when attempting
954: to bump a distinguished element past their column. Resolving the difficulties in this
955: proof, as well as developing tractable methods for dealing with |Si (λ)| when i is greater
956: than 1 but too small to be in the “tail-end”, are the most significant remaining questions
957: about enumerating tableaux inversions.

24: Generalized pseudostandard words were introduced by de Luca and De Luca in
25: 2006. In comparison to the palindromic and pseudopalindromic closure, only little
26: is known about the generalized pseudopalindromic closure and the associated gen-
27: eralized pseudostandard words. We present a counterexample to a conjecture by
28: Blondin Massé et al. from 2013 that estimated the complexity of binary generalized
29: pseudostandard words as C(n)  4n for all sufficiently large n. We conjecture that
30: C(n) < 6n for all n 2 N.
31: 
32: 
33: 
34: 1. Introduction
35: 
36: This paper focuses on generalized pseudostandard words. Such words were defined
37: by de Luca and De Luca in 2006 [9] who studied a generalization of standard epis-
38: turmian words, called generalized pseudostandard words, by considering the pseu-
39: dopalindromic closure of an infinite sequence of involutory antimorphisms. While
40: standard episturmian and pseudostandard words have been studied intensively and
65:      and the infinite sequence of antimorphisms that generates such words is stud-
66:      ied,
67: 
68:    • A conjecture is stated saying that the complexity of an infinite binary gener-
69:      alized pseudostandard word u, i.e., the map C : N ! N where C(n) equals the
70:      number of factors of length n of the infinite word u, satisfies:
71: 
72:                             C(n)  4n for sufficiently large n.
73: 
74:    In this paper, we provide a counterexample to the above conjecture by construc-
75: tion of a generalized pseudostandard word satisfying C(n) > 4n for all n 10. We
76: moreover show that C(n) > 4.5 n for infinitely many n 2 N.
77:    The work is organized as follows. In Section 2 we introduce basics from combi-
78: natorics on words. Section 3 deals with the palindromic closure and summarizes
79: known results. Similarly, Section 4 is devoted to the pseudopalindromic closure and
80: its properties. In Section 5, the generalized pseudopalindromic closure is defined
81: and the normalization process is described. A counterexample to Conjecture 43
82: from [2] is constructed and its complexity is estimated in Section 6. In Section 7 we
83: summarize known facts about the complexity of binary generalized pseudostandard
84: words and state a new conjecture: C(n) < 6n for all n 2 N.
85: 
86: 
87:

92: letters w contains. The empty word – the neutral element for concatenation of
93: words – is denoted by " and its length is set |"| = 0. The set of all finite binary
94:                              ⇤
